  - a unit's *themes* (`/unit-domains`): domains of any level below the top whose word weight is over-represented against the corpus share (Dunning's G², weight ≥ `serve.domains.min_weight`, the `serve.domains.top` strongest); Genesis 1 → Shine, Exist, Time, Land, Animals; Job 1 → Names of Supernatural Beings, Flee, Revile, Innocent;
  - domain chips in the word panel, `∥≠` and the deciding pairs on antithetic verse halves, the Poetry ranking by antithetic share, a name's kind source on Names.

  Domain names are SDBH's English labels; the two top levels (22 names) are also in the Hebrew catalog (`i18n/pages/domains.ts`), deeper ones stay English inside `<bdi>` in the Hebrew interface.
- **People and places** (`bsim entities`, §16.11): Strong's part of speech now decides first (`kind_source: lexicon` for 2,253 of 2,548 names); the context cues decide the rest (names Strong's gives both readings, or none). Where both decide (1,108 names) they agree 94.8 % of the time (person/place 35, place/person 23 disagreements), which validates the cue heuristic. `unclear` names drop to 156.

### 16.23 Senses and uses across the canon (`bsim senses`, `analysis/senses.py`, `/shifts`, `/lemma/{lemma}/senses`)
- **Question:** does a word mean or do something different in different parts of the canon (roadmap A3)?
- **Words compared:** 611 content lemmas with ≥ 50 occurrences as a word's only content lemma, ≥ 10 in each of ≥ 2 of six groups: Torah, Former Prophets, Latter Prophets, Psalms / Proverbs / Job, the Scrolls (Song, Ruth, Lamentations, Ecclesiastes, Esther), the late books (Daniel, Ezra–Nehemiah, Chronicles). Names are left out (OSHB `Np` and Strong's name types). 215,928 occurrences.
- **Two readings of each occurrence:**
  - *Dictionary sense*: the SDBH meaning tagged on it by `bsim lexicon` (occurrences with exactly one matched meaning; meanings with ≥ 5 of them; 384 lemmas have ≥ 2).
  - *Use in context*: its vector from pretrained BEREL (`berel_mean`, last layer, mean of the word's subword tokens, mapped by character offsets on `text_model`), clustered per lemma by k-means (on one thread, §16.52), k ∈ 2..4 by silhouette. A cluster is described only in Hebrew: the content lemmas of its verses most over-represented against the lemma's other clusters (G², particles and pronouns left out) and its occurrences nearest the centre.
- **Statistic:** MI(group; sense) in bits, i.e. the generalized Jensen–Shannon divergence of the groups' distributions. The null shuffles group labels over occurrences (200 times); p is empirical, q is Benjamini–Hochberg over lemmas, and the ranking uses the excess over the null mean (MI is inflated in small samples). With 200 shuffles the smallest q is 0.008.
- **Results (2026-10-05):**
  - Dictionary senses depend on the group for 296 of 384 lemmas (q ≤ 0.05). The strongest are textbook cases:
    - גאל: the blood avenger (Numbers, Joshua), redeeming property (Leviticus, Ruth), God the redeemer (Isaiah, Psalms);
    - פקד: muster (Numbers), punish (prophets), appoint (Kings);
    - עדות / עדת: the ark of the testimony (Torah), "your testimonies" (Psalms);
    - קנה: menorah branches (Exodus), measuring reed (Ezekiel).
  - Contextual uses depend on the group for 539 of 611 lemmas. This reading also carries genre and register, so it means "used differently", not necessarily "means something else". Its ranking is still informative:
    - תורה splits without supervision into the written "book of the Torah" (Torah, Former Prophets, late books), poetic "instruction" (Psalms, prophets) and ritual "torah of the burnt offering / leprosy";
    - נפש separates the legal "that person shall be cut off" (Torah) from the poetic "my soul" (Psalms).
  - **Check:** the clusters recover the SDBH meanings with NMI 0.20 on average against 0.03 for shuffled clusters (384 lemmas). Unsupervised contextual vectors do find dictionary senses, partially.
- **Served:** `lemma_shifts` and `lemma_senses` (+ `meta.senses`); `/shifts?by=sense|use&max_q=`; `/lemma/{lemma}/senses`. Viewer: Overview → Shifts, and *Senses across the canon* on every concordance page (share of each sense per group, domains, Hebrew collocates, highlighted examples).
- **Limits:**
  - BEREL's last layer and verse-only context.
  - SDBH tags 90 % of the text and some of its meanings are fine-grained.
  - Group sizes differ by an order of magnitude.
  - No diachronic claim: the groups mix date and genre.

### 16.24 A Late Biblical Hebrew profile (`bsim dating`, `analysis/dating.py`, `/language`)
- **Framing:** a *profile*, not a date. Whether linguistic features can date biblical texts is disputed (Hurvitz, Polzin and Rooker for; Young, Rezetko and Ehrensvärd against), so the score says how much a chapter's Hebrew resembles the undisputed late books, and the viewer says so.
- **Features** per chapter, over Hebrew words only (Aramaic, morph `A…`, left out). Each is a rate shrunk towards the training mean by 20 pseudo-counts, so a chapter without the context of a feature sits at the mean:
  - late words: מלכות, אגרת, בירה, מדינה, דת, קבל, בוץ, יחש, כתב, כנס, זמן, גנזים, שלט (per word);
  - אנכי among first-singular pronouns;
  - infinitive absolutes among verbs;
  - את with a suffix against a suffix on the verb;
  - directional ה (per word);
  - first-singular wayyiqtols written with ה (ואשלחה);
  - דויד among the spellings of David.

  On the training books each feature points the expected way: e.g. את + suffix 0.28 vs 0.10, directional ה 5.0 vs 2.4 per 1,000 words, ואשלחה 0.15 vs 0.49, דויד 0.4 % vs 100 %. Rejected after a look at the data: the relative ש (it marks Ecclesiastes and Song, not Chronicles) and the wayyiqtol rate (genre, not date).
- **Model:** a logistic regression of the late books (Chronicles, Ezra, Nehemiah, Esther, Daniel; 98 chapters) against Genesis–Kings (332 chapters). Chapters need 150 Hebrew words. Training chapters are scored by the model that left their book out.
- **Checks (2026-10-06):**
  - Leave-one-book-out AUC 0.948; with grammar and spelling only (no late words, which also carry topics) 0.895.
  - **Synoptic test:** trained without Samuel, Kings and Chronicles, the model scores both sides of the 26 parallel sequences between Samuel–Kings and Chronicles (`bsim sequences`, q ≤ 0.05, ≥ 40 Hebrew words a side). Chronicles comes out later in 26 of 26 (sign test p = 3 × 10⁻⁸; mean 0.77 vs 0.02), and in 25 of 26 with grammar and spelling only. Same content, two states of the language: the strongest evidence that the profile measures language and not topic.
- **What it shows (book means):**
  - Ecclesiastes is the latest-looking book outside the training set (0.45; chapters 2 and 4 at 0.99 and 0.84).
  - Ezekiel sits between the two (0.14), as its usual description as "transitional" would have it.
  - The post-exilic prophets look standard: Haggai 0.05, Malachi 0.08, Zechariah 0.19. So does Jonah (0.07), often dated late. This is the kind of evidence the critics of linguistic dating cite: late writers could write Standard Biblical Hebrew.
  - Single chapters can turn on one feature. Zechariah 12 (0.99), Amos 9 (0.56) and Zechariah 13 score late through the plene דויד; Amos 9:11–15 is the passage often argued to be a late addition, but a spelling can also be a late scribe's.
  - Ezekiel 27 scores late through בוץ in the Tyre trade list, a topical word.
  - `drivers` names the features behind every score.
- **Out of domain:** the poetic books, and chapters with half their verses parallel (in the training books Deut 32 and the Num 33 itinerary, whose short balanced lines score as parallel), are flagged. Poetry drops את and the directional ה whatever its date, so the poetic books' 0.21–0.30 is not a dating claim.
- **Served:** `dating_chapters`, `dating_books` (+ `meta.dating`); `/dating`, `/dating/book/{id}`, `/unit-dating/{unit}`. Viewer: Overview → Language (book ranges, the checks, the largest synoptic gaps, a book's chapters with their drivers) and a profile line on chapter pages.

### 16.25 Your labels: a third gold set (`store/labels.py`, `eval/labels.py`, `bsim eval-labels`, `/labels`)
Both gold sets lean towards famous links (§8.4): Sefaria follows the commentary tradition, OpenBible the Treasury of Scripture Knowledge. Neither says which of the pairs a system proposes are *not* connections, and Discoveries is made of exactly the pairs neither records.
- **Labelling:** every pair on Discoveries and in a passage's list of similar passages has *real / not / unsure* buttons; clicking the current label clears it. A label records the list that proposed the pair (`mode`, `score`) and an optional note (edited on the Labels page).
- **Storage:** `paths.labels` (`data/labels.sqlite`), a small SQLite file apart from `results.sqlite`, which `bsim build-db` rebuilds and the API opens read-only. A pair is undirected and stored once, the unit that starts earlier first. `PUT` / `DELETE /api/labels` write it; their responses are `no-store`, unlike the rest of `/api`. `serve.labels_writable: false` makes writes 403 and the viewer shows labels without buttons. CORS allows PUT and DELETE for the Vite dev server.
- **Live reading** (`/api/labels/eval`, the Labels page): per unit type and mode, from the DB's stored lists, how many real and not pairs the mode *finds* (either unit ranks the other within `labels.k`), the precision of what it finds, and the AUC of the reciprocal rank (outside the list = 0) once there are `labels.min_pairs` of each.
- **`bsim eval-labels`:** the real verse pairs of `labels.split` (dev; a pair's split is the Sefaria rule of its two books, as for OpenBible) as gold for the final verse systems (recall / MRR / nDCG on the top-k files), the same separation over that split's judged pairs, and how many real pairs Sefaria or OpenBible already have. Writes `artifacts/eval/labels.{json,md}`. Not part of `bsim all`: it needs labels.
- **Bias:** labels are judged on what the viewer showed, mostly the top of one mode's lists, so the numbers describe that sample; they are not used to choose or tune anything.

### 16.26 Syntax: clauses, phrases and who speaks (`bsim syntax`, `data/bhsa.py`, `lexical/syntax.py`, `analysis/speech.py`, `/speech`)
The ETCBC's BHSA annotates the WLC with phrases (and their function: predicate, subject, object, complement, adjunct, time, place …), clauses (type: wayyiqtol with or without a subject, x-qatal, nominal, participle, infinitive …; kind; relation) and text types (`txt`: narration N, quotation Q, discourse D, with the embedding, e.g. `?NQQ`), plus the clause-atom hierarchy. D3 left it out; it adds what OSHB morphology cannot: how words group and who speaks.
- **Data:** `bsim download --only syntax` fetches 18 Text-Fabric feature files of the 2021 data at a pinned commit (`sources.bhsa`; no `gloss`, D5). `data/bhsa.py` reads Text-Fabric directly (node / edge features, ranges, implicit nodes, empty values) rather than depending on the `text-fabric` package.
- **Alignment:** BHSA words follow the ketiv and split off ו, the article and prepositions (the article after a preposition has no consonants); OSHB keeps prefixes and reads the qere. Per verse the two consonant strings are aligned (difflib) and each BHSA word joins the OSHB word most of its consonants fall in. All 23,206 verses map; 22,108 have identical consonants (the rest: ketiv / qere and minor spelling).
- **Clause shapes as retrieval** (`bm25_syntax`): per clause its type, the type with its phrase functions in order, its relation, its text type, and the type pair with the clause before. Dev (`bsim retrieval-exp`): as a third list in the fusion +0.0005, CI [−0.0031, +0.0044], not adopted; in place of `bm25_morph` as the structural list −0.042 (Sefaria) / −0.024 (OpenBible) — it finds other verses (top-10 overlap 0.075) and keeps the text type better (0.82 of a verse's top 10 share it, vs 0.72); `bm25_morph_syntax` (both token sets): +0.0039, CI [−0.0007, +0.0086] on Sefaria, +0.0036, CI [+0.0015, +0.0058] on OpenBible — under the bar set beforehand (the Sefaria CI must exclude 0), so `structural` stays `bm25_morph`. What the clause shapes find is still telling: Gen 22:1 (ויקרא … ויאמר הנני) → Gen 22:11, 1 Sam 3:16, Gen 27:18, Gen 22:7, the call-and-answer scenes; so each verse page lists its top `syntax.neighbors` as *built the same way* (`syntax_neighbors`, 232k rows) instead of a sixth mode (~90 MB more, past the 600 MB `store.max_size_mb` of the time; since raised to 4 GB).
- **Speakers:** a quotation starts at a clause whose first clause atom's mother lies in a clause of a shallower text level: that clause introduces it (through לאמר to the clause it depends on). The speaker is the head of the introduction's subject phrase — a name, a participle used as a noun, or a noun whose SDBH sense here is a being or a name of a person or deity (`speaker_domains`; בני ישראל → ישראל) — `explicit`; for an introduction without a subject in the third person (ויאמר), the subject of the nearest of the 3 clauses before it at its level, if it agrees in gender (number is not checked: collectives take plural verbs) — `carried`; a quotation without its own speaker (a first / second person introduction, ואמרת; no introduction found) is inside the nearest enclosing quotation that has one — `enclosing`; a quotation ends when the text returns to a shallower level. Of 52,937 quotation clauses: explicit 17,925, carried 8,700, enclosing 4,696, unknown 21,616 (mostly psalms and oracles, which do not say who speaks).
- **Checks** (hand-judged, random quotation starts, drawn after the rules were set): 42 of 50 speakers right — explicit 29 / 31, carried 13 / 19; of 20 `enclosing` ones about 14 are reasonable readings of "within the speech of". The rules were tuned on an earlier sample of 60 (about 37 / 39 right there). The viewer labels inferred and enclosing speakers as such, with these rates.
- **Who speaks where** (`analysis/speech.py`, at build-db): every OSHB word takes its clause's text type and speaker; per chapter and book the shares of narration, speech, discourse, divine speech (`syntax.divine`: יהוה, אלהים, אדני) and speech with a speaker. Leviticus is 74 % divine speech (God's laws to Moses, passed on through ואמרת), Ezekiel 61 %, Jeremiah 54 %, Obadiah 98 %; Esther, Kings and Chronicles are mostly narration; Deuteronomy is 74 % speech but only 20 % attributed, because Moses' discourse opens without an introducing clause.
- **Clause mode (D62):** after `store.max_size_mb` was raised to 4 GB, the clause shapes became the sixth viewer mode, `syntax` ("Clauses"): `bm25_syntax` for verses, `tfidf_syntax` (unit TF-IDF over the same tokens) for chapters, pericopes and parashot, in `matches` and `discoveries` like the other modes (DB 530 → 616 MB). It is not fused and is not offered for free-text search (no clauses for arbitrary text).
- **Served:** `clauses`, `syntax_phrases`, `syntax_neighbors`, `speech_chapters`, `speech_books`, `speakers` (+ `meta.syntax`), empty without `bsim syntax`; `/syntax/{unit}`, `/speech`, `/speech/book/{id}`. Viewer: Overview → Who speaks (books as stacked bars, a book's speakers and chapters), and *Clauses and speakers* on every passage (each clause with its type, its phrases under their function, the speaker of a quotation and how it was found; *built the same way* on verse pages).
- **Not done:** whole-unit chiasm over clauses; chiasm in clause pairs and repeated words is §16.33. Addressees: §16.44.

### 16.27 Who borrowed (`bsim borrowing`, `analysis/borrowing.py`, `/borrowing`)
For two passages that run parallel in two books, which one drew on the other? The question has a long text-critical literature; here only signs that can be counted, set before looking at any result and checked on the cases scholars agree on.
- **Material:** the cross-book parallel sequences that `bsim diffs` aligned word by word (q ≤ 0.05): 43 sequences over 15 book pairs. A = the side earlier in the canon; each sign is > 0 when B looks later.
- **Signs:** *language* — the seven Late Biblical Hebrew features of §16.24 over each side's verses, rates shrunk to the corpus, each difference in units of its spread over chapters, signed by the literature (`borrowing.language_signs`; not the fitted dating model, which was trained on Chronicles); *spelling* — among `spelling` changes, which side is fuller (ו / י); *smoothing* — among substitutions, whether the rarer lemma was replaced by a commoner one (lectio difficilior); *expansion* — added minus omitted words (lectio brevior). A diff sign needs `min_ops` (2) changes of its kind.
- **Check:** `borrowing.known` — 1–2 Samuel, 1–2 Kings → Chronicles; Genesis, Joshua → 1 Chronicles (genealogies, Levitical cities); Psalms → 1 Chr 16; 2 Kings → Jer 52; Exodus → Deuteronomy (Decalogue): 35 sequences. Language right 30 / 35 (sign test p 2e-5), spelling 29 / 31 (p 5e-7), smoothing 15 / 28 (p 0.85), expansion 15 / 33 (p 0.73): the two text-critical canons do not hold here (Chronicles both abridges and expands, and keeps rare words as often as it replaces them). All four voting: 21 of 24 decided right, 11 ties.
- **Voting signs:** those right more often than not with sign test p < `select_p` (0.05) on the check — language and spelling. Because that choice uses the check, it is validated by leaving each known book pair out, choosing on the rest and scoring the left-out pair: 30 of 32 decided right, 3 undecided.
- **Caveat stated on the page:** every accepted direction runs from the earlier book in the canon to the later one, so canon order would pass this check too; the signs never see it, but any sign of "late Hebrew" also tracks canon position. A later-looking side may also come from a common source or a later copyist.
- **Results on the disputed pairs:** Nehemiah 7 → Ezra 2 in both sequences (Williamson's view against the older Ezra → Nehemiah); 2 Kgs 18–20 → Isaiah 36–39 for the second part (Isa 38:21–39:8), unclear for the first; 2 Sam 22 ↔ Ps 18 unclear (language says the Psalm is older, spelling says it is later); Exod 29 → Lev 8 and Lev 11 → Deut 14; Neh 11 ↔ 1 Chr 9 unclear. Two accepted pairs come out wrong: Ps 105 ↔ 1 Chr 16 (spelling and language both make the Psalm look later) and 2 Kgs 25 ↔ Jer 39.
- **Served:** `borrowing_sequences`, `borrowing_books` (+ `meta.borrowing`); `/borrowing`, `/borrowing/sequence/{id}`, `/borrowing/between`. Viewer: Parallels → Who borrowed (the check, which signs vote, book pairs with each parallel's signs), and a "Which borrowed?" line on parallel sequences and on Compare.
- **Not done:** citation formulas (ככתוב, כאשר כתוב) as direct evidence — the verses that carry them (2 Chr 25:4 ‖ 2 Kgs 14:6) cite the Torah, not their parallel. The directed echo network is §16.36.

### 16.28 Speaker voices (`bsim voices`, `analysis/voices.py`, `/voices`)
- **Question:** does a speaker have a style of their own, or does the book that tells their words impose its own (roadmap: speaker idiolects)?
- **Speech:** the BHSA quotation clauses of §16.26 with their speakers (a word in two clauses keeps its last, as `speech.word_types`). Profiled: the divine names together (`divine`) and persons, i.e. speaker lemmas OSHB tags `Np` in most occurrences, minus peoples (`voices.exclude`: ישראל); common-noun speakers (king, servant, messenger, people) are not one person and stay only in the reference. A speaker needs `min_words` (300). 23 speakers, 80k of the 107k attributed words.
- **Features and distance:** the 125 stylometry features (§16.6) counted per clause (`stylometry.feature_counts`), scaled by their spread over chapters (as §16.12); Delta = mean |rate difference| / sd.
- **Distinctiveness:** Delta between a speaker and the pooled other attributed speech of the books they speak in. Null: speaker labels permuted over the attributed quotation clauses of each book (999 shuffles; clause lengths and each book's mix kept), p by `empirical_p`, BH q; `effect` = (Delta − null mean) / null sd ranks speakers of different sizes fairly.
- **Results:** 18 of 23 distinct at q ≤ 0.05; one more shuffle scored as observed passes 0 of 23; with explicitly introduced speech only (11 speakers) the effects agree at Spearman ρ 0.88. Most distinct: God, Solomon, Moses, Daniel, Deborah, Hezekiah, Jeremiah. Not distinct: Saul, Abraham, Eliphaz, Zophar, Bildad.
- **Caveat:** the features include the 100 most frequent content lemmas, so a speaker with one long set piece stands out partly by its topic: Solomon's words are mostly the Temple prayer (1 Kgs 8 // 2 Chr 6: עבדך, שמע). Distinct means "unlike the others speaking in these books", not "an idiolect" in a strict sense.
- **Checks fixed in advance:**
  - *Author over character* (`cross` = d(A, narrator B) + d(B, narrator A) − d(A, narrator A) − d(B, narrator B); null: the speaker's clauses re-split between the two corpora): David, 1–2 Samuel (1,969 words) vs 1 Chronicles (1,004): cross 0.153, p 0.001 — each portrayal sounds like its own book's narrator; God, 1–2 Kings vs 1–2 Chronicles: cross 0.046, p 0.045, the same; Solomon, 1 Kings vs 2 Chronicles: no difference between the portrayals (p 0.92), as expected where the Chronicler copies the prayer.
  - *Elihu*, named as Job's most distinct speaker: second of five (Job 2.9, Elihu 2.2, Eliphaz 0.4, Zophar −0.1, Bildad −0.5). The expectation fails; Elihu is still distinct (q 0.04) where the three friends are not. Job's speakers are almost all inferred from an earlier clause (§16.26), so this check is the least reliable.
- **Pairs:** Delta between the speakers, the narrator and unattributed speech, described only (no size adjustment); God's speech is the voice closest to narration (0.40).
- **Served:** `voice_speakers`, `voice_features`, `voice_pairs` (+ `meta.voices`), empty without the stage or without `bsim syntax`; `/voices`, `/voices/{key}`. Viewer: Who speaks → Voices (the speakers table, the checks, a Delta heatmap, a speaker's over- and under-used features, the chapters where they speak most).

### 16.29 Where the text divides (`bsim segments`, `analysis/segments.py`, `/segments`)
- **Question:** do the divisions the tradition drew (the open פ and closed ס paragraphs, the 13th-century chapters, the parashot) fall where the text itself turns? And where do text and division disagree?
- **Score of a boundary** (a *gap*, named by the verse after it; none across books): for a window of `w` verses on each side, the cosine of the summed idf-weighted content-lemma counts (lexical) and of the summed verse embeddings of the final semantic system (semantic). TextTiling depth (Hearst 1997): how far the similarity lies below the highest points reached by climbing the curve on each side; ties (depth 0 on every local peak) broken by the dissimilarity. Each depth becomes a within-book percentile, and the score is the within-book percentile of their sum: 0.5 is a typical gap, near 1 a book's sharpest turns.
- **Window:** from `segments.window_grid` (2, 3, 4, 6 verses) on the dev books only, by the mean score at the MAM breaks: 4 (0.568; 2 and 3 within 0.002, 6 lower at 0.557).
- **Divisions:** MAM `break_after` and OSHB `oshb_break` (a mid-verse break snapped to the end of its verse, §4), chapter starts, parasha starts, style seams (§16.12).
- **Tests** (999 within-book shuffles, `stats.permute_within`): the mean score of each kind of division against the same count of gaps drawn at random in each book; contrasts between two kinds with their labels shuffled among their gaps in each book; one shuffled MAM labelling scored as observed (calibration); the share of style seams within `near_seam` = 2 verses of a paragraph break or chapter start against random gaps; per book, the top-K gaps (K = the reference's boundaries) against the MAM breaks and against the chapters by Pk and WindowDiff (k = half the mean reference segment), against K random gaps.
- **Results** (2026-10-09, 23,167 gaps, 25 s): every kind of division scores above chance (p 0.001 each): MAM open 0.650 (1,565), closed 0.554 (1,878); Leningrad open 0.653, closed 0.565; parasha starts 0.771 (49); chapter starts 0.708 (890); style seams 0.611 (241); gaps with no division 0.482. The semantic signal carries more than the lexical one (open breaks 0.650 vs 0.618, parashot 0.834 vs 0.660).
  - *Open over closed:* +0.095 (p 0.001): the פ marks a larger turn than the ס, as its name implies.
  - *Two traditions:* breaks both MAM and Leningrad mark 0.604 (2,912) against breaks only one marks 0.550 (740), p 0.001: where the manuscripts disagree the text turns less.
  - *Chapters:* chapter starts at a paragraph break 0.728 (725), without one 0.616 (165), p 0.002. The chapters that ignore the paragraphs still fall at turns more often than chance, but less sharply.
  - *Seams:* 68 % of style seams lie within 2 verses of a paragraph break or chapter start, against 55 % of random gaps (p 0.001).
  - *As a segmentation:* the top-K turns match the MAM paragraphs better than random in 18 of 38 books (mean Pk 0.399 vs 0.463) and the chapters in 10 of 38 (0.438 vs 0.487); short books have too few breaks to tell.
  - *Calibration:* a shuffled labelling scores 0.507, p 0.065.
- **Lists** (`kind`): 250 *unmarked turns* (score ≥ `turn_min` = 0.97, the highest within ± 3 gaps, no division within a verse), e.g. Isa 38:21 (the poultice verses after Hezekiah's psalm, in 2 Kgs 20:7 before it), Job 1:20, Gen 39:20, Isa 36:21; 20 *chapters in running text* (no paragraph break in either tradition, score ≤ 0.25), led by known disputed divisions: Gen 47:1, Isa 64:1 (63:19b runs on), Hag 2:1 (the date of 1:15b), Deut 6:1, Hos 6:1 (לכו ונשובה answers 5:15), Song 7:1; 114 *quiet breaks* (MAM break, score ≤ 0.05), mostly lists divided item by item: the curses of Deut 27, Lev 18, the Levitical cities of Josh 21, Neh 7, 1 Chr 25.
- **Served:** `segment_gaps`, `segment_books` (+ `meta.segments`), empty without the stage; `/segments`, `/segments/gaps?kind&book&unit`, `/segments/book/{id}`; a `divisions` dossier entry (D64) for the unit's opening gap and the flagged gaps inside it. Viewer: Patterns → Divisions (the tests table and contrasts, a book's boundary scores coloured by paragraph type with chapter lines, the three lists with the two verses around each boundary).
- **Limits:** cohesion is one reason to divide a text, not the only one (a list item by item, a reading by the calendar); a window of 4 verses blurs a turn by a verse or two and lets two similar neighbours (Ps 106 / 107, both opening הודו לה׳ כי טוב) hide a real boundary; the contrasts were named after a first look at the data (D65).

### 16.30 Written and read: ketiv and qere (`bsim ketiv`, `analysis/ketiv.py`, `/ketiv`)
- **Question:** what kinds of difference separate what the Masoretic text writes (ketiv) from what it reads (qere), where they cluster, and what they say about the text's history?
- **Pairs:** the corpus reads the qere (`text.kq`), so the written words are re-read from the raw OSHB (`data.oshb.kq_pairs`: ketiv words before a variant note pair with the note's qere words; either side may be empty). Each pair keeps both sides' text, lemmas and morphology and its position among the verse's words (checked against the corpus's qere words; where several OSHB verses make one verse, the earlier ones' words are counted first). 1,260 pairs, 8 s.
- **Letters** (consonants, final forms folded): `vowel_letter` (only א ה ו י inserted or dropped; `fuller` = the longer side) 583, `swap` (one letter replaced) 457, `vowel_position` (a vowel letter moved: הלוך / הולך) 56, `metathesis` (consonants reordered: בעברות / בערבות) 7, `division` 25, read-not-written 9, written-not-read 6, the same letters 5, `other` 112.
- **Grammar** (OSHB lemmas and morphology): same word and form 470, same word in another form 545, another word 230; the features that differ are listed per pair (`number s>p`, `suffix 3ms>1cs`, `prefix b>k`). Euphemisms (`ketiv.euphemisms`, lemma pairs): 14 (עפלים / טחרים, ישגלנה / ישכבנה, חריהם / צואתם, שיניהם / מימי רגליהם).
- **Findings** (named after a first look at the data, so given as descriptions with their p):
  - *Look-alike letters:* 71 % of the one-letter swaps are between letters alike in the square script (`ketiv.lookalike`: ו / י, ד / ר, ב / כ, ה / ח) against 3.9 % expected from the letters' frequencies; without ו / י, 12 % against 1.3 % (p 1e-12). ו / י alone: 304 swaps, ×25; ב / כ ×6. ה / ו (45, ×5) is the archaic 3ms suffix written ה and read ו.
  - *Late spelling:* where only a vowel letter differs, the written form is the fuller one in 82 % of the pairs of the Late Biblical Hebrew books (`dating.late`, 114 pairs) and 40 % elsewhere (469); with the late label permuted over the 36 books, p 0.006. The later the book, the fuller its written spelling against the reading.
  - *Parallels:* where an aligned parallel passage (§16.7–16.8) has the word, it writes the qere 57 times and the ketiv 11 (29 neither; p 1e-8 against even odds): the reading is often an attested text, not only a correction.
  - *Plurals:* 142 readings make a singular plural; 82 of them are a written ־ו read ־יו, the older defective spelling of the same plural suffix (ידו / ידיו).
  - *Spread:* Daniel 19.6 per 1,000 words, Lamentations 14.3, Ruth 10.0, Proverbs 10.0, Ezra 9.9, 2 Samuel 8.1; uneven across books (χ² against word counts, p ≈ 1e-160).
- **Served:** `kq_pairs`, `kq_letters`, `kq_books` (+ `meta.ketiv`), empty without the stage; `/ketiv`, `/ketiv/pairs`; a `ketiv` dossier entry (D64). Viewer: Patterns → Written and read (the findings, the kinds and grammar, the rate per book, the letter swaps, the grammatical differences in words, every pair with the read words marked in its verse and the parallel that writes it).
- **Limits:** one OSHB tradition of ketiv / qere (the Leningrad codex; other manuscripts and the Masorah lists differ in count); the class is read off the consonants, so a grammatical change by one letter (הוא / היא) is a `swap`; a pair's parallel is the first aligned one found.

### 16.31 Explicit citations (`bsim citations`, `analysis/citations.py`, `/citations`)
- **Question:** the text sometimes says it repeats or fulfils another text. Which source does each such verse point to, and do the formulas point to single verses at all?
- **Formulas** (`citations.families`, regular expressions over the consonantal text, first family wins): `written` (ככתוב, ככל הכתוב, כתוב בספר: sought in the Torah) 36 verses; `word` (דבר יהוה … אשר דבר / קרא, אשר דבר ביד: the fulfilment notices, sought in anything earlier) 31; `command` (כאשר / ככל אשר צוה יהוה את משה: sought in the Torah) 49.
- **Source:** every allowed verse (earlier, ≥ `min_distance` = 3 verses away, in the Torah where the family says so) scored by the mean of the citing verse's BM25 (final lexical index) and embedding cosine (final semantic system), each z-scored over the allowed verses; the best 5 are kept.
- **Resolved:** BM25 alone and cosine alone pick verses within `agree_window` = 3 of the source. The best score itself is no evidence: random verses of the same books find matches as strong (the first null tried, a percentile of the best score among random verses' best scores, gave every citation q > 0.4), because the corpus repeats its formulas. Each family's agreement is therefore set against the agreement of 500 random verses under the same rules (one-sided binomial).
- **Results** (2026-10-09, 3 s): 116 citations, 41 resolved. *As commanded:* 27 of 49 (55 %) against 32 % of random verses, p 0.0008: the compliance notices point to the command they carry out (Exod 39 → Exod 28, Lev 8 → Exod 29). *The word fulfilled:* 10 of 31 (32 %) against 27 %, p 0.32; *as it is written:* 4 of 36 (11 %) against 8 %, p 0.36. Neither does better than chance as a family: "as it is written" mostly invokes the law as a whole (Deut 29, 2 Chr 34), and a prophecy and its fulfilment share few words. The few that resolve are the well-known ones: 2 Kgs 14:6 and 2 Chr 25:4 → Deut 24:16, Josh 8:31 → Deut 27:5, Neh 8:15 → Lev 23:40, 1 Kgs 16:34 → Josh 6:26, 1 Sam 28:17 → 1 Sam 15:28, 2 Kgs 15:12 → 2 Kgs 10:30 (Jehu's four generations). A fulfilled word comes a median 637 verses after its source.
- **Check** (`citations.gold`, 20 citations with sources named by scholarship: the Deuteronomistic prophecy–fulfilment notices of Kings, the Torah quotations of Kings, Chronicles, Ezra–Nehemiah, the garments of Exod 39): 15 find the named source first, 17 within the best 5; all 10 resolved ones are right (precision 10 / 10, recall 10 / 15 of the rank-1 hits).
- **Served:** `citations`, `citation_books` (+ `meta.citations`), empty without the stage; `/citations`, `/citations/list`; a `citations` dossier entry (D64), citing from the unit or resolved into it. Viewer: Parallels → Citations (each family against random verses, the gold check, who cites whom by book, every citation with its verse, its source and the other candidates, a link to Compare).
- **Limits:** only the formulas listed (no unmarked allusion, §16.32); in synoptic texts the source found is often the parallel copy of the same notice (2 Chr 10:15 → 1 Kgs 12:15, Isa 39:8 → 2 Kgs 20:19), not the prophecy; the source is one verse, where many citations summarise a passage or a whole law; the gold list is short and was named from the literature, not drawn at random.

### 16.32 Rare words over a few verses (`bsim allusions`, `analysis/allusions.py`, `/allusions`)
- **Question:** an allusion can borrow a few rare words scattered over two or three verses without quoting a phrase (Deut 29:16–18 and Jer 9:12–14: לענה, ראש, שררות). Verse-level retrieval misses such a pair when no single verse carries enough of them; do passages share rare words more closely than their chapters' subjects explain?
- **Rare words:** content lemmas by Strong's number (sense letters dropped) found in 2 to `rare_max` = 15 verses, proper names, gentilics and Aramaic left out (lists and the Aramaic chapters would dominate); 2,431 lemmas, weight = idf over verses.
- **Pairs:** windows of `window` = 3 consecutive verses of one chapter; two windows of different chapters sharing ≥ `min_shared` = 3 rare lemmas score their idf sum; overlapping pairs keep the strongest. **Null:** verse order shuffled within every chapter (20 times): each chapter keeps its vocabulary and only the gathering of the words within a few verses is destroyed; q is the null ratio of §16.7. **Known:** a verse pair of the two windows is in the fused top 10 or a parallel sequence.
- **Result** (2026-10-09, 5 s): 176 window pairs, 171 of them known parallels (the Torah's repeated lists, Kings / Isaiah, Samuel / Psalm 18). Three beat the shuffles at q ≤ 0.05, all known. The 5 new pairs are real echoes — the Urim and Thummim of Exod 28:29–30 in Deut 33:8, the wormwood and gall of Deut 29:16–18 in Jer 9:12–14, the hospitality meal of Gen 18:4–6 and 1 Sam 28:22–24, Lev 13 and 14 on skin disease, the attribute formula in Ps 103 and Neh 9 — but each is as strong as the shuffles give (best q 0.82). A three-verse window with three shared words cannot tell an allusion from two passages on one subject; the new pairs are leads.
- **Served:** `allusions` (+ `meta.allusions`), empty without the stage; `/allusions?known&book&unit`; an `allusions` dossier entry (any q). Viewer: Phrases → Spread over verses (new pairs by default, the rare words, both passages with the words marked, q, Compare).
- **Limits:** three shared rare words is a low bar and a long allusion would be split into windows; rarity is by verse count over the whole corpus, so a word common in one book's subject (Lev 13–14) counts as rare.

### 16.33 Chiasm at the small scale (`bsim mirrors`, `analysis/mirrors.py`, `/mirrors`)
- **Question:** chiasm (A B … B′ A′) is held to be a favourite figure of biblical style, but the whole-unit test (§16.2, §16.14) finds none beyond chance. Is it the habit where the figure is said to live — repeated words inside a verse, the parts of two parallel clauses?
- **Words:** in a verse that uses two lemmas (Strong's numbers) exactly twice each with overlapping occurrences, the order is mirrored (x y … y x) or repeated (x y … x y), equally likely under a random order. A two-word phrase repeated whole (adjacent both times) is a *bigram*, set apart as formula. Each verse votes for its majority order (sign test); the pair share gets a 95 % interval by resampling chapters (2,000). Poetry = the poetic books and chapters of §16.24.
- **Clauses:** two consecutive BHSA clauses of one verse with the same two constituents (`mirrors.functions`: verb, subject, object, complement, adjunct, place, time) keep or reverse their order; the reversed share of poetry against prose is tested with the genre label permuted over chapters (9,999).
- **Results** (2026-10-09, 16 s):
  - *Repeated words keep their order:* mirrored in 2,099 verses, repeated in 2,790 (p 5e-23; 43 % of 13,544 overlapping pairs mirrored, chapters resampled 42–44 %; 2,137 bigrams apart). Poetry is no exception (39 %, 231 verses, interval 30–49 %): the figure exists but is not the habit.
  - *Poetry mirrors its clauses:* 23.5 % of the clause pairs in poetry (506) reverse their constituents against 16.7 % in prose (2,670), p 0.0003; object and verb 29 % vs 19 %, complement and verb 18 % vs 12 %, adjunct and verb 29 % vs 20 %. Subject and verb is the exception (23 % vs 29 %): prose reverses it more.
  - *Full mirrors* (≥ 3 lemmas all nested, no repeated order): 94 verses (Exod 32:16 הלחת … מעשה אלהים … מכתב אלהים … הלחת, Jer 11:10, 1 Chr 11:19); against their own words shuffled none survives BH (lowest q 0.08): leads.
- **Served:** `mirror_verses`, `mirror_clauses` (+ `meta.mirrors`), empty without the stage; `/mirrors`, `/mirrors/verses`, `/mirrors/clauses`; a `mirrors` dossier entry (full mirrors + mirrored clause pairs). Viewer: Structure → Small scale (both tests, the constituent table, clause pairs with the two parts marked in two colours, full mirrors marked by depth).
- **Limits:** only lemmas used exactly twice (Gen 9:6 has אדם three times and is missed); clause pairs inside one verse only; BHSA constituent labels as given.

### 16.34 ETCBC's parallel passages as a third gold set (`bsim eval-etcbc`, `eval/etcbc.py`)
- **Why:** Sefaria's links (the commentary tradition, §8.4) and OpenBible's (crowd votes, mostly thematic, §16.13) are both about meaning. ETCBC's *parallels* notebook (Roorda, Naaijer; Text-Fabric `crossref`, CC BY-NC, pinned in `sources.etcbc_parallels`) links verses whose text is nearly the same (similarity ≥ 75 %) — a string-similarity gold, for the synoptic parallels and the repeated passages. Evaluation only; nothing is tuned on it. *Since M64 (§16.41) its train split is training data for the semantic model and its dev split a check in choosing that model; the figures below are from before.*
- **Pairs:** 31,742 edges between BHSA verse nodes, mapped to verse ids by book, chapter and verse (all mapped), undirected, neighbours dropped: 15,618 pairs. **Formulas are not parallels:** וידבר יהוה אל משה לאמר has 157 partners, the offerings of Num 7 dozens; a pair counts only when both verses have at most `etcbc.max_partners` = 5 partners — 1,973 parallels, 13,645 formula and list pairs set apart. Split by the Sefaria book rule; dev: 234 directed pairs over 179 query verses.
- **Retrieval (dev, nDCG@10):** lexical 0.853 (recall@10 0.94), fused 0.832 (0.93), semantic 0.749, structural 0.679, domain 0.610, syntax 0.329. Near-verbatim parallels are found almost always; lexical is best, as a string-similarity gold predicts. Fused recall@10 by similarity: 75–84 % 0.90 (168 pairs), 85–94 % 0.96 (54), 95–100 % 1.00 (12).
- **The parallel analyses** (all 1,973 parallels): 94 % are in the fused top 10, 80 % share a phrase (§16.1), 38 % lie in a strong sequence (§16.7; a sequence needs a run of verses, most parallels are single verses); 56 % of the 1,317 verse pairs of the strong sequences are ETCBC parallels — the rest are reworded parallels a string measure misses.
- **Overlap:** 11 % of the dev parallels are Sefaria links, 20 % OpenBible references: the commentary seldom links a verse to its near-copy.
- **Served:** `artifacts/eval/etcbc.json` + `etcbc.md` (and `data/processed/etcbc_links.parquet`, written by `build-golds` since M64); `/eval` → `etcbc`; Evaluation page: a third section (both tables, the bands, the coverage line).

### 16.35 Incremental `bsim all` (`bsim/incremental.py`, `paths.pipeline_state`)
- **Why:** with 40 stages, a full `bsim all` retrained both models (half an hour) to change one analysis, and `--from` left it to you to know what depends on what. Staleness was not even recorded faithfully: `sound`, `parallelism`, `senses`, `map` and `network` read `structure.leitwort_skip_pos` without putting it in their meta `config_hash` (now `config_hash` takes dotted keys, and they do).
- **Fingerprint** (stored after each successful stage in `artifacts/pipeline_state.json`): *code*, the stage function, the module-level helpers it calls and every `bsim` module reachable through their import statements (parsed, not imported, and hashed as syntax trees, so reformatting, comments and line endings change nothing; editing `stats.py` reruns what uses it, editing `config.py` reruns everything); *config*, every key the stage read, recorded by passing it a `TrackedConfig` (a dict that logs `section.key` reads, a whole section when one is iterated or dumped; deep copies keep logging), so a key a stage forgot to hash still counts; *inputs*, the content hash of every file under `pipeline.roots` it opened for reading (a `sys.addaudithook` on `open`, which `pd.read_parquet`, `np.load`, `json` and SQLite go through) plus every output of the stages listed in `pipeline.DEPS` for it; *outputs*, every file that appeared or changed under the roots while it ran.
- **Skip rule:** same code hash, same value for every recorded config key, every input with its recorded hash, no new output from a declared stage, every output still on disk unchanged. Hashes are SHA-256 of the content, cached by size and mtime, so a stage that reruns and writes identical bytes leaves its dependents skipped (meta files with a `built_at` time do not, by design of what they record).
- **Declared graph:** `pipeline.DEPS` names, for each stage, the earlier stages whose outputs it reads. It is needed where the hook is blind: model folders read by native code (safetensors), systems found by globbing, inputs only checked for existence (voices without syntax), and stages adopted without a run. A read of a file written by a stage that `DEPS` does not list is noted in the log, so the graph can be checked against what stages really open; reads of a later stage's files are ignored (no cycles).
- **Flags:** `--force` (run every selected stage), `--rerun S` (run S; what depends on it follows only if its outputs change), `--dry-run` (each stage: run and why, maybe (after which stages), or skip), `--adopt` (record the selected stages as up to date without running: a stage with a record keeps it, its hashes taken as they are now, for a change known not to matter, and the outputs of its `DEPS` join its inputs (a dependency declared since it ran, M64); one without gets every section its code names as config and no inputs or outputs, so the first rerun of a stage it depends on reruns it). `--from`, `--to`, `--skip` select as before.
- **Measured** (2026-10-10): adopting download … evaluate and running the 26 analysis stages and build-db took 17 min (a first run stopped in `senses` on a console encoding error; the rerun skipped the ten stages already done and went on from there); a second `bsim all` with nothing changed took 2.3 s (40 stages up to date); `--rerun mirrors` ran mirrors (27 s) and build-db (3:09; `mirrors.meta.json` carries a build time, so build-db follows) and skipped the other 38 stages: 3 min 42 s, against 38 min for the whole pipeline.
- **First use** (M54): moving G² and `shuffle_within` into `stats.py` touched 8 modules; `--dry-run` listed the 21 stages whose code reaches them (and borrowing, dating, eval-etcbc after them), the 16 stages before were adopted, and `bsim all` reran those 24 stages in 13 min 30 s (the other 16 skipped). 65 of the 69 outputs were identical; entities' name links and parallelism's word pairs held the same rows with ties re-sorted (G² equal to the last digit, summed in another order); senses' examples and network's layout also differ between two runs of unchanged code, which led to two fixes (set order fed a seeded draw and a seeded layout).
- **Files that are no stage's** (M73): the server's `/search` caches (`artifacts/{serve.cache_dir}/`, rewritten by `bsim serve` whenever the DB changes) are ignored like the labels file. One had been recorded as an output of `phrases` because a server rewrote it while that stage ran, so every viewer session after a rebuild marked `phrases` and everything after it stale; the state now forgets recorded files the scope ignores.
- **Limits:** a file read by native code outside `DEPS` (pyarrow's own reader, not used here) is invisible; Hugging Face models are pinned by name and not hashed; an output rewritten by hand with the same size within the same clock tick keeps its cached hash.

### 16.36 Who echoes whom (`bsim echoes`, `analysis/echoes.py`, `/echoes`)
- **Question:** the network of echoes (§16.17) says which chapters resemble each other, not which drew on which. Three analyses already carry a direction; together, how far do they orient the cross-book echoes, and do they agree?
- **Pairs:** the chapter network's cross-book edges (2,395) plus the chapter pairs of resolved citations and decided borrowing sequences the network misses (27, weight `explicit_weight` = 1): 2,422. a = the chapter earlier in the canon.
- **Layers, strongest first** (the first that decides a pair gives its direction): *cited* — a resolved citation (§16.31) in one chapter points to a verse of the other (sources are earlier by construction); *borrowed* — a parallel sequence over the two chapters with a borrowing estimate (§16.27; `unclear` gives none); *language* — both chapters inside the domain of the late-language profile (§16.24) with scores `language_gap` = 0.3 apart: the later-looking chapter is taken to echo the earlier-looking one. A citation and a borrowing pointing opposite ways make a *conflict*.
- **Checks fixed in advance** (`min_check` = 10 cases, or "too few to judge"): the language direction against the citations, against the spelling sign of the borrowing sequences (fuller spelling on the later side; spelling is not among the profile's features, except the plene דויד) and against the borrowing directions (not independent: the borrowing vote uses the same language features). Also counted: language directions against the canon order, and the book cycles of the cited / borrowed layers (two books each drawing on the other).
- **Results** (2026-10-10, 5 s): 459 of 2,422 pairs directed — 14 cited, 82 borrowed, 363 language, no conflict; 1,963 undecided (569 with a side outside the profile's domain, 1,394 closer than 0.3).
  - *Checks:* language against spelling 23 of 23 (sign test p 2e-7); against the citations 2 of 2 (too few: most cited pairs are Torah law cited in Kings and Chronicles, where the profiles are close); against borrowing 58 of 59 (expected).
  - *Book to book:* the synoptic histories dominate — 1 Kings → 2 Chronicles 57 pairs, 2 Kings → 2 Chronicles 51, 2 Samuel → 1 Chronicles 47, 1 Samuel → 1 Chronicles 22; Chronicles is the great echoer. Drawn on most by cited or borrowed pairs: 1 Kgs 8 (the temple dedication), 1 Kgs 7, 2 Kgs 20, 2 Sam 7 (Nathan's oracle).
  - *Contradictions:* 2 Kings ↔ Jeremiah (Jer 52 borrows 2 Kgs 25, while 2 Kgs 25 ↔ Jer 39 comes out the other way) and Psalms ↔ 1 Chronicles (Ps 105 looks later than 1 Chr 16) — the two accepted pairs §16.27 gets wrong.
  - *Against the canon* (50 of 363 language directions, 14 %): 23 run between the late books of the Writings (Daniel, Ezra, Nehemiah, Chronicles), whose canon order is no order of date; 12 come from one chapter, 1 Kgs 20 (score 0.71 through late words, אנכי and את with a suffix), so the layer makes it the echo of the 2 Kings chapters it resembles — a chapter often held to be a late addition to the stories of the northern kings; Ezek 44 → Lev 21 (the priests' rules; whether the Holiness Code depends on Ezekiel or Ezekiel on it is an open question); Judg 5 → Num 24 and Deut 33 (archaic poems inside prose books); Micah 1 → Amos 6. Leads, not findings: one feature can carry a score.
- **Served:** `echo_edges`, `echo_books`, `echo_chapters` (+ `meta.echoes`), empty without the stage; `/echoes` (checks, book pairs, chapters drawn on most), `/echoes/list?basis&directed&backward&book&unit`; an `echoes` dossier entry (directed pairs of the unit's chapters). Viewer: Network → Directions (the checks, book to book, chapters drawn on most, every pair with its evidence and a link to Compare; "against the canon order only").
- **Limits:** the language layer is the profile of §16.24, so it inherits its training on Chronicles and Genesis–Kings, its blindness in poetry and its single-feature scores; a pair of similar chapters need not be a borrowing at all (common source, shared genre); citations and accepted borrowings point forward in the canon by construction, so only the language layer can disagree with it.

### 16.37 Stresses per line and the falling lament line (`bsim meter`, `analysis/meter.py`, `/meter`)
- **Question:** Hebrew verse is counted in stresses, and the lament (*qinah*) line is said to fall — a longer member, then a shorter one (3:2, Budde) — with Lamentations 1–4 written in it. Do the accents bear that out, against poetry that is not a lament?
- **Stresses:** a display token carries one when it has a letter and does not end in a maqaf (paseq, sof pasuq and dashes carry none).
- **Lines:** the te'amim divide a verse by continuous dichotomy: a span splits at its strongest pause (`text.accents.token_levels`, prose or poetic table), the first of equal pauses being the greater (Wickes), and each part splits again. A verse is cut top-down into lines, the largest spans of at most `max_stresses` = 7 stresses; a line splits at its own strongest pause into members of `a` and `b` stresses. Lines of `min_stresses` = 4 to 7 stresses with both members stressed are measured (2:2 … 4:3). Lam 3:1 אני הגבר ראה עני | בשבט עברתו is one line, 4:2 at the zaqef.
- **Statistic:** a chapter's *net fall* = share of measured lines with `a > b` minus share with `a < b`. The lament predicts both more falling and fewer rising lines; balanced lines are neutral. Falls alone mislead: the prose accents end a verse or half-verse on a short member (tipeha before silluq or etnahta), so prose lines fall 41 % of the time and rise 31 %.
- **Test fixed in advance:** the mean net fall of the `test` chapters (Lam 1–4) against equally many chapters drawn without replacement from them and a pool (`shuffles` = 9,999). Lamentations has the prose accents, so the pool is poetry under the same accents: the prose-accented chapters, outside Lamentations, whose mean probability of parallel halves (§16.9) is in the top quarter (`pool_quantile` 0.75, prob ≥ 0.23; 174 chapters with `min_lines` = 10). The parallelism model is trained on the poetic-accent books and scores prose-accented poetry lower, so the pool is ranked within its own accent system rather than at `parallel_at`. The same test against the chapters of Psalms, Proverbs and Job (190), and over a `grid` of line lengths (3–8, 4–6, 5–7, 4–8). Two checks: the `control` chapter (Lam 5, a lament in even lines) placed among the pool, and the chapters outside Lamentations that use the noun קינה (`lament_lemma` H7015) tested like the test chapters.
- **Results** (2026-10-12, 5 s): 54,594 lines, 52,007 split into two stressed members.
  - *Lamentations falls:* net fall 0.39 (Lam 1 0.19, Lam 2 0.44, Lam 3 0.61, Lam 4 0.32) against 0.17 for the pool, p 0.008; against Psalms, Proverbs and Job (0.14) p 0.001. Every grid length agrees (pool p 0.004–0.011, poetic p 0.0004–0.004). 3:2 is Lamentations' commonest line (82 of 237); the pool's are 3:3 and 2:2, the poetic books' 3:3. Lam 3, the most regular qinah, is above 99 % of the pool, Lam 2 above 93 %, Lam 4 81 %, Lam 1 only 53 % (its lines are long and the accents divide them unevenly).
  - *Control:* Lam 5 (mostly 3:3) at the 74th percentile of the pool: not distinct, as expected — but not low either.
  - *Chapters naming a lament:* 12 chapters, net fall 0.20 against 0.17, p 0.26: not borne out. The laments themselves fall like Lamentations (Ezek 19 at the 96th percentile, Ezek 27 93rd, Amos 5 90th, Ezek 26 83rd), but the word also picks chapters that only mention one (Ezek 2, Jer 7, 2 Sam 1, 2 Chr 35).
  - *Leads:* among the pool, Isaiah 14–16 (the taunt over Babylon, the laments over Moab), Isa 52, Jonah 2 and Song 6–7 fall most. Prose lists (Num 29, Lev 1, Lev 7) fall as much, through the short member before silluq.
- **Choices made in view of the data:** the line lengths and the net-fall statistic were chosen after looking at Lamentations (falls alone and a pool set by `parallel_at` were tried first and dropped: the first is swamped by prose accents, the second leaves 2 prose-accented chapters); the grid is there to show the result does not hang on them. The קינה check was fixed before its numbers were seen.
- **Served:** `meter_lines`, `meter_chapters` (+ `meta.meter`), empty without the stage; `/meter?group&book` (the tests and the chapters by net fall), `/meter/{unit}` (every line of the unit's verses and the chapter it starts in); a `meter` dossier entry (the chapter's net fall; a finding at or above `serve.dossier.meter_pct` = 0.9 of the pool); two findings (`meter`, `laments`); CSV export `meter`. Viewer: Poetry → Meter (the test with the control, other lengths, commonest lines, the chapters naming a lament, the ranking by group and book); on unit pages, *Stresses* next to the verse halves (each line as a:b, line ends marked, the chapter's net fall).
- **Limits:** stresses are read from the accents, not from a metrical theory (the accents mark the cantillation, which can join two short words or split a long colon); the line is a span of the accent tree, which does not always coincide with a poetic line, and Lam 1's long verses show it. The test speaks of chapters, not of single lines: a falling line is common everywhere (41 % of prose lines), so no single line is evidence.

### 16.38 Root families: words of one root side by side, Leitworte by root (`bsim roots`, `analysis/roots.py`, `/roots`, `/root/{root}`)
- **Question:** a Hebrew root gives a verb, nouns and more (ברך: to bless, a blessing); Buber's Leitwort is a *root* repeated through a passage, and the cognate object sets two words of one root side by side (ויחלם חלום). The concordance and the Leitworte of §16.2 key on lemmas, which splits a root into its words. Do the roots show what the lemmas hide?
- **Families:** OpenScriptures HebrewStrong.xml derives 5,673 of its 8,674 entries from another (`from`, `the same as`, `feminine of`, …; Aramaic entries, "corresponding to" / "compare" links, patronymics and gentilics left out). Each chain is followed at most `max_depth` = 2 links. Strong's etymologies are often guesses (בית and אבן "from" בנה, אלהים → אלוה → אל → איל → אול), so a word joins its root only when it is the root's strong radicals in a Hebrew shape (`holds`: at most one prefix letter מ ת א י נ ה, vowel letters between the radicals, a doubled radical, the radicals twice over for pilpel forms, a common ending); ה ו י are weak everywhere and נ as the first radical (א stays: it is written where it is a radical); a root with fewer than `min_radicals` = 2 strong radicals keeps no family (היה). Proper names neither join nor head a family (Strong tags גדול `a n-pr-m`: a name only when every tag is one). `exclude` keeps out derivations that still pass and are wrong (nine / ninety / ninth "from" שעה, ברית "from" ברר) and the divine names (אל, אלוה, אלהים). Result: 2,864 entries joined; 1,225 families gather 4,442 of the corpus's 6,123 content lemmas.
- **Side by side:** Hebrew content words of one verse at most `window` = 3 words apart: *cognate* = a verb and a common noun of one family (different lemmas); *other* = two other words of one family; *inf_abs* = an infinitive absolute with a finite verb of the same lemma. Cognate and other pairs are counted against the content words permuted within each chapter (`shuffles` = 200; positions and verse lengths kept); the infinitive absolute is a construction of the grammar and is only counted.
- **Leitworte by root:** each chapter's content words counted by family (names and `structure.leitwort_skip_pos` left out), ranked by G² against the corpus as §16.2 ranks lemmas. Check fixed in advance (`known`): eight Leitworte scholarship names — Gen 27 ברך, Gen 37 חלם, Gen 32 פנה, Jonah 1 ירד and גדל, Ruth 1 שוב, 1 Sam 15 שמע, 2 Sam 11 שלח — ranked by root against the best rank of any of their words by lemma. *Hidden* key roots: in a chapter's top `structure.leitwort_top` by root, gathering two or more Strong's numbers there, none of them in the top by lemma.
- **Results** (2026-10-12, 35 s):
  - *Cognate pairs are chosen:* 1,677 verb + noun pairs against 406 by chance (4.1×, p 0.005, the floor of 200 shuffles); other pairs 514 against 241 (2.1×; many are number phrases, בשנה השנית); 473 infinitive absolutes. Per 1,000 Hebrew words Leviticus leads (12.3: חטא חטאת, זבח זבח), then Ezekiel and Numbers; Psalms (2.9) and Job (1.8) use them least. The infinitive absolute is rare in Chronicles (0.15 per 1,000 words against 0.9–1.2 in Kings), as the Late Biblical Hebrew literature says.
  - *Known Leitworte do not rank higher by root:* 6 of 8 are in the top 12 either way; the root ranks higher for 3 (חלם 4 → 1, ברך 3 → 2, ירד 12 → 11), lower for 3 (גדל 6 → 8, שלח 4 → 5, שמע 14 → 15), the same for 2. Most famous Leitworte are carried by one word, and gathering a root's words also raises its count in the rest of the Bible, so G² need not grow. Not borne out.
  - *Hidden key roots:* 896, leads — the verb and its adjectives in the purity law (Lev 13 טמא, טהר), the joining of the Tabernacle curtains (Exod 26 חבר), the making of the priestly garments (Exod 28, 39 עשה + מעשה), "sow" and "seed" in Gen 1 (זרע), Ps 119's "delight" (שעשע).
- **Served:** `lemma_roots`, `root_pairs`, `root_books`, `root_leitworte` (+ `meta.roots`), empty without the stage; `/roots` (families, the tests, the known check, books), `/roots/pairs?kind&book&unit` (each pair with both words marked), `/roots/hidden?book&unit`, `/root/{root}` (the family with Strong's derivations and the verses holding any of its words; 404 for a word alone); `root_leitworte` on `/structure/{unit}` (computed on demand from `lemma_roots`); `roots` on `/words/{verse}`; a `roots` dossier entry (pairs in the unit); findings `cognate` and `root_leitworte`; CSV `root-pairs`, `hidden-roots`. Viewer: Patterns → Roots (side by side with the test, kinds and books; key roots with the known check and the hidden list), the root page, a root chip in the word panel, "By root" chips in the structure panel (✦ = hidden) that highlight every word of the root.
- **Limits:** the families are Strong's, filtered — homonymous roots stay merged (רעה: to shepherd, to associate), some guesses pass the shape check (דבורה "bee" in דבר), and roots with one strong radical have no family; the shuffle keeps each chapter's words, so a chapter that repeats a root everywhere (a law about sacrifices) gives its cognate pairs more chances by chance too, which the null already includes.

### 16.39 The layered reader: the analyses painted onto the words (`/read/{unit}`, `/reader/{unit}`)
- **Question:** the dossier (§10) links *out* from a unit to some thirty analyses, each on its own page. Reading a chapter, one wants them *in* the text: who speaks, where the verse halves fall, which words are the chapter's Leitworte, which sound alike, which are rare, which are shared with another verse, which are read otherwise than written, and where the text turns. A view, not a test: nothing new is claimed.
- **Layers** (`?layers=`, comma-separated; absent = speakers, Leitworte, turns; empty = none), each on its own visual channel so any can be on together: *speakers* (§16.26) a background tint per speaker, most words first (eight tints, then repeated; the unknown speaker grey); *verse halves* (§16.3) ‖ and | from `/parallelism/{unit}`; *Leitworte* (§16.2) the top 6 in bold colours, by word or by root (`lw=root`, §16.38) from `/structure/{unit}`; *shared phrases* (§16.1) underlined, thicker for more partner verses; *rare words* a small number, the verses holding the token's rarest content lemma (at most `serve.reader.rare_verses` = 3; names of §16.11 left out, else genealogies are all rare); *wordplay* (§16.4) a dashed box, a click marks the partner; *ketiv / qere* (§16.31) a small ק; *turns* (§16.29) a rule before a verse where the text turns, a chapter cuts running text or a quiet break falls, with the MAM's פ / ס; *refrains* (§16.40, added with them) a line above the words; *distress and praise* (§16.42) a bar under the word, solid for a word of praise, dashed for one of distress (its SDBH domains); *P / non-P* (§16.43) a tag after the verse number for its side of the split, outlined where the reference assigns it to the other source. Every marked word's tooltip lists what each layer says about it; a click opens the word panel. A verse is read in its chapter; prev / next keep the layers.
- **Served:** `GET /reader/{unit_id}` (up to `serve.max_compare_verses`) gathers the layers that had no unit view, keyed by display token: speech (BHSA quotation clauses → display tokens; an embedded quotation wins), phrase partners per token, rare words, sound-alike pairs with their partner, ketiv / qere with the read words, and the gap before each verse when it is flagged or a MAM break; `computed` says which stages ran. Cola and Leitworte reuse their existing endpoints. Viewer: the Reader page, linked from every unit page ("Read with layers").
- **Limits:** speakers are only as good as §16.26 (poetry and prophecy mostly unattributed, so speech without a speaker gets a dotted line rather than a tint; Deuteronomy shows as unattributed speech; a carried speaker can be wrong: in Genesis 3:1 the serpent's words go to יהוה, the subject of the clause before); Leitworte by colour stop at six; a word in a shared phrase is underlined whatever the partner (a refrain inside the chapter counts as much as a quotation elsewhere).

### 16.40 Refrains: phrases that come back inside a passage (`bsim refrains`, `analysis/refrains.py`, `/refrains`)
- **Question:** a refrain is a phrase a passage repeats at intervals — ולא שבתם עדי נאם יהוה closes each blow of Amos 4:6–11, כי לעולם חסדו every verse of Psalm 136. Shared phrases (§16.1) pair verses across the canon and Leitworte (§16.2) count single words; neither looks for a phrase that recurs nearby. Can refrains be found without being named, do they come back at even intervals, and do they stand where a passage turns?
- **Method:** each verse becomes its lemmas, prefixes dropped (ו, ה, ב, ל, כ, מ, ש vary between the repeats of one refrain). A *seed* is `min_tokens` = 3 lemmas in a row inside a verse with at least `min_content` = 2 nouns, verbs or adjectives that are not names; the verses of one book holding a seed, chained while consecutive ones are at most `max_gap` = 20 verses apart, form a *run*; a run in `min_count` = 3 or more verses is scored by its burst, the Poisson chance of that many verses in its span at the seed's rate in its book (a formula spread through a book is not a refrain where it merely recurs). Chance: the verse order shuffled within each book (`shuffles` = 20), runs found again; the false discovery rate of a burst is the mean shuffled count at least as strong over the observed count. Runs at q ≤ `alpha` = 0.05 are refrains, each extended to the longest phrase all its verses share; one per verse set and phrase. The *strongest* refrain of each group sharing a verse forms the sample of the spacing and turn tests.
- **Checks fixed in advance:** (1) nine refrains scholarship names (`known`: Genesis 1 ויהי ערב ויהי בקר, Amos 1–2 על שלשה פשעי, Amos 4 ולא שבתם עדי, Isaiah 9:11–10:4 בכל זאת לא שב אפו, Psalms 42–43, 80, 107, 136, Song of Songs השבעתי אתכם), each found when one refrain is in 3 of the verses named; holds at `known_share` = 2/3. (2) Even spacing: the coefficient of variation of the gaps against the same number of repeats placed at random between the first and the last (`draws` = 1,000); the share even at `alpha` against `alpha` (binomial). (3) At the turns: each refrain verse's sharper neighbouring gap (§16.29's within-book percentile, before or after the verse) against random verses of the same span.
- **Changed after the first run, in view of the data** (all three reported): the known check first matched any refrain *inside the range* of a known one, and the Song of Songs passed on את שאהבה נפשי (3:1–4), another repeat — it now names the verses; names first counted as content words, and a man named through his story (ישמעאל בן נתניה, Jeremiah 40–41) came out as a refrain — names no longer count; the spacing test first counted refrains too short for any placement to reach 0.05 (three repeats close together) as uneven — they are now left out as untestable. First run: 1,215 refrains, known 9 / 9 (one wrongly), even 30 / 627 (4.8 %, not borne out), turns 0.680 against 0.649.
- **Results** (2026-10-12, 6 s): 925 refrains against 45.5 runs as strong in shuffled books (551 distinct); most are lists — the census of Numbers 1, the offerings of Numbers 7 and 28–29, the genealogies of Genesis 5 and 11, the allotments of Joshua and Ezekiel 48, 1 Chronicles 25 and 27. (1) Known: **8 of 9 found**, all strong (q ≤ 0.004); the Song of Songs' adjuration is missed by design (3:5 → 8:4 is far beyond 20 verses). (2) Even spacing: **27 of 179** testable refrains (15 % against 5 %, binomial p 3 × 10⁻⁷) — holds, but the even ones are almost all lists (Genesis 5, 11; Numbers 7, 28–29) and Genesis 1. (3) Turns: **0.679 against 0.650** (p 0.001, the floor of 1,000 draws, 2,270 verses) — holds, though the difference is small. Leads among the poems and narratives: Job 1:15–19 ואמלטה רק אני לבדי להגיד לך (turn 0.95), Job 1–2 תם וישר וירא אלהים וסר מרע, Psalm 115:9–11, Isaiah 19 ביום ההוא, Ezekiel 32 חתיתם בארץ חיים, Jeremiah 36 the words of the scroll.
- **Robustness** (seed length × gap × names counted): known 6–8 / 9 in all twelve; even spacing p ≤ 0.0012 and turns p 0.001 in all twelve.

  | names | gap | seed | refrains | chance | known | even | even p | turns |
  |---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
  | out | 10 | 3 | 748 | 37 | 7 | 28 / 119 | < 0.001 | 0.680 / 0.647 |
  | out | 10 | 4 | 334 | 17 | 6 | 28 / 68 | < 0.001 | 0.677 / 0.644 |
  | **out** | **20** | **3** | **925** | **46** | **8** | **27 / 179** | **< 0.001** | **0.679 / 0.650** |
  | out | 20 | 4 | 427 | 21 | 7 | 25 / 101 | < 0.001 | 0.680 / 0.647 |
  | out | 30 | 3 | 1,011 | 50 | 8 | 22 / 214 | 0.001 | 0.680 / 0.650 |
  | out | 30 | 4 | 464 | 23 | 7 | 25 / 119 | < 0.001 | 0.682 / 0.647 |
  | in | 10 | 3 | 954 | 47 | 7 | 31 / 134 | < 0.001 | 0.679 / 0.646 |
  | in | 20 | 3 | 1,215 | 60 | 8 | 30 / 227 | < 0.001 | 0.680 / 0.649 |
  | in | 30 | 4 | 522 | 26 | 7 | 29 / 141 | < 0.001 | 0.682 / 0.649 |
- **Served:** `refrains`, `refrain_verses` (+ `meta.refrains`), empty without the stage; `/refrains` (the tests, the known table, books), `/refrains/list?book&unit&strongest&known` (each refrain with its verses, the phrase marked in the first `serve.refrain_verses` = 3); a `refrains` dossier entry (refrains with a verse in the unit); findings `refrains`, `refrain_regular`, `refrain_turns`; CSV `refrains`; the reader's *Refrains* layer (a line above the words). Viewer: Patterns → Refrains.
- **Limits:** exact lemma sequences only — a refrain that varies its words (Psalm 80's אלהים / אלהים צבאות / יהוה אלהים צבאות) is found by its shared core; the gap limit misses refrains far apart (Song of Songs); verses are the unit of distance, so a long verse and a short one count the same; refrains overlapping lists inflate the spacing test, which is why the result is read with the lists named.

### 16.41 More supervision for the encoder (`bsim supervision-exp`, `bsim build-golds`, `train/pairs.py`, `eval/supervision.py`)
- **Question:** the semantic model learns from 4,513 Sefaria links between train books (9,026 rows, both directions), the connections the commentary tradition made famous (§8.4). OpenBible's crowd-voted cross-references (§16.13) and ETCBC's near-verbatim parallels (§16.34) also link verses between train books, and mostly other ones (3 % of OpenBible's dev pairs and 11 % of ETCBC's are Sefaria links). Does the model get better when it also trains on them? *This changes the standing of both golds:* their train splits become training data and their dev splits help choose the model; their test splits, like Sefaria's, are never used.
- **Pairs** (`train/pairs.py`): each gold's train split (both books train books, by the Sefaria rule, so no dev or test book ever enters training), OpenBible pairs with at least `extra_min_votes` = 5 votes. Sefaria's rows come first in their order; a pair Sefaria links in any split is not added again, and one both other golds list is added once, under the first named. Hard negatives exclude every pair of every source used (any split). OpenBible adds 27,682 rows (13,841 pairs), ETCBC 2,074 (1,878 next to OpenBible).
- **Variants** (`supervision_experiments.variants`), each the §7.2 recipe with the same seed and a few overrides, trained into `models/berel-sup-{name}`: `seed14` (Sefaria only, seed 14: what a retrain alone moves), `etcbc`, `openbible`, `both`. Each best checkpoint is still chosen by recall@10 on Sefaria dev (as §7.2).
- **Scoring** (`bsim supervision-exp`, dev only, nDCG@10 with ±2 neighbours dropped): every verse encoded, CSLS top-50 (the semantic list), and that list fused with `bm25_lemma` by RRF (the fused list), as the pipeline does, in memory; against the Sefaria (884 queries), OpenBible (2,541) and ETCBC (179) dev golds. Gains over the Sefaria-only model are paired over queries with a 10,000-draw bootstrap CI. The in-memory baseline reproduces the stored fused list exactly (0.1895 / 0.1460 / 0.8319).
- **Rule, fixed before any training** (`eval/supervision.py:adoption`): a variant is adopted only if its fused list gains on OpenBible with a CI above 0 and by more than `seed14` moves it either way, and loses nothing significant on Sefaria or ETCBC (each CI reaching 0); among those that pass, the largest Sefaria gain. *Changed before training, not in view of results:* the first draft also required an ETCBC gain; the fused list already scores 0.83 there over 179 queries, so ETCBC became a check only.
- **Results** (2026-10-13; the four trainings took about 3¼ hours, 3.8 GB VRAM) — fused nDCG@10 gain (95 % CI); semantic list alone in brackets:

  | variant | rows (Sefaria + extra) | best step | Sefaria | OpenBible | ETCBC | passes |
  |---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
  | `seed14` | 9,026 | 100 | +0.0001 [−0.0037, +0.0038] (−0.002) | −0.0009 [−0.0033, +0.0014] (−0.002) | −0.0007 [−0.0097, +0.0085] (−0.010) | — |
  | `etcbc` | 9,026 + 2,074 | 132 | −0.0004 [−0.0053, +0.0044] (−0.001) | −0.0028 [−0.0055, −0.0001] (−0.004) | +0.0143 [+0.0030, +0.0268] (+0.032) | no |
  | `openbible` | 9,026 + 27,682 | 260 | +0.0071 [+0.0004, +0.0141] (+0.002) | +0.0063 [+0.0027, +0.0101] (+0.013) | −0.0296 [−0.0495, −0.0106] (−0.033) | no |
  | **`both`** | 9,026 + 29,560 | 310 | +0.0051 [−0.0018, +0.0122] (+0.007) | +0.0056 [+0.0017, +0.0096] (+0.012) | +0.0006 [−0.0163, +0.0174] (+0.016) | **yes** |

- **Reading:**
  - Each source teaches what it holds. ETCBC's near-copies lift ETCBC (+0.032 semantic) and cost a little on OpenBible; OpenBible's thematic links lift OpenBible (+0.013 semantic, 352 queries better, 247 worse in `both`) and, alone, cost ETCBC 0.03 — the model drifts from string likeness towards topic. Together they keep both: `both` is the only variant without a significant loss anywhere.
  - Sefaria gains a little from OpenBible (+0.005 to +0.007 fused), which is the §8.4 question: more of the crowd's notion of a link does not hurt the commentary's, and may help.
  - The gains are small: fused 0.1895 → 0.1946 on Sefaria and 0.1460 → 0.1516 on OpenBible (+3–4 %), beside a retrain alone moving ±0.001. `openbible` is best on Sefaria and OpenBible but fails the ETCBC check, as the rule says it should.
- **Adopted:** `train.supervised.extra_pairs: [openbible, etcbc]`. The model trained by the experiment is the one in use (copied to `models/berel-sup`, its meta naming `adopted_from`; the Sefaria-only model kept as `models/berel-sup-sefaria`), not a retrain, which would only add seed noise. The hard negatives mined with the pipeline's config (seeded) match the variant's run: 38,586 rows by the same sources, 45 fallbacks, 12,489 distinct negatives. (The experiment wrote every variant's negatives to one misnamed file — a loop variable shadowed the file name — so they could not be compared row by row; training used the frames in memory, and the bug is fixed and tested.) Gold pairs now come from a stage of their own, `build-golds`, right after `build-links` (OpenBible and ETCBC's evaluation stages read them); `bsim all --adopt` for build-links, train-simcse and train-sup (the change only touches `extra_pairs`), then `--rerun embed` and everything that follows.
- **After adoption** (2026-10-13, `bsim all`: 28 stages in 17 min 34 s, 17 up to date): the pipeline's own evaluation reproduces the experiment to four decimals — dev nDCG@10 fused 0.1946 (Sefaria), 0.1516 (OpenBible), 0.8325 (ETCBC); `berel_sup_csls` 0.1561; units, where the experiment did not look: chapter fused 0.168 → 0.171, pericope 0.114 → 0.118. The fusion grid keeps its weights. ETCBC parallels in the fused top 10: 94.4 % → 96.1 %. The recorded test split (M9) is unchanged and not rerun. All 18 findings keep their verdict; what reads the fused lists or the encoder moved a little — borrowing 30 / 32 → 26 / 28 right (3 → 5 undecided), the late-language check 26 → 23 parallels (all still later), echoes 23 / 23 → 21 / 21, chiasm in clauses 0.235 / 0.167 → 0.240 / 0.166, citations' chance 0.32 → 0.35 (p 0.003), strong-sequence pairs 1,317 → 1,326; earlier sections keep the figures of their own runs.
- **Served:** `artifacts/eval/supervision_experiments.json` + `.md`; `/eval` → `supervision`; Evaluation page: *More supervision* (both tables, the rule, the verdict). `bsim train-sup --extra openbible,etcbc|none` overrides the config for one run.
- **Limits:** chosen on dev among three variants, so a little of the gain is selection (the CIs do not account for choosing); the test split is not rerun, so the test figures (M9, RESULTS) are the Sefaria-only model's; Sefaria dev still picks each best checkpoint; OpenBible's and ETCBC's dev figures (§16.13, §16.34) are no longer from a model blind to those golds; Psalms stay out of OpenBible's pairs (versification).

### 16.42 Psalm genres and the lament's turn (`bsim psalms`, `analysis/psalms.py`, `/psalms`)
- **Question:** form criticism sorts the psalms by their form (Gunkel–Begrich): the hymn calls a group to praise and speaks of God, the individual lament speaks to God in the first person singular out of distress and often turns to praise before its end ("the certainty of being heard": Ps 6:9, 13:6, 22:23). Counted from the words, does the form recover Gunkel's genres? And can the lament's turn be found from the words alone?
- **Words:** each of the 150 psalms (chapters of Psalms) without its superscription: a leading run of `title_lemmas` (למנצח, מזמור, שיר, לדוד, משכיל, מכתם, תפלה, שיר המעלות, the guilds and musical terms, …) in the first verse, continued into the second when the first is all title — 320 words in 117 psalms. The run stops at the first word outside the list, so a title's historical note stays (Ps 3:1 בברחו מפני אבשלום בנו, Ps 18:1 after לעבד, Ps 60:1–2) and Ps 120's opening אל, a preposition of the psalm itself, is dropped; seen after the first run and left as it is, with titles kept as a robustness check.
- **Features** (`features`, per word): first person singular and plural, second person (finite verbs, suffixes, pronouns), imperatives, plural imperatives, interrogatives (OSHB morphology), and the shares of words with a sense in the SDBH domains of `distress` (afraid, angry, grieve, shame, sick, dead, weak, unsafe, enemies, forsake, complain, revile) and `praise` (praise, sing, rejoice, bless, worship, relief, safe, vow, musical instruments).
- **Genres** (`genres`): seven of Gunkel's — hymn (with the enthronement songs and the songs of Zion), individual lament, communal lament, royal psalm, thanksgiving, song of trust, wisdom psalm — for 119 psalms, the core cases of the standard summaries, entered by hand; the 31 mixed or disputed (Ps 9–10, 12, 14, 19, 24, 36, 40, 50, 52–53, 58, 67–68, 75, 77–78, 81–82, 94–95, 106–108, 115, 122, 124, 129, 134, 139) are left out. Not checked line by line against the 1933 text.
- **Tests, fixed before the first run:** (1) the features, standardized over all 150, grouped by Ward clustering into 7 clusters without the genres; normalized mutual information with the genres of the labelled psalms against the genres permuted (`draws` = 1,000). (2) Six profiles (`checks`), each a genre's mean against the other labelled psalms, one-sided, labels permuted, Benjamini–Hochberg: individual laments more "I" and more distress, communal laments more "we", hymns more praise, more plural imperatives and less "I". (3) The turn: each verse's mood = praise − distress words; the split of a psalm's verses (at least `min_side` = 2 a side) where the mood per word rises most is its turn, its p from 1,000 orders of its verses. Twelve turns scholarship names (`known_turns`: Ps 6:9, 13:6, 22:23, 28:6, 31:20, 54:6, 56:10, 57:8, 69:31, 102:13, 109:30, 140:13, Hebrew numbering) are found when the split starts within `tolerance` = 1 verse, against the chance of a split drawn at random (Poisson binomial over the twelve). (4) Individual laments with a turn at p ≤ 0.05 against hymns (Fisher, one-sided).
- **Results** (2026-10-13, 9 s):
  - (1) Genres: **NMI 0.262 against 0.082** with the genres shuffled (p 0.001) — holds. The clusters: hymns 24 of 31 in one cluster (with 12 unlabelled and 5 wisdom psalms); individual laments 26 of 37 in another, together with 6 of 9 thanksgivings, 5 of 11 songs of trust and 5 of 11 royal psalms — all spoken by an "I"; communal laments 6 of 11 in a "we" cluster (Ps 44, 79, 90, 123, 126, 137, with 20, 67, 95, 124); the calls to praise Ps 96, 100, 117, 134 and Ps 150 apart (plural imperatives); and a cluster of questions (Ps 14 / 53, 15, 24, 42–43, 94, 133: the entrance liturgies' "who shall ascend", the refrain מה תשתוחחי). Robustness: the form alone (persons, imperatives, questions) 0.294 (p 0.001), the domains alone 0.142 (p 0.001), titles kept 0.282 — the grammar of address carries the genre more than the vocabulary of mood.
  - (2) Profiles: **6 of 6** (q ≤ 0.008), per 100 words: individual laments "I" 24.1 against 7.5, distress 7.2 against 3.9; communal laments "we" 11.6 against 1.5; hymns praise 9.9 against 6.6, plural imperatives 5.4 against 0.7, "I" 1.3 against 14.3.
  - (3) Named turns: **7 of 12** within a verse (13:6, 28:6, 54:6, 56:10, 57:8, 69:31, 109:30) against 3.4 by chance (p 0.016) — holds, but it rests on the tolerance: exactly at the verse 4 against 1.0 (p 0.011), within 2 verses 7 against 5.6 (p 0.24).
