Missed: 6:9 (found at 6:4), 22:23 (22:18), 31:20 (31:24), 102:13 (102:3), 140:13 (140:4); why each one misses was not examined.
  - (4) Laments against hymns: **7 of 37 against 2 of 30** (p 0.13) — not borne out. A single psalm is short: 13 of 146 psalms turn at p ≤ 0.05, against about 7 by chance, so the turn of one psalm is seldom clear on its own; the 13 (Ps 5, 26, 28, 55, 102, 109, 141 among the laments; 27, 37, 46, 103, 118, 119) are listed as leads.
- **Served:** `psalms`, `psalm_verses` (+ `meta.psalms`), empty without the stage; `/psalms` (the four tests, the genres by cluster, what each cluster counts, the named turns), `/psalms/list?genre&cluster&turned&unit&sort` (each psalm with its features, genre, cluster, turn and a strip of its verses' mood); a `psalms` dossier entry (psalms the unit overlaps); findings `psalm_genres`, `psalm_forms`, `lament_turn`, `lament_turns`; CSV `psalms`; the reader's *Distress and praise* layer, for any chapter. Viewer: Patterns → Psalms.
- **Limits:** the genre list is one reading of Gunkel's, entered by hand, and some of his genres are defined by the very features counted here (the "I" of the lament), so (2) confirms the definitions are visible in the counts rather than testing a hidden pattern; SDBH domains are assigned per sense, and a word of distress in a vow of praise ("you rescued me from my enemies") counts as distress; superscriptions are removed by a word list (above); the turn is one split per psalm, so a psalm that turns twice (Ps 22, 102) or keeps lamenting after praising is described by its strongest rise.

### 16.43 The Pentateuch's sources: an unsupervised P / non-P split (`bsim pentateuch`, `analysis/pentateuch.py`, `/pentateuch`)
- **Question:** source criticism holds that a Priestly writer (P) and older non-Priestly sources were woven together in Genesis–Numbers. Koppel, Dershowitz and colleagues (Dershowitz et al., "Computerized Source Criticism of Biblical Texts", JBL 2015) split the text in two without being told where P is — chunks by their choice between synonyms, a choice a writer makes without thinking of the topic, then verses by function words — and found the split close to the critics' P. Does that hold here, and does it hold in Genesis, where both sources tell stories (so law against narrative cannot carry it)? The hypothesis is contested; the test is framed so it can fail.
- **Method** (`pentateuch` config, fixed before the first run): each verse's lemmas (with the verb stem, `@h` / `@q`) and prefixes; the divine names (`held_out`: יהוה, אלהים, אל, שדי, אדני, יה, עליון) are never features. Eighteen synonym sets (`synonyms`): P vocabulary from Driver's lists and function-word pairs — אני / אנכי, מטה / שבט, עדה / קהל, גוע / מות, אחזה / נחלה, אמה / שפחה, ברא / עשה, דבר / אמר, הוליד / ילד, נשיא / שר, שכן / ישב, גוי / עם, הוציא / העלה, את / עם (with), פן / לבלתי, הנה / הן, אך / רק, גם / אף. (SDBH's synonym relations were looked at first and set aside: they pair any words of one domain — כהן / איש, יעקב / ישראל, זהב / כסף — so they would sort by topic.) Step 1: each book is cut into chunks of `chunk_verses` = 10; a chunk is its share of each member within each set minus the set's overall share (0 for a set it does not use); the first principal component splits the 490 chunks in two, and the `core_share` = 30 % of each side furthest out (1,463 verses) are the core. Step 2: a logistic regression (standardized, C = 1) on the core verses over the 36 synonym members and the `n_function` = 60 commonest function words (prefixes and OSHB C, R, T, P, D), each per word of the verse; every verse's probability averaged over ±`smooth` = 2 verses of its book, the side above 0.5. The side holding most of Leviticus is called P-like, decided without the reference.
- **Reference** (`p`, `unassigned`): P after Noth (1948) as commonly tabulated, entered by hand in Hebrew versification (Exod 7:26–8:28 and Num 17 numbered as in the MAM), not checked line by line against a printed table: 2,787 verses; 88 verses split between sources (a half verse) or in a mixed chapter (Num 32) are unassigned; the other 2,015 are non-P.
- **Checks fixed in advance**, each against the split shifted circularly within each book (1,000 shifts; the runs are kept, their place is random): (1) Cohen's kappa with the reference over Genesis–Numbers; (2) the same within Genesis; (3) in Genesis 1:1 – Exodus 6:1 (`names_window`, before P has the name revealed in Exod 6:2–3), the share of יהוה among the divine names is lower on the P-like side. Robustness: chunks of 5, 10, 20 verses × smoothing 0, 2, 5 (200 shifts each).
- **Results** (2026-10-13, 6 s): 2,736 verses P-like.
  - (1) **Agreement 78 %, kappa 0.559** against 0.161 for shifted splits (p 0.001) — holds. Confusion: 2,216 P-like and P, 1,547 neither, 468 P-like but non-P, 571 P but not P-like. By book: Exodus 87 % and Numbers 87 % agree; Genesis 70 % (the split calls 38 % P-like against 22 % in the reference); Leviticus, all P, only 69 % P-like.
  - (2) **Genesis: kappa 0.301** against 0.002 (p 0.004) — holds, but modest: the genealogies (Gen 5 1.00, 11 0.75), the covenant of circumcision (17: 0.81), the cave of Machpelah (23: 0.75) and the flood (6–9: 0.73–0.95) come out P-like, as the critics' P; so do Gen 2 (0.80), 14 (0.62), 34 (0.94), 42 (0.76) and 49 (0.88), which they do not assign to P, and Gen 1 only 0.55.
  - (3) **Divine names: not borne out.** Before Exod 6, יהוה is 37 % of the divine names on the P-like side and 43 % on the other (gap 0.058 against −0.001, p 0.28), though in the reference itself the contrast is stark (1 % in P, 49 % outside it): the split finds the P vocabulary but places too many non-P stories with it for the names to follow.
  - **Robustness:** kappa 0.37–0.70 and Genesis 0.16–0.39 at every setting (p ≤ 0.01); the names gap is significant only with chunks of 20 verses (0.09–0.23, p 0.015–0.035) — larger chunks and more smoothing agree better with a reference drawn in long runs. The setting fixed in advance stays the reported one.
  - **What decides it** (standardized weights): toward P-like, כמו, דבר (piel), מעלה, את (with), the object marker את, גוע, הוליד, נשיא, אחזה, ל־, בעד, מטה; toward the other side, עם (with), אנכי, אמר, ו־, עמדי, אמה, עתה, עם (people), אם, כי, אחר, ה־ of the question. Synonym choices on the two sides: אני / אנכי 111 / 4 against 61 / 81; את / עם (with) 214 / 6 against 58 / 172; הוליד / ילד 60 / 25 against 2 / 78; נשיא / שר 70 / 18 against 1 / 34.
- **Served:** `pentateuch_verses`, `pentateuch_chapters` (+ `meta.pentateuch`), empty without the stage; `/pentateuch` (the checks, confusion, books, names, grid, the words that decide it and the synonym table, Hebrew forms from `lemma_gloss`), `/pentateuch/chapters?book` (each chapter's P-like share against the reference's); `source` on `/reader/{unit}` verses; a `pentateuch` dossier entry (P-like verses in the unit, linking to the reader); findings `pentateuch_split`, `pentateuch_genesis`, `pentateuch_names`; CSV `pentateuch`; the reader's *P / non-P* layer (a tag after the verse number, outlined where the reference disagrees). Viewer: Overview → The Pentateuch's sources.
- **Limits:** a two-way split cannot see J and E apart, nor H inside P (Lev 17–26); the reference is one critic's division entered by hand, and where critics differ (Gen 36, Num 20, the plagues) the agreement measures closeness to that one; the P-like side is partly a law side — Exodus 25–40, Leviticus and Numbers 1–10 hold most of P and most of the law, which is why the Genesis check matters; synonym sets and function words were chosen from the literature, not from this text, but not blind to what source critics say P's vocabulary is.

### 16.44 Who speaks to whom (`bsim syntax`, `bsim dialogue`, `analysis/dialogue.py`, `/dialogue`)
- **Question:** §16.26 says who speaks each quotation; to whom is it said? With addressees, the conversations themselves can be read: who speaks to whom in each book, the exchanges between two speakers, and whether the way people speak follows whom they speak to.
- **Addressees** (`data/bhsa.py`, part of `bsim syntax`; five more BHSA features: `nu` and the pronominal suffix `prs`, `prs_ps`, `prs_gn`, `prs_nu`). For each quotation, in order: (1) *explicit* — the head of the introduction's first complement opened by אל or ל (`addressee_preps`; ויאמר יהוה אל משה), or after צוה (`addressee_object_verbs`) its object; the head is the first word that can speak, passing over בן, אדון, בית, עבד to a later one (`addressee_pass_through`: בני ישראל, אדני המלך, בית ישראל, עבדך דוד); (2) *vocative* — the first being called in the quotation (a BHSA `Voct` phrase); (3) *pronoun* — the suffix on that preposition or verb (אליו, ויצוהו): "me" in an introduction inside a quotation is that quotation's speaker and "you" its addressee; "him / them" is a being named in the story since the previous quotation (a new scene), else the other party of the previous quotation at the same level ending at most `addressee_turn_gap` = 4 clauses before (its speaker, or its addressee when the same person speaks again), else the nearest being named before at the introduction's level that agrees with the suffix (gender; a singular suffix never stands for a plural noun); (4) *turn* — a narrated introduction with no complement at all (ויאמר, ויען) answers the previous speaker, when both speakers are known. A messenger formula inside a speech (כה אמר יהוה) gets no *turn* addressee. Each clause also records its quotation (`quote`). Speakers are unchanged (same counts as §16.26).
- **Tuning and check:** the rules were tuned on two samples (40 and 30 quotation starts: about 25 / 39 and 24 / 30 right), then frozen; a third sample of 50, drawn after that (seed 2026) and judged by hand: 42 right — explicit 21 / 21, vocative 11 / 11, turn 6 / 9, pronoun 4 / 9. The errors are mostly inherited from a wrong speaker (a carried subject, §16.26) or a resolution to the wrong earlier person. The checks below therefore rest on explicit and vocative addressees only (`reliable`), with every addressee as the sensitivity check; the viewer labels the inferred ones with these rates.
- **Coverage:** 5,489 quotations, an addressee for 2,901 (53 %): explicit 1,264, vocative 494, pronoun 622, turn 521. The most frequent pairs: God → Moses 188 quotations, God → Israel 93, God → אדם 55 (Ezekiel's בן אדם, which stands for the prophet), Moses → God 30, God → David 28.
- **Stage** (`bsim dialogue`, after syntax, 4 s): quotations (words: each OSHB word in its innermost clause; extent: until the text is shallower or the next quotation at its level starts), per-book pairs (the divine names one party, `divine`; self-address left out), and *exchanges*: runs of quotations the narrator introduces, at one level of one book, each starting at most `exchange_gap` = 4 clauses after the previous one ends, between exactly two known parties who both speak; an unknown or third speaker, or a turn addressed to someone other than the other party, ends a run. Exchanges are defined by the speakers, so the *turn* rule (which assumes alternation) does not make them. Rank: God 2, a king 1 (`kings`: מלך, Aramaic מלך, פרעה, נבוכדנצר, אחשורוש), anyone else 0.
- **Checks fixed in advance** (`dialogue.checks`, one-sided at α = 0.05; quotations not spoken by God; "up" = addressed to a higher rank than the speaker's; null: the "up" label permuted over the quotations of each book, 999 times):
  - *Deference* (a known answer: deferential self-reference to a superior, Revell 1996): "your servant" (עבד, אמה, שפחה with a second-person suffix) per 1,000 words. **13.1 up against 1.5** (99 in 7,548 words against 108 in 70,124), difference 11.6 against 1.1 shuffled, **p 0.001 — holds**. Solomon's temple prayer carries 23 of the 99 (1 Kgs 8:23, 2 Chr 6:14); without those two chapters, 11.7 against 1.5 (p 0.001), described, not a new claim.
  - *נא after an imperative* (contested: a politeness particle, Shulman 1996, Christiansen 2009, or a logical one, Lambdin 1971): **14 % of the commands to God or a king (26 / 185) against 4 % of the rest (86 / 2,008), p 0.003 — holds**: Gen 32:10, Exod 33:12, Num 11:11, 12:13, 14:13 (Moses to God), Judg 16:28, Gen 50:4 (Joseph to Pharaoh's house).
  - *The last word* (an open question): among exchanges between parties of different rank, does the higher one speak last more often than its share of the turns gives (the last turn drawn at random within each exchange, 999 times)? **96 of 170 against 89.4 expected, p 0.17 — not borne out.**
  - Sensitivity, every addressee: deference 10.4 more per 1,000 words (p 0.001), נא 8 points more (p 0.001).
  - Described without a verdict: the higher party opens 84 of 170 exchanges (89.4 expected, p 0.82); its turns are longer in 75 and shorter in 90 (median 11.7 against 11.0 words a turn, sign test p 0.28). God does not, by this count, out-talk or outlast the people he speaks with.
  - **Changed after the first run, stated:** the exchange definition. The first run counted God's names as separate parties (Ezekiel's oracles came out as "יהוה / אדני" exchanges), took turns at every level and did not end a run at a turn addressed to someone else (Exodus 12: God's words to Moses, then Moses' to the elders). It gave 595 exchanges, 250 unequal, the last word 142 against 130.5 (p 0.075, also not borne out). The verdict did not change; the two other checks do not use exchanges.
- **Served:** `clauses.addressee`, `addressee_source`; `dialogue_pairs`, `dialogue_exchanges` (+ `meta.dialogue`), empty without the stage; `/dialogue` (counts, hand check, checks, sensitivity, opener, lengths, books), `/dialogue/book/{id}` (pairs with each party's Hebrew form and rank), `/dialogue/exchanges?book&party&unequal` (paged, CSV `exchanges`); `addressee` on `/syntax/{unit}` clauses; `to` on a book's speakers (`/speech/book/{id}`) and on the reader's speakers; a `dialogue` dossier entry (exchanges touching the unit); findings `dialogue_deference`, `dialogue_particle`, `dialogue_last_word`. Viewer: Who speaks → To whom (the counts, the checks, a book's conversations drawn as a network with its pairs table, the exchanges linking to the reader with speakers on); "to X" on the clause panel, on a book's speakers and in the reader's speaker legend.
- **Limits:** the addressee of poetry and prophecy is mostly not found (no introduction); one addressee per quotation (the first of אל משה ואל אהרן); a party is a lemma, so David and "the king" are two parties, and בן אדם in Ezekiel shows as אדם; kings are the title and a few royal names, not every named king; Hebrew of the Second Temple period and the Aramaic of Daniel are in the same counts.

### 16.45 Graded numerical sayings and lists (`bsim forms`, `analysis/forms.py`, `/forms`)
- **Question:** two small forms no other analysis sees. The *graded numerical saying* names a number and then the next (על שלשה פשעי דמשק ועל ארבעה, Amos 1:3; שש הנה שנא יהוה ושבע, Prov 6:16; Roth 1962, Haran 1972). A *list* repeats one verse shape: genealogies, itineraries, rolls of names. Can both be found from the text alone, and do the sayings behave as scholarship says?
- **Numbers.** A word is a number when one of its morphemes has a lemma in `forms.numbers` (the cardinals 1–10, the tens, מאה, אלף, רבבה / ריבו, Hebrew and Aramaic; not a name; ordinals and dual nouns such as פעמים, יומים are not numbers here). Number words side by side are one compound number (שלשים ושנים, שלש מאות, שלש עשרה, the teen marker עשר having no value) and are left out, unless they are just two words a step apart (שנים שלשה, אלף ורבבה); the rest are *free*. 6,307 number words, 3,240 free.
- **Graded pairs.** Two free numbers of one verse, the second at most `window` = 8 words after the first, whose values make a step (`steps`: x / x + 1 up to 9 / 10, the tens, 1000 / 10000). 105 pairs in 91 verses (the *sayings*): 1 / 2 57, 3 / 4 14, 2 / 3 7, 1000 / 10000 6, 6 / 7 5, 5 / 6 4, 10 / 20 4, the rest 1–2 each.
- **Checks fixed in advance** (`forms.checks`, one-sided at α = 0.05):
  - *Known* (a known answer): 19 sayings named in advance (Roth's list, cardinals on both sides: Amos 1–2 as one, Prov 30:15, 18, 21, 29, Prov 6:16, Job 5:19, 33:14, 40:5, Mic 5:4, 6:7, Eccl 11:2, Ps 62:12, 91:7, Deut 32:30, Jer 3:14, Isa 17:6, Amos 4:8, 1 Sam 18:7 with 21:12 and 29:5), each found when half its verses hold a graded pair. **18 of 19 — holds.** The miss is Deut 32:30, אחד אלף ושנים יניסו רבבה: "one … a thousand, and two" stand side by side and read as one compound number.
  - *Chance*: graded pairs against the values of the free numbers permuted within each book (999 times). **105 against 96.2, p 0.196 — does not hold.** Most pairs are not sayings: שני אדנים תחת הקרש האחד and its like, in the measurements of the Tabernacle (Exod 26, 36), the offerings (Num 7, 28–29) and the Temple (1 Kgs 6–7, Ezek 41), are as common as chance makes them and outnumber the sayings. Described, added after the first run (`sensitivity_drop`): without 1 / 2, 48 against 37.0 (p 0.037).
  - *Parallelism* (Roth: the pattern is a device of parallelism): a verse with a graded pair against the other verses holding two free numbers within the window, by the probability of parallel halves (§16.9; verses the stage could not split left out), the labels permuted (999 times). **12.1 % of the 91 sayings are parallel lines (probability ≥ 0.5) against 5.1 % of 511 other verses (mean probability 0.19 against 0.15), p 0.026 — holds.**
- **Lists.** Each verse is the counts of its word-shape n-grams (the `structural` tokens of §16.5, 1..3), compared by cosine with the verses up to `lags` = 3 before and after it in its book; its best match is its *likeness*. A verse is list-like above the 0.99 quantile of the likenesses with the verse order shuffled within books (20 shuffles; 0.739); a list is a run of at least 5 list-like verses with at most one other verse between consecutive ones. **69 lists over 863 verses against 0.7 by chance**: most in 1 Chronicles (21 lists, 270 verses), Numbers (13, 172), Nehemiah (6, 113), Ezra (5, 88), Genesis (7, 80), Joshua (5, 56); and Eccl 3:2–7 (עת ל…). Kind, described: the first of `kinds` whose lemma is in half its verses (genealogy ילד, itinerary נסע / חנה, census פקד, offerings קרבן, kings מלך), else *names* when proper nouns are 30 % of its words: names 36, genealogy 5, itinerary 2, kings 2, other 24.
  - *Lists named in advance* (fixed in advance; found when half their verses are in a list, among more lists than chance): Gen 5:6–31, 11:12–25, 36:32–39, Num 1:20–43, 7:12–83, 33:5–49, Josh 12:9–24, 1 Chr 24:7–18, Ezra 2:3–35, Neh 3:2–32. **8 of 10, 69 lists against 0.7 — holds.** Missed: Num 7 (each chieftain's offering repeats every six verses, beyond `lags`) and Neh 3 (10 of 31 verses: the builders' verses differ in length).
- **Changed after the first run, stated.** (1) A NaN bug: verses without a parallelism probability made the comparison's mean NaN and its p a spurious 0.001; they are now left out (not a choice). (2) The *names* kind: 61 of 69 lists were "other". (3) The sensitivity without 1 / 2 (described only). (4) The lists draw on their own random stream, so that adding (3) did not move them (it had: 69 → 68 lists); with it, 69 lists against 0.7 (first run 69 against 1.8), Gen 5 covered 20 of 26 verses (first run 25). No verdict changed.
- **Served:** `forms_sayings`, `forms_pairs`, `forms_lists`, `forms_books` (+ `meta.forms`), empty without the stage; `/forms` (counts, the checks, the sayings and lists named in advance, per book), `/forms/sayings?book&unit&known` (the verses with both numbers marked), `/forms/lists?book&unit&kind` (with their first `serve.list_verses` verses), CSV `sayings` and `lists`; `list_verses` on every `/map/{unit_type}` point and `lists` on the response; a `forms` dossier entry; findings `forms_known`, `forms_chance`, `forms_parallel`, `forms_lists`. Viewer: Patterns → Numbers and lists (two views) and the Map coloured by the share of each unit's verses in a list.
- **Limits:** cardinals only, so ordinal or dual pairs (Hos 6:2, Job 33:29) are not sought; a saying across two verses is not seen; the step must be exact (Gen 4:24's 7 / 77 is not one); lists repeating at a longer period (Num 7) or with uneven verses (Neh 3) are missed; a list is found by shape, so a run of similar narrative verses can count (2 Kgs 2:2–6, Elijah and Elisha's journey).

### 16.46 Pattern search (`api/patterns.py`, `/patterns`, `/query`)
- **Question:** the analyses find patterns the project chose; a researcher often has a construction of their own in mind ("an infinitive absolute before a finite verb of the same root", "a command followed by a name called"), which until now needed SHEBANQ or Text-Fabric. A small query language over every word answers it in the viewer.
- **Language.** A query is a row of word patterns in square brackets, matched in order inside one verse. A pattern holds conditions separated by spaces, all of which must hold (`[]` is any word); a condition is `key=value` or `key!=value`, `a|b` any of the values. Between patterns nothing means the next word, `..N` at most N words on, `..` anywhere later in the verse. A variable `@name` as the value of `lemma`, `root` or `word` repeats what the first pattern using it matched; `!=@name` is any other. `[form=infabs root=@r] [root=@r form=finite]` is מות יומת; `[form=imperative] ..4 [function=Voct pos=name]` a command and then a name called out.
- **Keys.** From the OSHB lemma and morphology: `lemma` (Strong's number, or a Hebrew form: the lemma's commonest consonantal form, or a root's headword), `root` (the families of §16.38), `word` (the consonants, prefixes included, final letters folded), `pos`, `type`, `stem` (Hebrew and Aramaic names), `form` (perfect … infcon, with the groups finite, infinitive, participles), `person`, `gender`, `number`, `state`, `suffix` (yes / no / a person-gender-number), `prefix` (ו ה ב כ ל מ ש / none), `lang`. From the BHSA (§16.26): `function` (the phrase's), `clause` (its type), `speech` (inside a quotation) and `speaker`. A word is described by its main morpheme: the first that is neither a prefix nor a suffix; a word that is only a preposition with a suffix (לו) keeps the preposition.
- **Engine.** The word table (305,517 words, integer codes per key, the BHSA phrase functions and clause types as bit sets) is read from the results DB once per server, in a background thread at start (`serve.patterns.preload`; about 6 s). Each pattern becomes a boolean mask; matches are extended one word offset at a time inside the verse, the variables compared as codes. A query is refused (`too_broad`) when any step holds more than `serve.patterns.max_matches` partial matches; the matches of the last `serve.patterns.cache` queries are kept. A typical query takes 10 ms.
- **Errors** are part of the answer, not an HTTP error: `error` = `{code, at, token}` (`empty`, `expected_pattern`, `unclosed`, `bad_condition`, `unknown_key`, `bad_value`, `unknown_value`, `bad_gap`, `too_many`, `variable`, `too_broad`), `at` the character offset; the viewer words each code in the interface language and marks the place.
- **Check against a known count.** `[form=infabs lemma=@l lang=hebrew] ..3 [lemma=@l form=finite]` finds 432 pairs and the reverse order 41: together 473, the infinitive absolutes with a finite verb of the same lemma that `bsim roots` counts by its own code (§16.38).
- **Served:** `/patterns?q&book&unit` (verses with a match, paged; each match's words and display tokens; the matches per book), CSV `patterns`. Viewer: Patterns → Pattern search (`/query`): the query box, examples, the syntax guide with every key, the counts per book as filters, the verses with the matched words marked.
- **Limits:** one verse at a time (no pattern across a verse break, no clause or phrase as a unit of its own); no alternation between whole patterns or optional words; ketiv / qere follow OSHB's words; Hebrew lemma forms are the commonest spelling, so Strong's numbers are the sure way.

### 16.47 Your notebook (`store/notebook.py`, `/notebook`)
- **Question:** the pair labels (§16.25) record judgements of proposed pairs only. Reading with the viewer, one also wants to keep a verse or a passage, write a note on it and gather passages under a theme of one's own.
- **Model.** An *entry* is a unit kept (a verse, chapter, pericope or parasha, by its unit id) with a note, possibly empty; a *collection* is a named group of entries with an optional description. Adding a unit to a collection keeps it as an entry; removing an entry takes it out of every collection; deleting a collection keeps its entries. Tables `notebook_entries`, `notebook_collections` (names unique), `notebook_members`, in the labels file (`paths.labels`): written by the API, apart from the results DB that `bsim build-db` rebuilds, so a new build keeps them. Entries are listed in canon order (a chapter before its first verse); an entry whose unit a later build lacks stays in the file and is counted (`hidden`), not shown.
- **API:** `GET /notebook` (every entry with its unit and, for a verse, its text; every collection with its count; `no-store`), `PUT` / `DELETE /notebook/entries/{unit_id}` (keep or change the note, `note: null` keeping it; remove), `POST /notebook/collections`, `PUT` / `DELETE /notebook/collections/{id}` (409 on a name taken), `PUT` / `DELETE /notebook/collections/{id}/entries/{unit_id}`. Limits in `serve.notebook` (note 20,000 characters, name 80). Writes follow `serve.labels_writable` (403 when false), like the labels; CORS allows POST for them.
- **Viewer:** a Notebook panel on every unit page (keep, the note with save and unsaved state, the collections as chips, add to one or create one in place, remove with a confirmation in the page rather than a browser dialog); the Notebook page (`/notebook`): collections as filters with create, rename, describe and delete, a search over notes and references, each entry with its text and editor; export of what is shown as Markdown (a section per entry, a verse's text quoted, the note, its collections) or CSV, written in the browser so the file's wording comes from the interface catalogs.
- **Limits:** one notebook per server (no accounts); plain-text notes; collections are not ordered by hand (canon order); no word-level or pair-level entries (pairs are labelled, §16.25).

### 16.48 Newer encoders (`bsim encoder-exp`, `eval/newer_encoders.py`)
- **Question:** the semantic model is BEREL 3.0, a BERT pretrained on rabbinic Hebrew (2023), fine-tuned on the cross-references (§7.2, §16.41). Newer models exist: DictaBERT (Dicta, modern Hebrew; base and large, CC BY 4.0) and multilingual sentence embedders trained on hundreds of languages (multilingual-E5-large, MIT; Qwen3-Embedding-0.6B, Apache-2.0). Does any of them retrieve the cross-references better on dev? Availability checked first (2026-10-14): all four on the Hugging Face hub, ungated, fast tokenizers, every verse within 128 tokens and no unknown token. NeoDictaBERT (2025) was left out: it needs code run from the model repository (`trust_remote_code`).
- **Candidates** (`encoder_experiments`): *trained* — DictaBERT base and large through `train-sup` with the recipe, pairs (Sefaria + OpenBible + ETCBC, 38,586 rows) and seed of the model in use, from the base model with mean pooling, into `models/{name}-sup` (large with `mini_batch_size` 16, which bounds memory only); the checkpoint is chosen by Sefaria dev recall@10, as for BEREL. *Zero-shot* — the multilingual embedders as they are, each verse with the model's own instruction before it (E5's `query: `; for Qwen3 "Instruct: Given a verse of the Hebrew Bible, retrieve other verses related to it / Query:"); since verse similarity is symmetric, every verse gets the same one. fp32 throughout.
- **Scoring:** as §16.41, in memory: the semantic list (CSLS top-50) and its fusion with `bm25_lemma`, on the Sefaria (884), OpenBible (2,541) and ETCBC (179) dev queries, nDCG@10, gains paired over queries with a 10,000-draw bootstrap. The zero-shot candidates are also compared with BGE-M3, the zero-shot system already built, on the semantic list.
- **Rule, fixed before any candidate ran** (`eval/newer_encoders.py:adoption`): a candidate replaces BEREL only if its fused list gains on Sefaria with a CI above 0 and by more than 0.0009 — the largest fused gain, either way, of supervision-exp's reseeded retrain (`seed14`) — and loses nothing significant on OpenBible or ETCBC (CIs reaching 0).
- **Results** (2026-10-14) — fused nDCG@10 gain over `berel_sup` (fused 0.1946 / 0.1516 / 0.8325), 95 % CI; semantic list alone in brackets:

  | candidate | kind | Sefaria | OpenBible | ETCBC | passes |
  |---|---|---|---|---|---|
  | `dictabert` (DictaBERT base) | fine-tuned, best epoch 3.9, dev recall@10 0.184, 3.83 GB | −0.0164 [−0.0264, −0.0068] (−0.024) | −0.0031 [−0.0084, +0.0022] (−0.006) | +0.0275 [+0.0024, +0.0532] (+0.061) | no |
  | `dictabert_large` (DictaBERT large) | fine-tuned, best epoch 0.7, dev recall@10 0.175, 7.85 GB | −0.0213 [−0.0320, −0.0110] (−0.027) | −0.0085 [−0.0139, −0.0032] (−0.009) | +0.0160 [−0.0125, +0.0440] (+0.031) | no |
  | `e5_large` (multilingual-E5-large) | zero-shot, `query: ` | −0.0271 [−0.0395, −0.0151] (−0.029) | −0.0209 [−0.0270, −0.0148] (−0.027) | +0.0376 [+0.0105, +0.0659] (+0.080) | no |
  | `qwen3_06b` (Qwen3-Embedding-0.6B) | zero-shot, an instruction | −0.0476 [−0.0606, −0.0351] (−0.067) | −0.0352 [−0.0422, −0.0285] (−0.052) | +0.0068 [−0.0224, +0.0360] (−0.002) | no |

  Zero-shot against BGE-M3 (semantic list; BGE-M3 0.135 / 0.090 / 0.852):

  | candidate | Sefaria | OpenBible | ETCBC |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | `e5_large` | −0.0083 [−0.0195, +0.0027] | +0.0052 [+0.0002, +0.0102] | −0.0064 [−0.0267, +0.0118] |
  | `qwen3_06b` | −0.0458 [−0.0602, −0.0321] | −0.0199 [−0.0267, −0.0131] | −0.0890 [−0.1227, −0.0578] |

- **Verdict:** no candidate passes, so `berel_sup` stays the semantic model and nothing in the pipeline changes. Every candidate loses on Sefaria, the gold the encoder is chosen by (CIs below 0), and scores higher on ETCBC's near-verbatim parallels (significantly for DictaBERT base and multilingual-E5); a reading, not tested here: BEREL's pretraining on rabbinic Hebrew, which quotes and echoes the Bible, suits cross-references better than DictaBERT's modern Hebrew or a multilingual model's breadth. Zero-shot, multilingual-E5-large is about level with BGE-M3 (not worth a second zero-shot system) and Qwen3-Embedding-0.6B below it. The candidates' models and negatives stay on disk under their names; `bsim encoder-exp --only NAME` reruns one.
- **Viewer:** the Evaluation page's *Newer encoders* section (fused and semantic tables, the rule, the zero-shot comparison); `/eval` → `encoders`.

### 16.49 Comparing two builds (`bsim compare-builds`, `store/compare.py`)
- **Question:** after a change, what did it change in what the viewer serves? M54 answered this by comparing the stage outputs by hand, and found two nondeterministic stages that way (§16.35). `bsim compare-builds OLD NEW` does it for two results DBs.
- **Method.** Both files are attached read-only to one in-memory SQLite connection, so every count is a query and no table is loaded into Python (85 tables and 8.3 M matches in about 75 s). Per table in both: the row counts, columns added or removed, and then
  - *with a primary key*: the rows joined on it, so rows only in OLD, only in NEW, and rows whose other columns differ, counted exactly and beyond `compare_builds.float_tol` (a relative tolerance on REAL columns, absolute below 1, so a sum taken in another order is not a change), with a few example keys and the columns that differ;
  - *without one*: whole rows as a multiset (duplicates counted), and whether the same rows come in another order.
  `meta` is compared key by key, as flattened JSON paths; keys in `volatile_meta` (the build time, the `/similar` timings) are reported apart. Tables only in one build are listed.
- **Output:** a Markdown report on stdout (a row per table that differs, the `meta` paths that changed, "Identical." or "Different."), `--json PATH` for the whole comparison, `--fail-on-diff` to exit 1 when the builds differ (for a script or CI). Run by hand, not a pipeline stage.
- **First use** (2026-10-14, M71): the build before M71 against the one after (phrases, eval-etcbc and build-db rerun after `surface_bm25.json` was rewritten): all 85 tables the same, `meta` the same apart from the build time and the timings, which is why `similar_benchmark` joined `volatile_meta`. On a copy with one discovery deleted, one match score moved by 1e-12 of itself and another by 0.1, and one `meta.syntax` count changed, the report lists exactly those: 1 row only in OLD, 2 changed (1 beyond the tolerance), and the `syntax` path.
- **Limits:** indexes, views and triggers are not compared, nor the order of rows in a keyed table (the key orders them); an unkeyed table costs a sort of both copies.

### 16.50 A third fusion list for chapters and pericopes (`bsim unit-exp`, `eval/unit_experiments.py`, `retrieve/unit_pairs.py`)
- **Question:** passages are retrieved by fusing a unit TF-IDF list with the semantic BMA list (§6.2), and that is the weakest level (test nDCG@10: chapter 0.157, pericope 0.068). At chapter level the fusion did not even beat TF-IDF alone on dev (§5.3). The verse-level analyses already know which verses of two passages belong together; does a list built from them help?
- **Candidates** (`unit_experiments`), each a unit list aggregated from weighted verse pairs: a unit pair scores the sum of the weights of the verse pairs with one verse in each unit, either way round (pairs inside one unit ignored), and each unit keeps its best 50.
  - *sequences*: the aligned pairs of the parallel sequences (§16.7) with their weights, from every chain or from the chains with q ≤ 0.2;
  - *phrases*: the verse pairs sharing a phrase (§16.1), weight the alignment score;
  - *hits*: each verse's fused top 10 or top 50, weight (N + 1 − rank) / N, neighbours dropped.

  Each enters as a third RRF list beside the unit lexical and semantic lists at w 0.25 / 0.5 / 1. None of them reads a gold: phrases come from the lexical lists, sequences and hits from the fused verse lists.
- **Protocol:** as retrieval-exp (§16.21), per unit type, on three dev golds: Sefaria's unit gold (§8.3) and the same rule applied to OpenBible's and ETCBC's dev verse pairs (unit pairs sharing ≥ 2). *Selected* = the member best on Sefaria; *cross-fitted* = chosen on one half of the queries, scored on the other (2 folds); the selected member's gains on OpenBible and ETCBC, which no choice looks at; paired bootstrap, 10,000 draws. The baseline is the two-list fusion, rebuilt in memory.
- **Rule, fixed before the first run** (`eval/unit_experiments.py:adoption`): a family passes for a unit type when its cross-fitted Sefaria gain has a CI above 0 and the selected member loses nothing significant on OpenBible or ETCBC (CIs reaching 0); of those passing, the largest cross-fitted gain is adopted.
- **Results** (2026-10-14), nDCG@10 gain over the two-list fusion (chapter 0.1710 / 0.1483 / 0.6959, pericope 0.1178 / 0.1603 / 0.7851; Sefaria / OpenBible / ETCBC, 154 / 296 / 18 and 348 / 416 / 16 queries), 95 % CI:

  | unit | family | selected | cross-fitted (Sefaria) | OpenBible | ETCBC | passes |
  |---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
  | chapter | sequences | all chains, w 0.5 | +0.0085 [−0.0045, +0.0225] | +0.0072 [+0.0008, +0.0145] | −0.0376 [−0.1308, +0.0524] | no |
  | chapter | **phrases** | w 1 | **+0.0266 [+0.0066, +0.0461]** | +0.0123 [+0.0027, +0.0224] | +0.0677 [+0.0145, +0.1378] | **yes** |
  | chapter | hits | top 10, w 1 | +0.0087 [−0.0014, +0.0186] | +0.0081 [+0.0026, +0.0139] | +0.0064 [−0.0086, +0.0248] | no |
  | pericope | sequences | q ≤ 0.2, w 0.25 | +0.0011 [−0.0002, +0.0034] | −0.0004 [−0.0011, +0.0000] | 0 | no |
  | pericope | phrases | w 0.5 | +0.0054 [−0.0043, +0.0154] | +0.0046 [−0.0045, +0.0137] | −0.0024 [−0.0610, +0.0457] | no |
  | pericope | hits | top 50, w 1 | +0.0095 [−0.0002, +0.0190] | +0.0165 [+0.0090, +0.0243] | −0.0418 [−0.1153, +0.0087] | no |

  Alone, the chapter phrase list (906 of 929 chapters have one) scores 0.188 on Sefaria and the fused verse hits 0.187, both above the two-list fusion (0.171) and close to TF-IDF (0.192). The best weight is the grid's largest (w 1) at chapter level; the grid was not extended after seeing that. Sequences help little: most chains are chance (median q 0.61), and the strong ones cover few chapters.
- **Adopted:** phrases for chapters, `fusion.unit_phrases: {chapter: 1.0}`. `bsim fuse` writes the chapter phrase list as `topk/chapter/phrases.parquet` and fuses the three lists; the `phrases` stage now runs before `fuse` (it reads the lexical verse lists only; its old dependency on `fuse` was not needed). Chapter dev nDCG@10, fused: 0.1710 → **0.1976**, now above TF-IDF (0.1916). Pericopes and parashot keep two lists. Rerun after adoption, the experiment gives the same numbers. The test split is not rerun (chapter 0.157 stays the recorded figure).
- **Effects** (`bsim compare-builds` before / after): only the chapter fused matches changed (38,652 rows; 79 % of each chapter's top 10 kept; 11,887 rows carry a phrase rank), with what is built on them: chapter discoveries, the chapter network and its communities, and the echoes (§16.36) over it (2,454 → 2,638 directed chapter pairs, 366 → 405 dated by language; the echoes finding stays 21 / 21 and holds). All 32 findings keep their verdict.
- **Served:** `matches.phr_score`, `phr_rank`; `/similar` → `phrases` (the fused list has the third list) and each hit's `phr_*`; the viewer's score breakdown gets a third bar (*Phr*) on chapter pages; `/eval` → `units`, the Evaluation page's *A third list for chapters and pericopes* section.

### 16.51 Code health: modules by feature, generated viewer types, a panel registry
- **Question:** three files had grown past 2,000 lines (`api/models.py` 2,425, `api/queries.py` 2,145, `store/db.py` 2,031), the viewer's `api/types.ts` (3,130) restated every response model by hand, and the unit page (557) held the hooks and markup of every panel. Can they be cut down without changing what is served?
- **API models and queries:** `api/models/` and `api/queries/` are packages with one module per route module (`api/routes/`) and `common` for what several routes share. Each name went to the route module that uses it, or to `common` when two features need it, so `common` never imports a feature module. Routes import models from their own module; `queries` re-exports every function, since routes call it as `queries.fn`. One query was renamed (`domains` → `domain_tree`: a package attribute cannot be both a function and a submodule). Check: the OpenAPI schema came out byte for byte the same.
- **Results DB build:** `store/db.py` (526 lines) keeps the core tables, `matches`, `discoveries`, the meta and `similar()`. Each analysis stage's contract is one `Source` in `store/analyses.py`: its folder, each Parquet file and the table it fills, whether it is required, a small `fix` (integer flags, a book column, an id, columns older artifacts lack) and the meta keys copied. This replaced the code that read, adapted and inserted each analysis one by one. The 85 tables' column lists (590 lines) were the same as `schema.sql`'s, column for column; they are now read from it (`store/schema.py`). Lemma helpers moved to `store/lexicon.py` (the analysis modules import them at the top now, not inside functions to avoid a cycle), gold links and discoveries to `store/links.py`. Check: `bsim build-db` with the new code against the DB built before: all 85 tables and the meta the same (`bsim compare-builds`).
- **Viewer types:** the response models' plain `str` fields that the viewer narrows (unit types, `/similar` filters, sort keys, rewrite operations, sequence directions, list kinds, mood, sides, acrostic order) are now `Literal`s in pydantic, so the schema carries them. A type of `types.ts` whose schema type matches it (both assignable either way) is an alias, `export type X = S['X']`: 214 of them. The 19 responses with a JSON field the schema leaves open (eval results, `meta` checks, `synoptic`, `typing`, `leitwort_numbers`, `runtime`) are the schema type with those fields typed here (`Omit<S['X'], …> & {…}`); the drift check (D55) now covers only those. `types.ts`: 3,130 → 1,263 lines.
- **Unit page:** its seven data panels (names, network, themes, dating; shared phrases, wordplay, parallel sequences) are components in `components/UnitPanels.tsx`, each listed with the unit types it is for, its place (`context`: under the text; `after`: under the similar units) and the dossier entry that gates its fetch. The structure, syntax and word panels stay in the page, since they share state with the text. 557 → 445 lines.
- **Checked:** ruff, pytest, oxlint + `tsc`, vitest, the fixture and real-data e2e; `bsim all` reran 25 stages (the 17 whose code changed, import paths only, and 8 after them). The rebuilt DB matched except in `senses` and `word_pairs`: a second run of `bsim senses` with no code change gives another `senses.parquet` (an example of lemma 1696), so that stage was still not deterministic. The cause was k-means threads, not the GPU (fixed in §16.52). `word_pairs` holds the same rows in another order, as in M54.

### 16.52 One answer per seed: k-means on one thread (`stats.kmeans`)
- **Question:** two runs of `bsim senses` on unchanged code wrote different `senses.parquet` files (§16.51; M54 saw the same). Where does the difference come from?
- **Not the GPU:** §16.51 and M54 put it down to the CUDA encoding of the contexts. That guess was wrong. The 299,551 word vectors came out bitwise the same in two encodings in one process, and again in two separate processes.
- **The cause:** sklearn's `KMeans` gives another answer on the same vectors with the same `random_state`. Its OpenMP threads add their partial centre sums in the order they finish, so the centres differ in the last bits from fit to fit. Mostly that changes nothing, or only which uses lie nearest a centre: the examples of דָּבַר (1696). For a large lemma with two optima of near-equal silhouette it changes the clustering: כֹּל (3605) came out at silhouette 0.11445 or 0.11388 from call to call.
- **Fix:** `stats.kmeans` runs the fit on one thread (`threadpoolctl`); the senses and map clusters use it. sklearn is imported before the limit is set, since the limit only reaches an OpenMP runtime already loaded. Before that, the first call of each process still varied.
- **Checked:**
  - Each lemma's labels, centres and examples are now the same in every call and every process.
  - Two runs of `bsim senses`, and of `bsim map`, write byte-identical files. Senses takes 166.6 s, against 170.7 s before; map 23.7 s, against 21.0 s.
  - A test fits 6,000 random points four times. Plain `KMeans` gave 5 different results in 5 fits; `stats.kmeans` gives 1.
- **What changed in the results:** against the last multi-threaded run, the use clusters of three lemmas, בֵּן 1121a, כֹּל 3605 and מֶלֶךְ 4428, and one example list of דָּבַר 1696. The counts in the meta are unchanged: 539 of 611 lemmas with uses that differ by group, dictionary senses for 296 of 384, NMI 0.2042. The map is unchanged; its clusters were stable before and are now guaranteed.

### 16.53 Gematria: letter values (`bsim gematria`, `text/gematria.py`, `analysis/gematria.py`, `/gematria`)
- **Question:** a reader of gematria asks what a word or a verse adds up to and what else has the same value (אליעזר = 318, נחש = משיח = 358). This is a lookup that later tests build on (G2: do equal values link related words?); it claims nothing itself.
- **Systems** (`text/gematria.py`, summed over `consonantal` letters, so points, te'amim and maqaf drop out):
  - `standard` (mispar hechrechi): א 1 … ת 400, a final form as its letter
  - `gadol` (mispar gadol): the final forms ך ם ן ף ץ at 500 … 900
  - `ordinal` (mispar siduri): 1 … 22
  - `katan` (mispar katan): the standard value without its zeros, 1 … 9 a letter

  These are definitions, not tunables, so they are in code; the config holds the equivalences checked.
- **What gets a value:**
  - **Words:** the MAM display tokens, the text as shown with the qere read. A form is a written word with its prefixes (והארץ is not הארץ).
  - **Verses:** the sum of their words, with letters and words counted.
  - **Lemmas:** valued by their Strong's headword (HebrewStrong.xml, read as `bsim roots` reads it), else by their commonest form (`lemma_gloss.he_lemma`).
- **Checked:** the equivalences of `gematria.known` (eight, among them אליעזר 318, נחש / משיח / יבא שילה 358, יין / סוד 70, אחד / אהבה 13) must come out at their value, or the stage stops. The tests also pin the tables and Genesis 1:1 = 2701 in 7 words and 28 letters.
- **The corpus:** 39,555 written forms (305,452 words), 9,204 lemmas (all by Strong's headword), 23,206 verses, in 16 s. Seven verses add up to 2701, as Genesis 1:1 does (Gen 8:14, Lev 20:27, 2 Sam 1:18, 1 Kgs 8:15, Mic 6:9, Nah 2:11). That is a reminder for G2 of how common such coincidences are; it is not a test.
- **Served:**
  - DB: `gematria_forms`, `gematria_lemmas`, `gematria_verses` (+ `meta.gematria`), indexed by each system's value, empty without the stage.
  - API: `/gematria/value?text=` (any Hebrew text, computed on request); `/gematria?value&system` (counts, the equivalences, `computed`); `/gematria/forms`, `/gematria/lemmas`, `/gematria/verses?book` (paged); `gematria` (each display token's standard value) on every `/reader/{unit}` verse; a `gematria` dossier entry (D100): the tradition's texts of `gematria.known` among the unit's words and, for a verse, its value and how many other verses share it.
  - Viewer: Patterns → Gematria (a word or a number, its four values, its forms, lemmas and verses, the equivalences); the word panel's value chip; a Gematria layer in the reader (each word's value, each verse's total linked to the verses of that value); `= 318` in the jump box (Ctrl+K).
- **Limits:** one text, the MAM with the qere; the ketiv's values (G4) and other systems (atbash is a cipher, G3) are not here. Lemma values follow Strong's headword spelling (full or defective as Strong's writes it).

### 16.54 Atbash and albam: names hidden by a letter swap (`bsim ciphers`, `text/ciphers.py`, `analysis/ciphers.py`, `/ciphers`)
- **Question:** Jeremiah writes Babel as ששך (25:26, 51:41) and the Chaldeans (כשדים) as לב קמי (51:1). That is atbash, the alphabet folded on itself (א↔ת, ב↔ש …). Can a scan find these cases, does atbash stand out there against chance, and does it, or albam (א↔ל, ב↔מ …), hide names anywhere else?
- **Swaps** (`text/ciphers.py`): both pair the 22 letters, so a word reads back by the same swap. Final forms are read as their letter, and the last letter of each word is written final.
- **Where a cipher may hide** (the MAM display tokens with the qere read; letters only, finals folded; at least `min_letters` = 3):
  - one word whose written form occurs at most `rare_form` = 2 times, since a cipher makes an odd word (ששך is written twice);
  - or two adjacent words read as one (לב קמי).

  That gives 268,699 places (167,785 distinct).
- **A hit** is a place that a swap turns into a name of the text: the name part of an OSHB word tagged `Np`, with the divine names of `entities.skip_lemmas` left out. There are 2,120 names.
- **The null:** `shuffles` = 999 random pairings of the 22 letters (eleven swaps, as atbash and albam are), each scored the same way.
- **Checks fixed in advance** (`ciphers.checks`, one-sided at 0.05):
  - `known`: the three cases are found.
  - `jeremiah`: atbash reads more names in Jeremiah 25 and 51 than the random pairings do.
  - `elsewhere`: atbash reads more names in the rest of the Bible (expected not to hold).
  - `albam`: albam reads more names anywhere (expected not to hold).

  Per chapter, each swap's hits get p (the share of random pairings that do as well there) and BH q over the 929 chapters.
- **Results:**
  - **Known:** all 3 found.
  - **Jeremiah 25 and 51:** 3 names against 0.64 for a random pairing (p 0.036): holds. These are exactly the three named cases.
  - **Elsewhere:** atbash reads 78 names against 104.0 (p 0.76): does not hold. A random pairing reads about 105 names over the whole Bible, so outside Jeremiah atbash reads no more than chance gives (fewer was not tested), and its other hits (השמת → צביא) are what chance gives.
  - **Albam:** 94 against 104.6 (p 0.53): does not hold.
  - **Per chapter:** no chapter passes after correction (min q 1.0). Jeremiah 51 alone is atbash p 0.066 and Jeremiah 25 p 0.18; the claim was fixed on the two chapters together.
  - **Reading:** atbash is a device of these oracles against Babylon, not a code running through the text. The run took about 170 s and gives the same files every time (one seed).
- **Not tested:** Isa 7:6's טבאל, which Rashi reads by albam as רמלא (Remaliah). רמלא is not a name as the text writes it (רמליהו), so it is noted, not tested.
- **Served:**
  - DB: `cipher_hits`, `cipher_chapters` (+ `meta.ciphers`), empty without the stage.
  - API: `/ciphers` (counts, the checks, the cases named in advance, the chapters with a hit); `/ciphers/hits?cipher&book&unit&known` (paged, with the hiding words' display tokens); `/ciphers/value?text=` (any text in both swaps, with how often each result is written: `gematria_forms`); a `ciphers` dossier entry (D100): the hits in the unit's verses, the first one's words and name.
  - Findings: `ciphers_known`, `ciphers_jeremiah`, `ciphers_elsewhere`, `ciphers_albam`.
  - Viewer: Patterns → Atbash and albam (the checks, the cases named in advance, the hits with their words marked, the chapters against random pairings); the word panel's "Swapped" line (each word in both swaps, linked to a text search when the result is written in the Bible).
- **Limits:**
  - Only names are targets. A swap into an ordinary word (ששך → בבל is a name) is not sought.
  - Spans of three or more words are not read.
  - The rare-form rule leaves out a cipher hidden in a common word.

### 16.55 The Masorah's counts (`bsim masorah`, `analysis/masorah.py`, `/masorah`)
- **Question:** the first scribes "counted all the letters of the Torah" (Kiddushin 30a): the ו of גחון (Lev 11:42) is its middle letter, דרש דרש (Lev 10:16) its middle words, Lev 13:33 its middle verse. The Masorah's notes give Lev 8:8 as the middle verse, the middle verse of each book and each book's verse total. Counted on our text, where does each middle fall and why does a total differ? This checks our letters and our versification against the tradition; it is not a test against chance.
- **Sources** (in the config, each with its source code): Kiddushin 30a (`talmud`); the Masorah's summaries as C. D. Ginsburg collates them from the manuscripts (*Introduction to the Massoretico-Critical Edition*, 1897, ch. VI: the totals of most manuscripts, with the others in `also`; `masorah`); Tractate Soferim 9:3 (`soferim`, Lev 8:23); the Torah's 304,805 letters printed at the end of Deuteronomy in the Rabbinic Bible (`printed`).
- **Two texts**, from the MAM display tokens (consonants, final forms kept; a paseq is no word, a maqaf separates words):
  - `ketiv` (as written) and `qere` (as read). MAM shows the qere but keeps the written letters of many words (`mam-kq-trivial`, the perpetual הוא), so at each of OSHB's 1,260 ketiv / qere (§16.30) each text takes its own side wherever MAM shows the other: 1,036 words rewritten for the written text, 237 for the read one (one pair is not aligned to MAM).
  - A word written but not read goes after the word before it; one read but not written drops from the written text.
  - Where MAM spells a word unlike both of OSHB's sides (Aleppo against Leningrad: צבוים), the read text keeps MAM's spelling.
- **Middles** (`masorah.middles`): a span (a book, or a section) counted in letters, words or verses has its middle at (n − 1) / 2 from 0, between two units when n is even. The tradition's is the letter named (its place in the word), the words named (between two words: halfway) or the verse. Distance = theirs − ours, in units and in verses; a middle *lands* within `tolerance` = 1 unit.
- **Totals:** our verses for each book of the Masorah (`masorah.books`; Samuel, Kings, the Twelve, Ezra–Nehemiah and Chronicles one book each), the sections and the whole (`masorah.sections`, also the Torah's words and letters in each text). A book that differs carries a reason code the viewer explains: `split` (the Masorah counts a verse as two), `decalogue` (the Ten Commandments divided otherwise), `absent` (verses the Masorah's count leaves out), `untraced`, `witnesses` (the manuscripts disagree, and one gives our figure). A section shows the sum of the Masorah's book totals beside its own.
- **Results** (3.7 s):
  - **The Torah, counted:** 5,846 verses; as written 79,978 words and 304,801 letters (exactly the modern count of R. Yaakov Auerbach; the printed 304,805 is 4 more); as read 79,980 words and 304,830 letters. The Masorah gives 79,856 words.
  - **Middle letter:** ours, as written, is letter 152,401, the ל of ליהוה (Lev 8:28). The ו of גחון is 4,829 letters later, 94 verses on (as read, 4,824.5), so it does not land; the usual modern count finds the same distance.
  - **Middle words:** ours fall between two words of Lev 8:15 (79,978 written words). דרש דרש are 932 words later, 61 verses on (as read, 933), so they do not land.
  - **Middle verse:** 5,846 verses put the middle between Lev 8:8 and 8:9, so the Masorah's Lev 8:8 lands (−0.5); with its own 5,845 it would be the middle exactly. The Talmud's Lev 13:33 is 159.5 verses on, Soferim's Lev 8:23 14.5.
  - **Each Torah book's middle verse** (the Masorah) lands: Gen 27:40 (+1), Exod 22:27, Num 17:20, Deut 17:10 (+0.5 each), Lev 15:7 exactly.
  - **Psalms:** the Masorah's middle verse 78:36 is ours exactly. The Talmud's 78:38 is 2 verses on. The suspended ע of מיער (80:14) is 3,814.5 letters past the middle (119 verses on).
  - **Book totals:** 18 of the Masorah's 24 books agree. Each that differs:
    - Genesis 1,533 against 1,534: the Masorah counts 35:22 as two verses (the verse has two sets of accents).
    - Exodus 1,210 against 1,209 and Deuteronomy 956 against 955: MAM divides the Ten Commandments (Exod 20:2–14, Deut 5:6–18) into 13 verses, the Masorah's total into 12. Its weekly-portion counts (Yitro 72, Va'etchanan 119) divide them into 10.
    - Joshua 658 against 656: MAM has 21:36–37, which the Masorah's total leaves out. Manuscripts that have them still give 656.
    - Kings 1,536 against 1,534: the chapters give 1,536; Ginsburg could not trace the difference.
    - Jeremiah 1,364 against 1,365: the Babylonian Codex (916) gives 1,364.
  - **Sections:** Torah 5,846 against 5,845 (its books add to 5,845) and the Talmud's 5,888 (−42). Prophets 9,296 against 9,294 (its books add to 9,293). Writings 8,064, as given. The whole 23,206 against 23,203.
  - **Reading:** the tradition's middle letter and words are thousands of letters and hundreds of words from the middle of this text, as every modern count finds. The verse division is the Masorah's except for the Ten Commandments and four other verses.
- **Served:**
  - DB: `masorah_middles`, `masorah_totals`, `masorah_books` (+ `meta.masorah`), empty without the stage.
  - API: `/masorah` (the middles with both verses, the totals with their reasons and verses, the books).
  - Findings: `masorah_letter`, `masorah_words`, `masorah_verse` (the first Torah claim of each unit, in `masorah.text`, the written letters), `masorah_books` (every book agrees).
  - Viewer: Patterns → The Masorah's counts (the middles, as written or as read, each with the tradition's verse and ours marked; the verse totals with the reason a book differs; the sections; every book counted).
- **Limits:**
  - One text: MAM, with OSHB's Leningrad ketiv / qere. The scrolls' letter traditions (full and defective spellings, the large and small letters) are not modelled.
  - The middle verses of the books of the Prophets and Writings are not checked: the manuscripts mark either the last verse of the first half or the first of the second.
  - The totals come from one collation (Ginsburg). A book's figure varies between manuscripts; the other figures are listed.

### 16.56 The gazetteer: which place, where it lies (`bsim places`, `data/geo.py`, `analysis/places.py`, `/places`)
- **Why:** the names of §16.11 say person or place but not *which* place, nor where it lies. Geography (P2–P5) needs coordinates, and many names are several places: Abel is a stone in 1 Sam 6:18 and Abel-beth-maacah in 2 Sam 20:18, and Tekoa is a person and a town.
- **Sources** (`bsim download --only geo`, pinned commits, both CC BY 4.0):
  - **TIPNR** (STEPBible): 4,238 records (1,007 places, 3,129 persons, 102 others). Each has its names, each name with a disambiguated Strong's number, its Hebrew and every reference (English numbering; a psalm title's verse 0 is read as verse 1).
  - **OpenBible.info's geocoding** (`ancient.jsonl`): 1,342 ancient places, each with its proposed modern identifications voted 0 to 1,000 and resolved to a point.
- **Which place a mention is.** A mention is a name lemma in a verse (`entity_mentions`, 24,332). It goes to the TIPNR record of its Strong's number whose references come nearest:
  - the verse itself (`exact`, 5,786), or within `places.max_shift` = 3 verses where English and Hebrew numbering differ (`shifted`, 457);
  - failing both, a number with one place and no person takes it (`only`, 220).
  - Nearer a person: no place (16,886). Nothing near: no place (983). These are mostly the second word of a compound name: the גלעד of יבש גלעד, which TIPNR files under Jabesh alone.

  That gives 6,463 place mentions of 885 name lemmas. 92 Strong's numbers are several sites.
- **Where it lies.** A TIPNR place joins its OpenBible record by the OpenBible name TIPNR gives (OpenBible numbers a name's places: `Bethel 1`, `Bethel 2`) and by OpenBible's links to TIPNR (an older TIPNR: `Babylon@Gen.10.10` for `Babylon@Gen.10.10-Rev`). Among the candidates the one sharing most verses wins. Its point is the best-voted identification; `runner_up` is the second's votes as a share of the best (how contested the site is). Without a record, TIPNR's own map point (`coord_source = tipnr`).
  - 820 sites, 818 located, 802 through OpenBible.
  - Votes run low: 363 sites are voted below 300 of 1,000. For 138, a second identification has at least half the votes of the first.
- **Kinds.**
  - Every name occurrence carries SDBH's name subdomain (`places.sdbh`: Locations, Landforms, Waterbodies, Groups, People). A site is water or a landform when its mentions mostly say so; otherwise OpenBible's types (`places.ob_kinds`) decide town or region.
  - Result: 574 towns, 97 regions, 94 landforms, 41 water, 14 unknown.
  - The same subdomains give every name its commonest kind (`name_kinds`): 1,587 persons, 65 peoples, 714 locations, 58 landforms, 22 waters. Of the names §16.11 calls persons, 24 are mostly peoples in SDBH and 43 locations (tribes and lands named after a person).
- **Hand check** (`places.hand_check`): 50 place mentions drawn (seed 11) after the rules were set. In 49 the site is the place the verse means, or the land of the people it names. Some are kept apart correctly: נחל מצרים (Isa 27:12) is the Brook of Egypt, not Egypt, and the Sharon of 1 Chr 5:16 is the one east of the Jordan, not the coastal plain. Amos 4:3's הרמונה is no sure place. 4 of the 50 points have fewer than 300 votes.
- **Hebrew only** (D5): a site is shown by TIPNR's Hebrew headword (consonants; the first of its spellings); TIPNR's and OpenBible's English names stay internal join keys, and sites are numbered (`place_id`, canon order of first mention).
- **Names of several words as the text writes them** (`compound_name`). TIPNR writes a compound as the name, then the words added to it (`חֶבְרוֹן+H6010K«H6010=עֵמֶק`). Read as one string this gave חברוןעמק; Hebrew puts the construct noun first (עמק חברון), an adjective after (בית חורן תחתון), and the text inflects (ערבת מואב, not ערבה + מואב).
  - A compound's name is therefore taken from a verse TIPNR gives for it, or one up to `max_shift` away (English numbering), the reference itself first. The source is the consecutive words whose OSHB lemmas cover all the parts, as many words as the parts have.
  - The first word loses its prefix letters (ו ב כ ל מ ש), and a word the morphology marks as directional loses its ה; a run that needs neither is preferred.
  - If no verse has the parts together, TIPNR's forms are used, a common noun (Strong's part of speech) moved first.
  - A name of one Strong's number keeps TIPNR's spelling, without a ה of direction the text marks (עלמן דבלתימה, Num 33:46, is עלמן דבלתים).
  - 33 headwords changed (2026-10-17): the 32 compounds (valleys, בית חורן תחתון / עליון, מדבר סיני, חרשת הגוים, מגדל חננאל, גיא המון גוג …) and עלמן דבלתים. The other names of §16.57's lookup are read the same way.
- **Its names** (`names`, for the jump box, §16.57): the headword, then every other Hebrew name TIPNR gives the site whose Strong's number the text uses for it: ציון, שלם, יבוס for Jerusalem, לוז for Bethel, עין משפט for Kadesh. TIPNR's epithets the text never uses as this place's name (עזב, שממה for Jerusalem) are left out. The lemma's own form will not do: OSHB writes a compound name under its first word's lemma (בית for בית אל).
- **Served:**
  - DB: `places`, `place_mentions`, `name_kinds` (+ `meta.places`), empty without the stage.
  - API: `/places?kind&book&unit` (the sites, most named first; in a book or unit, `n_here`); `name_kind` and `sites` on `/entities/{lemma}`; a `places` dossier entry (D100): the places the unit's verses name and the most named one, linking to the map of the unit.
  - Viewer: on the Names page a name's SDBH kind and its sites (kind, point, votes, a contested second site). The map is P2.
- **Limits:**
  - One gazetteer (TIPNR's decisions on who is who, OpenBible's votes on where).
  - A compound name's second word is not its own place.
  - Points are points: a region or a river is placed at one representative point.
  - Israel and Judah are mostly people or person in both TIPNR and SDBH, so the land of Israel has no site of its own.

### 16.57 A real map: the places where they lie (`data/basemap.py`, `/places/basemap`, Places on the map)
- **Why:** the gazetteer (§16.56) gives each place a point; a reader wants to see them on the ground, a book's or a chapter's together, and to go from a place to its verses and from a passage to its places.
- **The base map** (`bsim places`, `data/basemap.py`; `places.map`). Natural Earth's 1:10m land, lakes and rivers (public domain; `bsim download --only geo`, pinned commit):
  - clipped to −10…70° E, 5…47° N (from Tarshish to Hodu), polygons by Sutherland–Hodgman and lines by Liang–Barsky, and thinned by Douglas–Peucker at 0.01° (about 1 km), all in plain Python (no GIS library);
  - islands and lakes under 0.005 square degrees left out, rivers up to Natural Earth's rank 6 (the Jordan is 6);
  - 7 land shapes, 61 lakes (the Dead Sea and the Sea of Galilee among them), 85 rivers: 17,689 points, 280 KB as JSON (`map_shapes`, fetched once and cached).
- **A fix on the way:** TIPNR writes a place without a point as a map link `@0,14z`, its zoom read as a longitude; such links are no point now (Nod, Bithron), so 816 of the 820 sites are located.
- **Drawing** (viewer, `lib/geo.ts`, `components/GeoMap.tsx`): an SVG projected by hand like the other charts. Equirectangular at 31.5° N, so a degree east and a degree north are the same length around Jerusalem. Land, lakes and rivers under the points:
  - a point's radius grows with the log of the verses naming it, on one fixed scale, so a size means the same in a chapter as in the whole Bible; its colour is its kind (town, region, landform, water; grey for unknown);
  - the names of the most named places in view are written beside them, leaving out any label that would overlap one already placed;
  - drag pans, + / − zoom; with keyboard focus the arrow keys step through the places (most named first) and Enter opens one.
- **Places on the map** (Overview, `/places`): filters by book, chapter, unit (`?unit=` from other pages) and kind. Three views: the land, the Near East, and these places (fitted to the points; the default when filtered). Beside the map are the most named places or a place's panel: its point and votes, its names (linking to the Names page), its verses per book, the places named in the same verses (`serve.places.partners` = 15), and its verses with the words that name it marked.
- **From the jump box** (Ctrl+K, D99): a place's name (`/places/lookup`) opens the map at it (`/places?p=`). When the place lies outside the view (Babylon from the land), the view moves to centre on it at the same size; the same happens when a panel's partner is picked. A place matched by its whole name (חברון, ציון for Jerusalem, ירושלים for ירושלם) comes before the words, unless a word that is not one of its names is in more verses (עין the eye, 800 verses, before עין the town, 5). A place matched by the start of a name (בית → בית אל) comes after them. Each choice shows its kind, its verses and its first verse, which tell apart the places of one name (the three Aphek).
- **Where this happens:** on chapter and pericope pages, a small map fitted to the places the passage names; a point or the link opens the full map on the passage.
- **The reader's places layer** (§16.39): each word naming a place is boxed in its kind's colour, and its tooltip names the place; the legend lists the passage's places, each opening the map.
- **Served:** `map_shapes` (+ `meta.places.map`); `/places/basemap`, `/places/{id}`, `/places/{id}/verses`; `places` on `/reader/{unit}`.
- **Limits:**
  - A point per place: a region or a river is shown where OpenBible puts its representative point, not its extent.
  - Votes are not drawn on the map; they are in the place panel.
  - The rivers and coasts are today's (Natural Earth), not the ancient ones: the Mediterranean coast and the Persian Gulf's head have moved since.

### 16.58 Place lists on the ground (`bsim place-lists`, `analysis/place_lists.py`, `/place-lists`, Place lists on the ground)
- **Why:** the text lists places: the stations of the wilderness, the borders of the land, the towns of Judah and of the tribes, the cities of the table of nations. With the gazetteer's points (§16.56), are they named in an order that walks the ground, and do Joshua's districts hold their towns together? Two claims fixed in `place_lists` before the first run, with known answers (an itinerary walks; tribes hold land together).
- **The lists** (`place_lists.lists`): Num 33:3–49 (itinerary), Num 34:3–12 and Josh 15:1–12 (boundaries), Josh 15:21–62 and Josh 19 (town lists), Gen 10 (the table of nations' cities). A list's places are those its verses name (`place_mentions`), each at its first mention, ordered by verse and then by word (`words.content_lemmas`); itineraries name each station twice ("from X … at Y"), so the first mention is the station's place. Regions are left out (no single site): towns, landforms and water for itineraries and boundaries, towns for town lists.
- **Order:** the path is the sum of great-circle km between consecutive places. Against `shuffles` (9,999) random orders of the same places, one-sided, `p` = the share as short (+1 smoothed). For scale, a short path through the same places (nearest neighbour from every start, the best improved by 2-opt; a yardstick, not the shortest) and `order_score` = (random mean − path) / (random mean − short path): 0 no better than random, 1 as short.
- **Districts** (`place_lists.districts`): Judah's towns in the eleven districts closed by "ערים … וחצריהן" (Josh 15:21–32, 33–36, 37–41, 42–44, 45–47, 48–51, 52–54, 55–57, 58–59, 60, 61–62), and the towns of Benjamin (Josh 18:21–28) and of the six tribes of Josh 19 (a known answer). The mean km between two towns of one district against the district labels shuffled over the towns (sizes kept); per district, its own mean against the same shuffles; and the share of towns whose nearest town is of their district.
- **Sure sites:** every test is run again on the places whose OpenBible identification has a vote of at least `sure_score` (500 of 1,000). An obscure town is often placed by the company it keeps in the list, so the first run alone could be circular.
- **Results** (all located / sure sites alone):

| List | Places | Path | Random orders | Short path | Order | p | Sure sites: places, p |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Gen 10 | 15 | 4,971 km | 11,233 km | 3,751 km | 84% | 0.0001 | 7, 0.004 |
| Num 33:3–49 | 48 | 2,172 km | 8,728 km | 1,276 km | 88% | 0.0001 | 11, 0.0001 |
| Num 34:3–12 | 15 | 1,561 km | 3,600 km | 798 km | 73% | 0.0001 | 7, 0.029 |
| Josh 15:1–12 | 30 | 1,058 km | 2,145 km | 518 km | 67% | 0.0001 | 17, 0.026 |
| Josh 15:21–62 | 117 | 1,951 km | 4,463 km | 780 km | 68% | 0.0001 | 32, 0.0001 |
| Josh 19 | 107 | 2,033 km | 8,963 km | 931 km | 86% | 0.0001 | 37, 0.0001 |

  - All six lists are shorter than every one of 9,999 random orders, and on sure sites alone all six still are at p < 0.05.
  - Judah's districts: two towns of one district are 27.6 km apart on average against 38.5 km shuffled (p 0.0001; sure sites 17.0 against 34.8 km). 74% of the towns have a town of their own district nearest. Seven of the ten districts with two towns or more cluster on their own (p ≤ 0.05); the Negev district (15:21–32, 37 towns: 35.4 against 38.4 km, p 0.24), the three Philistine cities (15:45–47) and 15:52–54 (p 0.06) do not.
  - The tribes: 23.6 km against 84.8 km (p 0.0001), every tribe on its own.
- **The longest steps as leads:** a step far longer than its neighbours is often an identification to doubt, not a list out of order. In Josh 15:3 Hezron (חצרון) resolves to the northern Hazor (285 km out of the line), and in Num 34:11 the Ain by Riblah to the southern Ain (365 km); both are TIPNR / OpenBible joins of §16.56, kept as they are and listed, not mended here.
- **Viewer** (Overview → Place lists on the ground, `/place-lists`): the lists with path, random mean, short path, order, p and the sure-site test; one list on the map (`?list=`) as a line in the text's order or along the short path (`?show=short`), numbered points (names too for a list of 20 or fewer), the random orders as a histogram with the text's path and the short path marked, and the steps with their km (the three longest marked) and verses; the districts (`?group=`) on the map coloured by district, with each district's test.
- **Served:** `place_lists`, `place_list_steps`, `place_districts`, `place_district_towns` (+ `meta.place_lists`); `/place-lists`, `/place-lists/{key}`, `/place-districts/{key}`; findings `place_lists_order`, `place_lists_districts`.
- **Limits:**
  - A random order is a weak null for a list that keeps to one region: any list of nearby places in coherent pieces beats it. The order score and the short path say how much better than that the text's order is, not whether it is a route.
  - Distances are as the crow flies between OpenBible's best-voted points, today's identifications.
  - A boundary list's landforms and water (Hermon, the Jordan, the Sea of Kinneret) are representative points, not the line they draw.

### 16.59 Equal values, related words? (`bsim gematria-links`, `analysis/gematria_links.py`, `/gematria-links`)
- **Why:** gematria (§16.53) pairs words of equal letter value, נחש = משיח = 358, יין = סוד = 70 (Eruvin 65a), and reads the pairing as a link in meaning. Are words of equal value any closer in meaning than words that merely look like them? And the numbers claimed for Genesis 1:1 (2701 = 37 × 73, a triangular number; 7 words, 28 letters): are they rarer than any verse's own? The claims are fixed in `gematria_links` before the first run; the expected answer is no to both.
- **Words** (`gematria_links.min_count` = 3): content lemmas valued by their headword (`gematria_lemmas`), names left out, at least two letters, with an SDBH domain profile, a root family (§16.38) and a vector: 3,373 lemmas.
- **Relatedness**, three ways:
  - `domains`: the cosine of the two lemmas' SDBH domain profiles (the domains tagged on their occurrences, weighted);
  - `root`: one root family;
  - `vector`: the cosine of their BEREL vectors (as §16.23: the encoder's last hidden state over the word's subword tokens in its verse, the mean of up to `max_occ` = 20 occurrences drawn with the seed; 41,616 word vectors from 17,697 verses, under a minute on the GPU).
- **The test:** all pairs of equal standard value (14,751); the 3,423 spelled with the same letters (homographs such as בית's lemmas, anagrams) are equal by construction and left out, so 11,328 are tested. Their mean relatedness against `draws` = 999 sets of matched pairs: each pair keeps one word (either, at random) and replaces the other by a lemma of the same letter count and frequency band (`bands`, occurrences) whose value differs and which is not spelled with the kept word's letters. One-sided: `p` is the share of draws as related. The finding holds when the pairs are more related on any measure at α / 3.
- **A check of the measures:** 2,183 pairs of one root family (spelled apart, of different values), matched the same way, must come out closer, and they do: domains 0.257 against 0.013, vectors 0.673 against 0.596 (p 0.001 each). The measures see relatedness where it is.
- **Results:** equal values link nothing.
  - Domains: 0.0085 against 0.0097 (90% of draws 0.0086–0.0110; p 0.96): if anything, slightly less related than matched pairs.
  - One root family: 0.01% of pairs against 0.04% (p 0.99).
  - Vectors: 0.602 against 0.601 (90% of draws 0.600–0.602; p 0.06, above α / 3).
- **The tradition's equivalences** (`equivalences`), each ranked among the tested pairs: נחש = משיח shares no domain and no root, and its words are used more alike than in 74% of the equal-value pairs; יין = סוד 65%; אחד = אהבה 31%. Unremarkable.
- **Genesis 1:1** (`verse`): of the 23,206 verses, 1,875 have 7 words, 635 have 28 letters, 675 a value divisible by 37, 6,211 a value of two prime factors and 305 a triangular value. Only Genesis 1:1 has all five, where 0.005 verses would if the properties were independent. But they were chosen by looking at it. The look-elsewhere control describes every verse by its own numbers (word count, letter count, the largest prime factor of its value): 63% of verses are the only one with their description, and Genesis 1:1 is not even among them. Four verses share its 7 words, 28 letters and largest prime factor 73: Ps 44:5 (1679 = 23 × 73), Ps 54:9 (1387 = 19 × 73), Prov 7:7 (1533) and Job 24:10 (1752).
- **Viewer** (Patterns → Equal values, related words?, `/gematria-links`): the test and the check as tables with their verdicts; the tradition's equivalences with their rank; Genesis 1:1's properties, the verses with all of them and its twins; the pairs of equal value, most used alike first, by domains or by value, a value filter and the pairs spelled alike on request, each lemma linking to its page and each value to the calculator.
- **Served:** `gematria_pairs` (+ `meta.gematria_links`: the tests, the equivalences, the verse and its properties); `/gematria-links`, `/gematria-links/pairs`; findings `gematria_links`, `gematria_measures`, `gematria_genesis`.
- **Limits:**
  - Lemmas, not word forms: a form's value includes its prefixes (והארץ), which is how some of the tradition's equivalences are reckoned; those are on the calculator page, not tested here.
  - The standard values only (`system`); mispar gadol, ordinal and katan would each be a test of their own.
  - BEREL vectors carry genre and style along with meaning (§16.23); the domains and the roots are the sense-only readings.

### 16.60 The geographic arc (`bsim place-arc`, `analysis/place_arc.py`, `/place-arc`, The geographic arc)
- **Why:** with every place located (§16.56), each chapter can be placed where it happens, and the text read as a path across the map. Do consecutive chapters stay close, does the centre move where the story goes, and do the oracles against the nations range wider than the rest of the prophets? Three claims fixed in `place_arc` before the first run.
- **A chapter's centre:** the mean on the sphere (unit vectors averaged) of the located places it names (`place_mentions` of every kind: a land, Egypt or Moab, says where as much as a town), each place counted once per verse. A chapter is placed with at least `min_mentions` = 2 such mentions: 556 of the 929. Its **spread** is the mean km of those mentions from the centre; its **reach** the centre's km from Jerusalem (the `canaan` anchor).
- **The arc:** the km between the centres of a book's consecutive placed chapters (an unplaced chapter is passed over), summed, against `shuffles` = 9,999 random orders of the same chapters, one-sided; per book with at least `min_chapters` = 3 placed, and over all 31 such books together (each shuffled on its own, the sums added).
- **Known moves** (`place_arc.moves`, a check of the centres): Abram from Mesopotamia to Canaan (Gen 11 → 12), Jacob's house down to Egypt (Gen 35–37 → 46–47), out of Egypt into the wilderness (Exod 1–12 → Num 10–21), Judah to Babylon (2 Kgs 21–23 → 24–25). A span's centre pools its mentions. A move holds when the centre after lies nearer the region entered than the centre before does, and the centre before nearer the region left than the centre after does; the regions are anchor points (`regions`: Babylon, Jerusalem, Egypt's point, Kadesh-barnea).
- **The oracles against the nations** (`oracles`: Amos 1–2, Isa 13–23, Jer 46–51, Ezek 25–32; 27 chapters, all placed) against the other placed chapters of the prophets (`prophets`: Isaiah to Malachi, 143): the oracles' mean spread and mean reach, each against the labels shuffled over the 170 chapters (9,999 times), one-sided at α / 2.
- **Results:**
  - **The arc holds:** consecutive placed chapters lie 223 km apart on average against 267 km shuffled (p 0.0001). 9 of 31 books hold on their own: Genesis (228 against 355 km), 2 Kings (145 against 268), Jeremiah (321 against 436), Ezekiel, Isaiah, Judges, Joshua, Exodus and 1 Chronicles. Samuel, 1 Kings, Psalms, Daniel and the books of the return do not: they keep to one land, or a place named in passing moves the centre.
  - **All four moves hold:** Gen 11's centre lies 135 km from Babylon, Gen 12's 106 km from Jerusalem (786 km apart); Gen 35–37 by Jerusalem, Gen 46–47 152 km from Egypt; Exod 1–12 22 km from Egypt, Num 10–21 60 km from Kadesh; 2 Kgs 21–23 by Jerusalem, 2 Kgs 24–25 333 km from Babylon, a third of the way there (Jerusalem is named with it).
  - **The oracles do not hold:** spread 412 km against 296 (p 0.07), reach 331 km against 275 (p 0.19), both above α / 2. Their medians are wider (spread 425 against 225 km, reach 260 against 166), but an oracle against one nation names that nation's own towns: those against Moab are the tightest chapters of all (Jer 48 26 km, Isa 15 27 km, Isa 16 40 km), while Isa 23 (Tyre and Tarshish, 1,198 km) and Isa 20 (Egypt, Cush and Assyria, 906 km) are among the widest.
- **The longest steps as leads:** a far step is mostly a far place named in passing: Jonah 4 (Tarshish, 2,204 km from Jonah 3), Ps 48 after Ps 45 (1,615 km), Isa 47 (the Chaldeans, 1,552 km), Jer 10 (silver from Tarshish, 1,477 km).
