- **Viewer** (Overview → The geographic arc, `/place-arc`): the arc's test; one book (`?book=`, a select or the table) on the map, its chapters' centres numbered and joined in order (the oracles' in the town colour), beside them each placed chapter with the place it names most, the step from the one before (the chapter named when chapters between are unplaced; the three longest marked) and its spread, linking to the chapter and to its places on the map; the books' table; the known moves as a table and as arrows on the map; the oracles' tests and chapters.
- **Served:** `place_arc_chapters`, `place_arc_books` (+ `meta.place_arc`: the arc, the moves, the oracles); `/place-arc`, `/place-arc/{book_id}`; findings `place_arc_order`, `place_arc_moves`, `place_arc_oracles`. `GeoMap` takes a point's title (`titleOf`).
- **Limits:**
  - Every place named pulls on the centre: a chapter set in Jerusalem that remembers Egypt is placed between them. The text does not say which places are the scene.
  - A chapter is a coarse unit: Gen 12 goes from Haran through Canaan to Egypt and back.
  - Points are OpenBible's best-voted identifications, and a land is one point (§16.56); the anchors are points too.
  - Random order is a weak null for a book that keeps to one land, as for the place lists (§16.58): a book can hold without telling a journey.

### 16.61 Named together, near together? (`bsim place-links`, `analysis/place_links.py`, `/place-links`)
- **Why:** the names of §16.11 are linked by the verses they share. With every place located (§16.56), are places the text names together also near each other, as the places of one story or one district would be? Or does the text join places regardless of distance? Fixed in `place_links` before the first run; and the far pairs it joins anyway are the leads.
- **Links:** two located places are linked when at least `min_together` = 2 verses name both, more often than independence over the verses expects (each place counted once per verse). The link's strength is Dunning's G² of the 2 × 2 table over the verses (`g2_table`, as for names). 510 pairs of 277 places.
- **The test:** Spearman's rho between the linked pairs' G² and their great-circle km, against `shuffles` = 9,999 runs with the coordinates shuffled among the linked places, one-sided. The shuffle keeps who is linked with whom, and how strongly; only where the places lie is drawn again. Three variants:
  - `all`: every located place (the test);
  - `sure`: both places sure sites (an OpenBible vote of at least 500);
  - `no_lists`: without the verses of the text's place and town lists (`lists`: Gen 10, Num 33–34, Josh 12–21, 1 Chr 1–9, Ezra 2, Neh 7, Neh 11), which name neighbours by design.
- **The decay curve:** every pair of places each named in 2 verses or more (an unlinked pair included), by km bins (`bins`): how many are linked, and the linked ones' median G².
- **Leads:** linked pairs at least `far_km` = 150 km apart, most strongly linked first. A known answer: "from Dan to Beersheba" (`known`), the land's length, among the first `top` = 10.
- **Results:** the claim holds on all three variants.
  - Every located place: rho −0.32 against −0.00 shuffled (90% of shuffles −0.14 to 0.13; p 0.0001).
  - Sure sites: −0.33 (315 pairs; p 0.0002).
  - Without the lists: −0.30 (381 pairs; p 0.0003).
  - The decay: 6.3% of the pairs within 10 km are linked, 2.2% at 10–25 km, 0.8% at 25–50 km, 0.2–0.4% beyond 50 km. The linked pairs' median G² falls from 28 within 10 km to 15–19 beyond 100 km.
  - Dan and Beersheba (236 km, 9 verses together) are the third far pair of 149. The others near the top:
    - Babylon and the Chaldeans (one land);
    - the cities of the Assyrian boast (Hamath, Arpad, Sepharvaim, Hena, Ivvah: 2 Kgs 18–19, Isa 36–37);
    - the exiles' places of 2 Kgs 17:6 (Halah, Habor, Gozan);
    - Babylon and Riblah, and Babylon and Jerusalem;
    - Tubal and Meshech;
    - the powers of the age, Assyria and Egypt (1,300 km), and Egypt and Cush.
- **Viewer** (Overview → Named together, near together?, `/place-links`): the test and its variants; every linked pair as a scatter of G² against km, both on log scales, the far pairs marked; the decay per variant (`?bins=`); the known answer, the leads as a table and as lines on the map; the linked pairs, strongest, farthest or nearest first, and one place's pairs (`?place=`, from any place name on the page).
- **Served:** `place_links`, `place_link_bins` (+ `meta.place_links`); `/place-links`, `/place-links/pairs`; findings `place_links_decay`, `place_links_known`.
- **Limits:**
  - Co-mention in a verse is the only link: a chapter's places are not linked across its verses.
  - A land is a point (§16.56), so its links are measured from one representative site; places at one point (Sodom and Gomorrah, Damascus and Aram) are 0 km apart.
  - Lists are not the only text that names neighbours by design; the no-lists variant leaves out the long ones only.

### 16.62 Letters at equal skips (`bsim els`, `analysis/els.py`, `/els`)
- **Why:** read every 50th letter from the ת of Genesis 1:1 and you get תורה (Rabbenu Bahya, on Gen 1:1). Since the 1990s "Bible codes" have read names and dates in the Torah's letters at skips in the thousands. Any long text holds such sequences by chance. The question is whether the Torah holds more of them than its controls. Counts against controls, not codes; fixed in `els` before the first run, and the expected answer is no.
- **The letters:** the written text (`els.text` = ketiv, built as for the Masorah, §16.55), consonants, final forms folded, verse after verse: 304,801 letters in the Torah, 78,063 in Genesis.
- **Terms** (`els.terms`), 27 names, a list of names rather than the rabbis and dates of Witztum, Rips and Rosenberg:
  - the fathers and mothers;
  - Moses, Aaron and Miriam;
  - kings and prophets;
  - Ezra, Nehemiah, Mordecai and Esther;
  - Hillel, Shammai, Akiva, Rashi and Rambam.
- **Hits:** an occurrence at a skip of `min_skip` = 2 to `max_skip` = 1,000 letters, forwards or backwards (a backward hit is the reversed name forwards; a palindrome counts once). Counted on the GPU: each start at the name's first letter against every skip, then narrowed letter by letter.
- **Expected:** under a shuffle of the scope's letters the expected count is exact: the placements (Σ over skips of n − (L − 1)·d) times the chance that L given positions spell the name, Π (count of the letter − its earlier uses in the name) / (n − k). Each name's log ratio is log((hits + 0.5) / (expected + 0.5)); a text's statistic R is the mean over the names.
- **Controls**, for the Torah and for Genesis alone:
  - the same letters shuffled (199 draws);
  - the same verses in shuffled order, each verse's letters kept (199 draws);
  - blocks of the same length cut from the other books in canon order (Joshua on), each against its own expectation: 2 for the Torah, 11 for Genesis.

  One-sided, p is the share of a control's R at least the text's (+1 smoothed). The claim, "no more hits than the controls", holds when no control has p ≤ 0.05.
- **Known answers** (`els.known`, a check of the letter string): the sequences read at the start of each book.
  - תורה at 50 from Gen 1:1 and from Exod 1:1;
  - יהוה at 8 from Lev 1:1;
  - הרות (backwards) at 50 from Num 1:1, and at 49 from Deut 1:5.

  Each is reported with how often the word occurs at that skip anywhere in the Torah.
- **Results:** the claim holds.

| Text | Hits | Expected | R | Letters shuffled, p | Verses shuffled, p | Other books, p |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Torah | 1,428,731 | 1,429,940 | −0.011 | 0.62 | 0.57 | 0.33 |
| Genesis | 320,646 | 319,141 | −0.009 | 0.47 | 0.45 | 0.25 |

  - **Torah against its controls:** its R sits inside the shuffles' 90% range (letters −0.049 to +0.026, verses −0.049 to +0.033); the two blocks of the other books have −0.030 and −0.049.
  - **Name by name**, against the letter shuffles:
    - three short names run high (מרים, דוד, הלל, p 0.005);
    - five run low (שרה, רחל, אסתר, שמאי, רשי, p 1.0);
    - both groups are what 27 tests on letters that are not independent give, and they cancel in R.
  - **The tradition's five are all in our letters**, each starting in the verse named. But they are not rare:
    - תורה occurs at skip 50 nineteen times in the Torah (about 10 expected);
    - יהוה at skip 8, 36 times (27 expected);
    - הרות at skip 49, 4 times (10 expected).
- **Viewer** (Patterns → Letters at equal skips, `/els`): the Torah or Genesis (`?scope=`). The page shows:
  - the counts and R against each control, with a verdict;
  - every control draw as a dot beside the text's R;
  - the blocks;
  - name by name: hits each way, expected, ratio, the letter shuffles' 90% range and p, the verse shuffles' mean, the other books' ratio;
  - the tradition's sequences with how often each recurs.
- **Served:** `els_terms`, `els_draws` (+ `meta.els`); `/els`; findings `els_counts`, `els_known`.
- **Limits:**
  - Counts, not the proximity of pairs: Witztum, Rips and Rosenberg (1994) measured how close a rabbi's name lies to his dates, and McKay, Bar-Natan, Bar-Hillel and Kalai (1999) answered it on War and Peace. Their test needs their list and their spellings and is not run here.
  - A different list of names, or skips past 1,000, are other tests.
  - A rare name moves R in steps (יחזקאל is expected about once), so the shuffles' R falls in two clusters.
  - The letter string is ours: 4 letters fewer than the tradition's 304,805 (§16.55).

### 16.63 The family tree (`bsim genealogy`, `analysis/genealogy.py`, `/genealogy`)
Status: data built (M88), viewer (M89); review M90–M91. Progress and hand-off: `docs/genealogy/PROGRESS.md`.
- **Why:** one tree of everyone the Hebrew Bible names, where each parent → child link is backed by the verse(s) that state it, can be collapsed at any node, and marks where the text disagrees with itself.
- **The backbone:** TIPNR's PERSON records (already downloaded with `geo`, §16.56). Each record has these columns: parents (father + mother), siblings, partners, offspring, tribe or nation, and type (Male / Female / Group).
  - Markers on the links: `(?)` an ambiguity decided by STEPBible, `(a)` an ancestor rather than the parent, `(d)` a group descended from them, `(f)` the founder of a place.
  - Only people with Hebrew Bible references are kept.
  - TIPNR gives no verse per link, and settles each conflict into one choice, so both of those come from here.
  - Its parents and offspring columns agree in every case but one, and no parent field names two people.
- **A person's verses:** TIPNR's references are in English (KJV) numbering.
  - `data/kjv.py` moves them to ours with 39 rules, one per chapter whose break falls elsewhere in the Hebrew (Exod 8:1 = 7:26, 1 Chr 6:1–15 = 5:27–41, Joel 3 = 4). Each rule gives where an English run of verses starts; the verses after it follow in canon order.
  - Without the rules, 1 Chr 6, the Levites' genealogy, is 15 verses off throughout.
  - With them, 98.3% of TIPNR's 29,782 Hebrew Bible references name the person in that very verse; the Psalm titles and the Decalogue's merged verses are what is left.
  - Each reference then snaps to the nearest verse within `max_shift` that names the person, and the name's words there are its tokens.
  - References marked by their occurrence in the verse (`Exo.7.10a`, 15% of all) are read too. `bsim places` still drops them, and its output is unchanged.
- **Who is in the tree:**
  - people;
  - nations and clans (Gen 10, the tribes, founders), as group nodes;
  - unnamed people needed as links (אשת חצרון, Lot's daughters), as `anon:` nodes;
  - spouses and concubines, so each child sits under its mother.
- **Proof verses, found automatically:**
  - **candidates:** the verses naming both people;
  - **ranking by cues**, strongest first (`genealogy.cues`):
    - `construct`: child, בן / בת, parent within `construct_span` words (יצחק בן אברהם);
    - `sons_of`: בני / בנות right before the parent, the child after (ובני יצחק יהודה);
    - `begot`: a form of ילד;
    - `kin`: any son, daughter, father or mother word;
    - `together`: both named, no cue;
    - spouses: `wife`, אשה next to either name.

    Ties go to the nearer names, then canon order. The first `proof_k` are kept, with the display tokens of both names.
  - **a hint, not a proof:** a link with no verse naming both gets the child's verse whose previous `near_verses` name the parent (`nearby`: a list's heading, "ובני אלפעל" then the sons). With neither, the link is unproved.
- **The review:** block by block in Jewish canon order (19 blocks, `PROGRESS.md`), each checked against the Hebrew and kept in `curation/genealogy/NN-<block>.yaml`.
  - The files hold person keys and Sefaria-style references in Hebrew numbering, never scripture text.
  - A link is confirmed, fixed, added or rejected. Its kind is son, descendant, adopted, levirate, founder or unstated.
  - A conflict lists its readings, each a parent and its verses, and the first reading is the one the tree draws.
  - Conflicts in this round:
    - `bible`: two passages disagree, e.g. Zerubbabel son of Shealtiel (Ezra 3:2) or of Pedaiah (1 Chr 3:19);
    - `descendant`: בן spans generations, e.g. Jehu son of Nimshi (2 Kgs 9:2, 9:20).
  - Rabbinic identifications are deferred.
  - A key or reference that does not resolve stops the build.
- **Identities (M91):** one person under several TIPNR keys is a `same:` entry with its proof verses (user, 2026-10-22).
  - `merged`: the keys become one person, the first key's, before any link is decided. Its verses, names and links join, two links that become one fold, and the other keys still name the person in every file. A link that two files decided under the two pairs of keys must agree.
  - A merged person left with two fathers (or two mothers) stops the build unless a conflict lists them as readings: the text then disagrees with itself.
  - `possible`: both persons stay, and the panel says "perhaps the same person as …" with the verses.
  - They go in `identities.parquet` / `genealogy_identities`. The cross-book steps' files (`genealogy.cross_blocks`) have no verse range and no row in the blocks table.
- **Adoption:** an `adopted` father link (Jacob to Ephraim and Manasseh, Gen 48:5) is never the drawn one while the child has another father. It is a cross-link in the tree, marked in the panel, and its children are not the adopted child's siblings.
- **Placement:**
  - Each person has one primary parent: a conflict's first reading, else the father (an adoptive one last), else the mother. Other parents and other readings are cross-links (`drawn` = 0).
  - Someone without a parent who married in is drawn beside the spouse rather than as a node: a spouse, or a parent only of children drawn under the other parent (Eve, Sarah, Keturah). Of a couple neither of whom has a parent, the husband is placed and the wife is beside him.
  - Anyone else without a parent hangs on the eponym of TIPNR's tribe or nation of the father (`genealogy.tribes`: the twelve tribes, Israel, Edom → Esau, Canaan, Egypt, Ammon, Moab, Midian, Aram, Asshur, Cush) by an `unstated` link. Otherwise they go under "unattached", grouped by the book that first names them. A review file's `people:` correction `tribe: ""` takes a person off TIPNR's tribe where the text does not support it (M91: Seir the Horite and the kings of Edom, off Esau).
  - A cycle is an error.
- **The review sheet** (`bsim genealogy-review <block>`, `artifacts/genealogy/review/<block>.md`):
  - every link whose best verse is in the block (an unproved one by its child's first verse), as a `child <- parent` line with its verses and the names marked;
  - the people first named there without a parent;
  - the cue verses (בן / בת next to a name, or ילד, with two names or more) that no link uses as proof and no review file has dismissed.
- **Results (M88, before any review):**
  - 2,845 people: 2,737 persons, 73 groups, 35 unnamed.
  - 1,818 parent links (1,553 father, 265 mother), 128 spouse pairs, 2,603 sibling pairs.
  - 1,463 parent links (80%) have a verse naming both; by best cue: construct 616, sons of 461, begot 217, kin 146, together 23. Another 218 have a `nearby` hint and 137 have nothing.
  - 12 links are TIPNR's "ancestor" or "descended group", and 71 carry its `(?)`.
  - Placement: Adam at the root, 1,553 under a parent, 169 on a tribe's eponym, 67 beside a spouse, 1,055 under "unattached". Most of those 1,055 have no family in the text at all (officials, eunuchs, Esther's courtiers).
  - The stage runs in about 3 s.
- **Viewer** (Overview → Family tree, `/genealogy?p=`):
  - **Layout:** a tidy tree laid out by `d3-hierarchy` over the expanded part only. Nodes are HTML buttons over an SVG edge layer, and nodes off screen are not drawn.
  - **Collapsing:** any node collapses or expands, showing a count of the descendants it hides. Search opens the path to a person.
  - **Children by mother** are grouped under mother headers. A mother who is the wife of the child's other father, a conflict's reading not drawn, heads no group under the drawn one (M91: Asriel, drawn under Gilead, born of Manasseh's concubine).
  - **Markers**, each a colour token plus a second cue:
    - a conflict is amber with ⚠;
    - a descendant link is violet and dashed;
    - an unstated link is grey and dotted;
    - a link not yet reviewed has a faint outline.
  - **Side panel:** parents, spouses, children, siblings, the proof verses in Hebrew with both names marked, the readings of a conflict, and the identities ("read as one person, though STEPBible lists 2 entries"; "perhaps the same person as …", with its verses).
  - On a phone the panel becomes a bottom sheet. Names are shown in Hebrew only (D5, D92).
  - **As built (M89):**
    - Every expanded person's children hang from a header row: one header per mother when they have more than one, those of an unnamed mother and those attached by a link not stated in groups of their own; otherwise an unseen header. Each generation is then two levels of the hierarchy, so generations line up across the tree and no two nodes overlap.
    - The unnamed get a name from what they are to the one they are drawn under or beside ("daughter of לוט", "בת לוט"); the "unattached" groups take the book's name.
    - It opens on Adam's first three generations. A person in the address opens the line down to them and brings them into view; one chosen in the tree stays where it is, and opening or closing a node keeps that node in place.
    - The panel can open or close everything below a person. Hebrew mirrors the tree, so the first-born is on the right.
    - Drag pans; pinch, Ctrl + wheel or the buttons zoom; the arrow keys pan when the tree has focus. `lib/panZoom.ts` is shared with the map (`GeoMap`), which gains pinch and Ctrl + wheel.
    - The skeleton is about 650 KB of JSON (gzipped in transit) for 2,816 nodes, 64 spouses and 113 cross-links, and is fetched once.
- **The look** (M92, D103):
  - **On most of the screen.** The page drops the site's column width. The tree fills the width and the screen's height below a one-line header (the lede folds into "About the tree"). The search, the zoom bar and the legend (behind its button) float over it. A chosen person opens as a card over the tree's far side, and the person is brought into the part the card leaves. On a phone the card is still the bottom sheet.
  - **Avatars, as in a printed family tree.** Each person is a round avatar with the name under it: a man's or a woman's silhouette, three heads for a nation or clan, a book for the "unattached" groups (`components/Avatar.tsx`). A spouse drawn beside is an avatar of their own, joined by a dashed line, the first on the side of the first-born and a second on the other side, so no mother's line crosses another's; any more are a count. A mother drawn beside needs no header: her children hang from the middle of the line between her and the father. Rings mark a conflict (amber, with ⚠) and a link not yet checked (dashed).
  - **The chosen person's line.** The line from Adam down to the chosen person is lit: yellow avatars, a thick line along each person's route down from their parent (`LaidNode.route`), and the mothers on it.
  - **Zoom.** A plain wheel zooms about the pointer (`usePanZoom`'s `wheel: 'zoom'`; the map keeps Ctrl + wheel). Shift + wheel or a sideways swipe pans; double-click zooms in. The bar has + / −, the zoom level (back to 100%), fit the whole open tree (down to 2%), back to Adam and full screen; button moves ease. Below 45% the names stand alone; below 22% each person is a dot, which a click zooms into. A tree opened all the way (about 1,500 avatars) stays smooth.
- **From elsewhere** (M91, D102):
  - **Jump box** (Ctrl+K): a People group after the words and places. A person's Hebrew name (`/genealogy/lookup`) opens `/genealogy?p=` at them, each told apart by the parent they are drawn under (or their tribe's eponym, or the spouse beside them) and the verse that first names them, as the tree's own search does (`lib/people.ts`).
  - **Dossier** (D64): a `people` entry, the people of the tree that the unit's verses name, the most named first (ties: the most named in the whole text), linking to the tree at that person. Its verses are TIPNR's references with merged keys folded (`genealogy_mentions`, from `mentions.parquet`); the unnamed and the virtual nodes have none.

### 16.64 The workbench: your sets contrasted and checked (`api/workbench.py`, `/workbench/contrast`, `/workbench/check`, `/workbench`)
- **Question:** each analysis answers a question the project chose, with one stage per question. A reader has questions of their own ("what does the Elohistic Psalter say more than the other psalms?", "is נא commoner in speech?") and the method to answer them is the same every time: two sets of words counted against each other, or one count set against shuffles. The workbench puts that method in the viewer, over every word, without a new stage.
- **Sets.** Items separated by `;`, their verses joined: `book:ID` / `book:ID-ID` (canon order), `chapters:BOOK:FROM-TO`, `section:torah|prophets|writings`, `unit:ID` (any unit of the results DB), `collection:ID` (a collection of the notebook, §16.47, read live), `pattern:QUERY` (the verses where a pattern search query matches, §16.46). `only:speech` / `only:narration` is a filter, not a part: of those verses' words, only those inside or outside a BHSA quotation (§16.26). Errors are part of the answer, `{set, code, item, token, pattern}` (`empty`, `too_long`, `bad_item`, `unknown_kind`, `unknown_book`, `bad_range`, `unknown_unit`, `unknown_collection`, `pattern` with the pattern's own problem, `no_words`, `filter_in_check`), worded by the viewer.
- **Contrast.** One feature of the words (lemma, root §16.38, SDBH domain §16.22 where a word over k domains counts 1/k in each, part of speech, stem, verb form, person, BHSA phrase function or clause type) counted in A and in B (empty B: every word not in A; words in both count for A only, and the overlap is reported). Each value with at least `serve.workbench.min_count` words over both is scored by Dunning's G² over the 2 × 2 table (`stats.g2_table`), p from χ²(1), Benjamini–Hochberg q over every value scored; its side is where its rate is higher. Rates are per 1,000 words that have the feature, so a stem or a form is compared among verbs. The strongest `rows` of each side come with example verses of that side and a link to pattern search for the value.
- **Check.** A pattern's matches (counted at their first word) per word in the set's verses, against the set's verses drawn again at random `draws` times among the verses of the same book, the same chapter or the whole Bible (`within`), so a set is compared with its own surroundings rather than with the canon. Groups wholly inside the set never move and are reported (`fixed_verses`); a set that shares no group with other verses is `testable: false`. Both one-sided p (+1 corrected) are returned, denser and sparser. `only:` filters are refused, since the check counts whole verses.
- **Engine.** The word table of pattern search, plus each word's domains (`DomainTable`, read once) and each verse's chapter. A contrast takes under 0.1 s and a check 0.02–0.3 s once the tables are loaded; answers are kept in `serve.workbench.cache` unless a set names a collection. Settings in `serve.workbench` (rows, examples, draws 999, seed).
- **Check against a known answer** (2026-10-23): Psalms 42–83 against the rest of Psalms by lemma, 740 verses (5,879 words) against 1,787 (13,710); 671 lemmas scored, 7 commoner in A and 20 in B at q ≤ 0.05. The first of A is אלהים (245 against 117, G² 222, q 1e-47) and the first of B יהוה (40 against 647, G² 260): the Elohistic Psalter, found by a contrast that was not told about it. Asaph (אסף, 12 against 0) follows. `[lemma=430]` checked on Psalms 42–83 is 41.7 per 1,000 words against 18.5 for as many Psalms verses drawn at random (p 0.001); within chapters it is untestable (each chapter lies wholly inside the set).
- **Viewer:** Patterns → Workbench (`/workbench`), two tabs. Contrast: set A and set B built item by item (book, chapters, section, notebook collection, pattern, speech or narration) or edited as text, the feature, both sides as tables (the value linked to its lemma, root or domain page and to pattern search, counts and rates, ratio, G², q, example verses), CSV. Check: the query, the set, where to draw, the verdict in words, the rate around the set and the counts by book. Sets and queries live in the URL.
- **Limits:** a tool, not a finding: values and patterns picked after looking are not corrected for the looking (the page says so), and a large G² on a few words is a lead. Sets are whole verses (and speech or narration within them), not clauses or phrases.

### 16.65 The names of God across the canon (`bsim divine`, `analysis/divine.py`, `/divine`)
- **Question:** which name of God a passage uses has been a clue to its history since Astruc; the Psalter's second and third books (Psalms 42–83) prefer אלהים so strongly that scholarship calls them the Elohistic Psalter, and where a psalm stands twice (14 / 53, 40:14–18 / 70) the copy inside them reads אלהים for the other's יהוה. Are these found from the counts alone, and where else does the mix change?
- **Counts.** A word counts for a name when one of its OSHB lemma's parts is in `divine.names`: יהוה (3068, and 3069 where it is pointed as אלהים), אלהים (430; God and gods alike), אדני (136, not אדון of a man), אל, אלוה, שדי, יה, עליון (5945b, not the adjective) and צבאות as a title (6635b). Per chapter and book; a chapter's *mix* is יהוה's share of יהוה and אלהים (`divine.measure`). Every name occurrence is stored with its word, so the reader and the dossier can show it.
- **Splits.** A book with at least `min_names` (20) of the two names and `min_chapters` (9) chapters is cut in three runs of at least `min_segment` (3) chapters where the binomial log-likelihood of three rates gains most over one rate (every pair of cuts scored at once from cumulative sums); the gain against the best cut of its chapters shuffled `shuffles` (999) times gives p, BH q over the books split.
- **Checks fixed in advance:** (1) the Elohistic Psalter: Psalms' best cut puts its middle part within `tolerance` (2) psalms of 42 and 83 at both ends, and beats its shuffles at `alpha`. (2) The doubled psalms (`doubled`: 53 / 14, 70:2–6 / 40:14–18, 57:8–12 / 108:2–6, 60:7–14 / 108:7–14): the words aligned by `bsim diffs`' Needleman–Wunsch, every aligned place with a name on either side classed by direction; the copy inside 42–83 has אלהים for the other's יהוה more often than the reverse (one-sided sign test at `alpha`).
- **Results** (2026-10-23, 11 s): יהוה 6,827, אלהים 2,599, אדני 445, אל 245, צבאות 287, אלוה 59, יה 50, שדי 48, עליון 30; 84 chapters with neither. (1) **Holds:** Psalms falls into 1–41, 42–84 and 85–150 with יהוה at 85 %, 17 % and 85 % of the two, p 0.001 (the floor of 999 shuffles), q 0.026: the Elohistic Psalter found to within one psalm without being told where it is. (2) **Not borne out:** 6 places of אלהים for יהוה against 1 of the reverse (Ps 70:6 יהוה for 40:18 אלהי), p 0.0625; 10 places keep the name, 2 trade אדני. The direction is the expected one, but 7 informative places are too few for the test. Of 26 books split only Psalms and Isaiah pass q ≤ 0.05; Isaiah's middle part is 35–37, the Hezekiah narrative (57 % against 91 % and 80 %). Leads at p < 0.05 that BH does not keep: Leviticus 18–21, Exodus 1–3 (19 %: אלהים until the name is revealed in 3:14), Numbers 22–25 (Balaam), Jeremiah 42–44, Zechariah 6–8, Amos 4–6, Job 13–38 (the dialogue against the frame).
- **Viewer:** Patterns → Names of God (`/divine`): the two checks with their verdicts; the canon as a strip, every chapter a square coloured on a diverging scale (יהוה blue, אלהים orange, a neutral middle; the pair checked for colour blindness in both schemes), a bar where a book's cut passes q, a square opening the chapter in the reader; the books as a table (counts, share, cut, the three shares, p, q) with each book's chapters; the doubled psalms name by name. A reader layer draws a line above each name (blue, orange or muted), a dossier entry counts the names of a unit with יהוה's share, and two findings.
- **Limits:** lemmas decide, so אלהים meaning gods counts, and a name written without its usual lemma (a pronoun, an epithet such as קדוש ישראל) does not; chapters are the unit of a split, so a change inside a chapter (Genesis 2:4) is seen only as a mixed chapter; three parts is a fixed choice, not a selected number of segments.

### 16.66 Naming etiologies (`bsim etiologies`, `analysis/etiologies.py`, `/etiologies`)
- **Question:** when the text gives a name it often says why, in words that sound like the name: ותקרא שמו ראובן כי ראה יהוה בעניי (Gen 29:32), ויקרא את שמו נח לאמר זה ינחמנו (5:29). Does a name echo the words of its own verse more than the words given for other names, and are the etiologies everyone recognises found that way?
- **Namings.** A form of קרא (`call`; not with לא up to `negation_before` words before it), then within `trigger_within` words שם with a 2nd / 3rd person suffix or in construct (שמו, שם המקום, שם בנו; no preposition: קרא בשם יהוה is another formula), למקום, or a pronoun object (לו, אתה) directly before the name; the name is the first proper noun within `name_within` words (a multi-word name, OSHB's `+` lemma, whole); a name of God (`skip_names`) is no naming. A place's naming when מקום, עיר or באר stands between the verb and the name.
- **Explanations.** The verse's other content words (not the verb, שם and the name; no conjunction, preposition, particle, pronoun or proper noun), each without its prefixes.
- **Echo.** The most of a name word's consonants (finals folded, at least `min_shared`) found in order in one explanation word, as a share of that name word; the best over the name's words and the explanation's words (ties shown by the word closest to the name). Gen 29:32 ראובן: 3 of 5 in יאהבני, 2 in ראה.
- **Claim fixed in advance:** the mean echo of the names with their own explanations exceeds the mean with the explanations shuffled among the namings (`shuffles` 9,999), at `alpha`. **Known answers fixed in advance:** the 16 etiologies of `known` (Jacob's eleven sons of Gen 29–30, שת, נח, משה, באר שבע, בית אל of 35:7) are all found with their names, and at least `known_share` (75 %) of them echo: their own explanation beats all but `known_rank` (10 %) of the others for that name.
- **Each naming.** Its rank among the 112 explanations says how it stands among them; its own p is its echo against `verse_draws` random verses without a naming, BH q over the namings. The first run had only the rank, which cannot pass BH over 112 (floor 1/112), then 999 draws (floor 0.001; the best q 0.056); `verse_draws` 9,999 was set after seeing that, as a resolution and not a threshold: the claim and the known answers were not touched.
- **Results** (2026-10-23, 27 s): 112 namings in 688 verses with קרא, 27 of places; 82 have an echo. (1) **Holds:** a name's own verse echoes 51 % of its consonants on average, a shuffled one 25 %, p 0.0001 (the floor of 9,999). (2) **Not borne out:** all 16 found, but 11 echo in the top 10 % against the 12 asked for; ראובן (0.13), שמעון (0.12), לוי (0.36), שת (0.18) and בית אל (0.27) are echoed, but short names of common letters are echoed by chance too, so they do not stand out. Beyond chance one by one (q ≤ 0.05): נפתלי / נפתולי … נפתלתי (Gen 30:8), יעקב / ויעקבני (27:36), זבלון / יזבלני (30:20), אלון בכות / אלון (35:8), פרץ / פרצת (38:29) and פרץ עזא (1 Chr 13:11), עשק / התעשקו (Gen 26:20), תבערה / בערה (Num 11:3).
- **Viewer:** Patterns → Naming etiologies (`/etiologies`): the two checks with their verdicts; every naming with its verse, the name and its echo marked, the share of its letters, the name's typical echo elsewhere, p and q; all, the ones beyond chance or the ones named in advance, in canon order or strongest first. Two findings.
- **Limits:** the echo is consonantal and ordered, so a play on sound alone (ש / ס, one guttural for another) or on meaning (Isaac's laughter, told in Gen 21:6, not 21:3) is missed; the explanation is the naming's own verse, so one told in the verse before (בית אל of 28:19) counts only when it is there; the formula misses namings without קרא (Gen 17:5 והיה שמך אברהם) and takes one negated by אל (Ruth 1:20 אל תקראנה לי נעמי) as a naming.

### 16.67 Resumptive repetition (`bsim brackets`, `analysis/brackets.py`, `/brackets`)
- **Question:** an editor who sets a block into a story often returns by repeating the clause that stood before it (Wiederaufnahme, Kuhl 1952): Joseph's sale to Potiphar is told at Gen 37:36 and again at 39:1, with Judah and Tamar between. Is a clause said again a few verses later a sign that the verses between interrupt the text around them?
- **Repetitions.** Every pair of verses of one book `min_gap` (3) to `max_gap` (40) apart is a candidate when they share at least `min_tokens` tokens by lemma or by form (a band of shifted sparse rows: 259,501 pairs); the two content streams are aligned with `bsim phrases`' Smith–Waterman (a match scores idf × formula weight, its mismatch and gap), a token matching by its lemma or by its form without prefixes, so a Hebrew clause told again in Aramaic (Ezra 4:5 / 4:24: מלכות דריוש מלך פרס) still aligns; a repetition has `min_tokens` (3) tokens and `min_score` (14). With `mutual`, a verse and its repeat are each other's best partner; the `window` must fit inside the book.
- **Bracket score.** The `window` (4) verses before the first verse and after the repeat, the two frame verses left out (their shared clause would make any frame cohere), against the block between: cos(before, after) − cos(block, before + after), on words (idf-weighted lemma counts, randomly projected to `lex_dims` 512 so that window sums are prefix differences) and on meaning (the final semantic system's verse embeddings), averaged. High when the two sides belong together and the block does not.
- **Claim fixed in advance:** the mean bracket score of the repetitions exceeds that of random brackets of the same span in the same book (`draws` 999 sets), at `alpha`. **Known answers fixed in advance:** Gen 37:36 / 39:1, Exod 6:10–12 / 6:29–30 (ערל שפתים around the genealogy of Moses and Aaron) and Ezra 4:5 / 4:24 (around the Aramaic letters) are found and each beats its own random brackets at `alpha`. Each repetition's p against its draws, BH q.
- **Results** (2026-10-23, 210 s): 5,280 repetitions, 2,105 after the mutual best and the windows. (1) **Holds:** a mean bracket score of −0.156 against −0.190 for random brackets, p 0.001 (the floor of 999). (2) **Not borne out:** Exod 6:12 / 6:30 is found and beyond chance (0.09, p 0.003); Gen 37:36 / 39:1 is found but not (−0.21, p 0.19); Ezra 4:5 / 4:24 aligns (22.4) but is lost to the mutual rule, since 4:24's best partner is 5:16 (the work on the house of God, a repetition too). One by one, 205 repetitions beat their random brackets at p ≤ 0.05 where 105 are expected; none passes BH over 2,105, the floor of 999 draws being too coarse. The strongest: Ezekiel 48's tribal portions (a list), 1 Sam 28:1 / 29:1 (the Philistines' muster around the medium of Endor, a resumption the commentaries also name), Num 4:6 / 4:14, Josh 22:16 / 22:29, Lev 23:10 / 23:22, Exod 6:12 / 6:30, 2 Kgs 13:12 / 14:15 (Jehoash's closing formula told twice), Isa 2:11 / 2:17 (a refrain).
- **Viewer:** Patterns → Resumptive repetition (`/brackets`): the two checks and the known brackets; the repetitions a page at a time, each with its two verses and the clause marked, the verses between, the bracket score with its parts, p and q, a link to read the block and to compare the two; all, p ≤ 0.05 or named in advance, in canon order or the strongest first, a book at a time. Two findings.
- **Limits:** a repetition is a whole-verse alignment, so a clause repeated inside one long verse or across a verse division counts only if the rest lets it through; refrains, lists and laws repeat for their own reasons and are among the strongest; the bracket score measures cohesion, which a speech set into a narrative also lowers; the mutual rule loses a frame whose repeat is repeated again nearby (Ezra 4:24 / 5:16).

### 16.68 Retold speech (`bsim retold`, `analysis/retold.py`, `/retold`)
- **Question:** a retelling shows its teller (Sternberg 1985, the structure of repetition): the woman tells the serpent God's command and adds ולא תגעו בו (Gen 3:3); Abraham's servant tells Laban his master's charge and leaves part of it out (Gen 24:37–41). Does speech told again by another speaker change more than speech the narrator tells again as done (command and execution, §16.7)?
- **Tellers.** Every word belongs to the narrator or to the speaker of the narrated quotation whose extent holds it (`bsim dialogue`); a quotation inside a speech belongs to the one who quotes it. A run of one teller in one book is cut into equal pieces of at most `chunk_tokens` (60) content tokens.
- **Retellings.** For each piece, windows of earlier verses of its book within `max_back` (400) verses, as long as the piece's span + `margin` (2) verses either side, are scored by the idf the piece shares with them (lemmas in more than `seed_df` 2 % of verses left out); the `seeds` (3) strongest windows of at least `seed_min` (12) are searched with `bsim phrases`' Smith–Waterman (a token matching by lemma or form), never in the piece's own run. The best alignment with `min_tokens` (5) and `min_score` (18) is a retelling; its part of the piece is masked and a second looked for (`per_piece`).
- **Changes.** The two spans, first to last aligned word, are diffed word by word with `bsim diffs`' Needleman–Wunsch: the change rate is the share of positions substituted, omitted, added or moved; a change of spelling or form keeps the word (a command's ועשית is the execution's ויעש).
- **Groups:** `retold` (a speech told again by another known speaker), `repeated` (by the same speaker), `carried` (by the narrator as done), `told` (narration told in a speech), `narrated` (narration told again), `unclear` (a speaker not known).
- **Claim fixed in advance:** `retold` changes more than `carried`, the labels shuffled within quartiles of the aligned length (`shuffles` 9,999). **Known answers fixed in advance:** Gen 2:16–17 / 3:2–3, Gen 24:2–8 / 24:37–41 and Gen 41:1–7 / 41:17–24 are found.
- **Two runs.** The first scored single verses as seeds and kept every piece away from all narration (the narrator's runs shared one teller id, against the rule "not the piece's own run"): 853 retellings, `retold` 29.8 % against `carried` 29.7 % (p 0.39), 1 of 3 known found (Gen 41). It showed the seeding's flaw: a retelling in common words shares little with any one verse of its source, so neither Gen 2:16–17 (עץ and גן alone) nor Gen 24:2–8 was ever searched. Windows (and the narrator's runs told apart) were adopted then; the claim and the known answers were not touched. The result below is the second run.
- **Results** (2026-10-23, 68 s): 12,339 pieces, 1,330 retellings: `retold` 109 (29.0 % of words changed), `repeated` 197 (24.9 %), `carried` 232 (30.0 %), `told` 126 (31.1 %), `narrated` 420 (29.3 %), `unclear` 246 (26.7 %). (1) **Not borne out:** speech retold by another speaker changes no more than speech carried out (difference −1.0 points, p 0.66); the one clear contrast is that a speaker repeating their own words changes least. (2) **Not borne out:** 2 of 3 found. Gen 3:2–3 against 2:16–17 (`retold`, 6 of 13 words changed: 4 substituted, 2 added) and Gen 41:17–24 against 41:1–7 (`told`, 45 % and 28 %); Gen 24:37–41 is missed, because its piece also holds the servant's account of his master's wealth and of Isaac's birth, whose windows (12:16, 18:11, 21:5) outrank the charge's. The largest retellings by another speaker: Balak's message told by Balaam (Num 22:4 / 22:10), the tabernacle's furnishings in God's command and in Moses' (Exod 25:2 / 35:5) and back (35:12 / 40:3), Rehum's letter and Artaxerxes' reply (Ezra 4:9 / 4:17), Nebuchadnezzar's fall told again by Daniel to Belshazzar (Dan 4:28 / 5:20).
- **Viewer:** Patterns → Retold speech (`/retold`): the two checks with the known retellings, each group's count and change rate, and the passages a page at a time: both sides with their tellers and every change marked as in Compare (a legend), filtered by group or to those named in advance, in canon order or the most or least changed first. Two findings.
- **Limits:** a teller is whoever `bsim syntax` gives the quotation, and 246 retellings have a speaker not known on one side; a piece of 60 tokens can hold several retellings, and three seeds per piece may miss one (Gen 24:37–41); a retelling that paraphrases with other words is not found at all, and the change rate counts only what lies between the first and last aligned word.

### 16.69 Haftarot (`bsim haftarot`, `analysis/haftarot.py`, `/haftarot`)
- **Question:** each Sabbath's Torah reading is followed by a reading from the Prophets chosen to match it (Noach with Isaiah 54, כי מי נח זאת לי; Vayera with II Kings 4). The sages' pairing is an independent judgement of which passages belong together, and the parasha level's first gold (§16.33 has none: the Torah is all train). Do the site's systems find it?
- **Data** (`bsim download --only leyning`). Sefaria's export has no haftarah list; Hebcal's leyning (`sources.hebcal_leyning`, `src/aliyot.json` at v10.1.0, BSD 2-Clause, "Copyright (c) 2020, hebcal"; download group `leyning`, chosen by the user on 2026-10-23) gives each parasha its Ashkenazi haftarah (`haft`, the gold), the Sephardi where it differs (`seph`, shown) and `haftTheme` on the ten the calendar fixes (three of rebuke before the Ninth of Av, seven of consolation after). Aligned with the 54 parasha units by order.
- **Ranks.** Each parasha against the 380 chapters of the Prophets (Joshua..Malachi): `lexical` (TF-IDF of the lemma streams as `bsim lexical`, idf over the parashot and the chapters), `semantic` (the final semantic system's best-match average of verse scores, as `bsim units`), `fused` (their RRF with `fusion.rrf_k`, `w_lex`, `w_sem`, over all 380). A chapter is the haftarah's when it holds `min_verses` (3) of its verses; the haftarah's rank is its best chapter's.
- **Claims fixed in advance** (`claim_mode` fused): (1) the 44 haftarot chosen for their parasha rank higher (mean rank percentile) than the same haftarot shuffled among those parashot (`shuffles` 9,999; the shuffle keeps how much each chapter tends to rank high); (2) they rank higher than the ten the calendar fixes, labels shuffled. **Known answers:** Noach and Vayera have their haftarah within the first `known_rank` (10) chapters.
- **Results** (2026-10-23, 6 s): (1) **Holds:** percentile 12.4 % on average against 32.4 % shuffled, p 0.0001. (2) **Holds:** the calendar's ten at 32.5 % (p 0.004): no better than chance, as their choice predicts. (3) **Not borne out:** Vayera 3rd, Noach 17th (6th by meaning, 98th by words: Isaiah 54 shares the flood's oath, not its words). First among all 380: Tetzaveh (Ezekiel 43, the altar), Vayakhel (I Kings 7, the temple's vessels), Kedoshim (Ezekiel 22), Emor (Ezekiel 44, the priests), Chukat (Judges 11, Jephthah recounting Sihon); last: Achrei Mot (Amos 9, 334th), Shemot (Isaiah 27–28, 199th), Vayeshev (Amos 2, 189th). As retrieval of the 44 (the parasha's first gold): words MRR 0.236 (16 in the first 10, median rank 27), meaning 0.204 (15, median 23, the best mean percentile 11.5 %), both 0.199 (16, median 18.5). Words put the most haftarot first, meaning ranks them highest on the whole.
- **Viewer:** Patterns → Haftarot (`/haftarot`): the three checks, the systems as retrieval of the haftarah, every parasha with its haftarah (the calendar's tagged, the Sephardi where it differs) and its rank by both and by each, and a parasha's first ten chapters with the haftarah's marked and a link to compare. Three findings. The credit line names Hebcal.
- **Limits:** chapters, not the haftarah's own verses, are ranked (a haftarah of 11 verses in a chapter of 50 is found by the chapter); one tradition is the gold; the parashot that are sometimes read together, and the special Sabbaths' haftarot, are left out.

### 16.70 The text's own chronology (`bsim chronology`, `analysis/chronology.py`, `/chronology`)
- **Question:** the text dates itself: the fathers' ages at their sons' births run from Adam to Abram (Gen 5, 11), stated spans carry on (Gen 15:13, Exod 12:40, 1 Kgs 6:1), and Kings gives every reign its length and dates each accession by a year of the king of the other kingdom. Read from the words, do the numbers add up to the tradition's dates, and do the two kingdoms' counts agree?
- **Numbers.** A *number phrase* is a run of number words (`chronology.numbers`: the cardinals with עשר of the teens as ten, the duals מאתים / אלפים doubled by the morphology, the ordinals second..tenth), side by side or joined by ו, or carried on by ו after שנה (the Genesis style: תשע מאות שנה ושלשים שנה, 930). Side by side they add (שלש עשרה); a hundred multiplies the word before it (שלש מאות); a thousand or more multiplies everything before it when that is smaller (ששה וארבעים אלף). The dual absolute שנתים is a phrase of two years (the construct שני חיי is not). A phrase after בן / בת is an *age*, after construct שנת a *date*, else a span; its unit is the word after it, or an absolute שנה before it.
- **Dates.** `chronology.events`: each event is the year of another plus (or minus) the n-th number phrase of a verse, from Adam as year 0; an event given twice is computed the first way and checked the second. Names of people are Hebrew (`names`).
- **Kings.** `chronology.kings` (each king's verses, in order within the kingdom) and `syncs` (each "in year N of X, Y became king", taken in the text's order: 31, three of them second statements, 2 Kgs 1:17, 9:29, 15:30). A reign's length is the first span with a unit in its verses (days or months: under a year). Each kingdom counts from the division (its first accession is year 0) under one of two rules: *accession* (a king's year 1 is the year after he comes to the throne) or *non-accession* (that year is his year 1 and his predecessor's last). A synchronism ties a year of Judah's count to a year of Israel's.
- **Claims fixed in advance:** (1) `known`: the Flood, Abram's birth and the Exodus at Seder Olam's years (1656, 1948, 2448), a check of the reading of the numbers (the Exodus by the tradition's reading of Gen 15:13's 400 years from Isaac's birth, Seder Olam 3; the plain reading of Exod 12:40 is the check). (2) Between two synchronisms in a row, the years counted by Judah's reigns equal those counted by Israel's within `tolerance` (1: the kingdoms began their years in different months) more often than with the reign lengths shuffled within each kingdom (`shuffles` 9,999); the best of the four pairs of rules is taken, and so for each shuffle. Intervals, not each synchronism against the division, so that one bad number breaks one interval and not everything after it. (3) Gen 5's year numbers (at least 10; dates left out) end in 0, 2, 5 or 7 (Cassuto's multiples of five, or of five plus seven) more often than the year numbers elsewhere in the Bible, labels permuted.
- **Before the first run** the reading of every configured verse was printed and checked by eye: two bugs were fixed then (the homonym letter on בן, `1121 a`, hid the ages; the construct שני חיי was read as שנתים). No claim value was seen before the fix.
- **Results** (2026-10-24, 4 s): (1) **Holds:** the Flood 1656, Abram 1948, the Exodus 2448. Two conflicts, both known: Arpachshad is born 1658 "two years after the Flood" (Gen 11:10) but 1656 by Shem's 100 years from Noah's 500 (Gen 5:32); Exod 12:40's 430 years in Egypt put the Exodus in 2668. Methuselah dies in 1656, the year of the Flood; Peleg dies in 1996; Shem outlives Abraham (2158 against 2123). The Temple is founded in 2928 and the kingdom divides in 2964, as in Seder Olam. (2) **Holds:** 19 of 30 intervals agree, both kingdoms counting by the accession rule, against 4.7 with the reigns shuffled (p 0.0001); Judah non-accession with Israel accession also gives 19, the two pairs with Israel non-accession 16. Only 5 of 31 synchronisms agree outright with the division as a common start: the counts drift (Omri's twelve years from Asa's 31st overlap Tibni; Azariah's 52 years and Jotham's 16 overlap, as the coregencies scholarship posits). (3) **Holds:** 27 of 28 (96 %) end in 0, 2, 5 or 7, against 72 % of the 307 year numbers elsewhere (p 0.002; against a uniform last digit p 3·10⁻¹⁰). The exception is Methuselah's 969. Shem to Terah (Gen 11:10–26): 65 % of 17. Laid end to end, Judah's reigns run to 3357 and Israel's to 3205; Seder Olam has the Temple destroyed in 3338 by overlapping reigns.
- **Viewer:** Patterns → Chronology (`/chronology`): the three checks; the lifespans from Adam to Moses on one axis (the Flood and the Exodus marked); every date with how it is counted, the Hebrew words its number was read from and its verse, the conflicts listed; the two kingdoms' reigns end to end with a line for each synchronism (upright when the counts agree, slanting by their drift; dashed where the interval disagrees), the synchronisms and the four pairs of rules as tables, every reign; Genesis 5's last digits against the rest. Three findings.
- **Limits:** the chain of events and the kings' verses are named in config (the numbers are read, the structure is not found); one rule per kingdom for the whole period (Thiele's switches and coregencies are not modelled, and show as the disagreeing intervals); Chronicles' parallel reigns and the post-exilic dates are left out.
